# Majmu' al-Fatawa - Tome 19

## Page 9522

VOLUME NINETEEN
THE BOOK OF THE PRINCIPLES OF ISLAMIC JURISPRUDENCE
PART ONE:
FOLLOWING

In the name of Allah, the Most Merciful, the Bestower of Mercy.

Praise be to Allah alone, and may blessings and peace be upon him after whom there is no prophet.

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

CHAPTER

The Quran, the Sunnah, and consensus—and, set against these by others, dreams, Israelite traditions, and anecdotes.

The truth containing no falsehood is what the messengers brought from Allah. For us, this is known through the Quran, the Sunnah, and consensus. As for what the messengers did not bring from Allah, or what they brought but which we have no means of knowing, it contains both truth and falsehood.

Thus, the binding authority that must be followed belongs to the Quran, the Sunnah, and consensus. These constitute truth containing no falsehood, which must be followed and may under no circumstances be abandoned. This obligation is comprehensive: nothing established by these foundations may be abandoned, and no one may depart from anything they establish. They rest upon two fundamental principles:

---

## Page 9523

First: that this is what the Messenger brought.

Second: that whatever the Messenger brought must be followed. This second principle is a matter of faith; its opposite is disbelief or hypocrisy. Some groups among the speculative theologians, philosophers, rulers, and Sufis have fallen into aspects of this: by attributing some deficiency to the message, claiming some superiority over it, turning away from it outright, or holding that it is accepted only in matters subject to change, such as subsidiary rulings, but not in rational or political fundamentals—or through other positions that undermine faith in the message.

As for the first principle, it is a factual premise founded upon knowledge of chains of transmission and the texts themselves. This is the province of those versed in the Quran, the Sunnah, and consensus: their wording and meaning, their chains of transmission and their texts. Anything beyond these is either transmitted from the prophets or is not.

The former includes the Israelite traditions found among Muslims and the People of the Book, in which truth has become mixed with falsehood. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“When the People of the Book relate something to you, neither believe nor disbelieve them, for they may tell you something false and you believe it, or tell you something true and you disbelieve it.”

Nevertheless, such accounts may be heard and related when we know that they agree with what we already know, for they offer reassurance and are corroborated

---

## Page 9524

and are known to be true. As for establishing a ruling solely on their basis, this is impermissible by consensus. The law of those before us is law for us only where it has been established that it was indeed their law—not simply whatever they have related to us. Many devotees, storytellers, some Quran commentators, and some speculative theologians err in this regard.

As for the latter category, it includes reports from the ancient philosophers and their like; what is cast into the hearts of Muslims while awake or asleep; what reasoning indicates concerning foundational or subsidiary matters; and the statements of the leading figures of this faith, both its scholars and its rulers. Such following of others, reasoning, and inspiration contain both truth and falsehood: they are neither to be rejected wholesale nor accepted wholesale.

The weakest of these is what is transmitted through a weak chain from someone whose statements do not constitute authoritative evidence, such as reports from the ancients. This differs from reliably transmitted reports from some members of our own community: their transmission is sound, but the speaker may be mistaken or correct. Following the actions of certain people, as recounted in anecdotes, is also a form of following others.

These things are not to be rejected unconditionally, because they contain elements that accord with the truth; nor are they to be accepted unconditionally, because they contain falsehood. Rather, whatever in them accords with the truth is accepted, and whatever is false is rejected.

Rational arguments concerning foundational matters and legal analogies concerning subsidiary matters fall into this category. Not all rational arguments are sound, nor are they all unsound; rather, they contain both truth and falsehood.

---

## Page 9525

but what is contained in the Book, the Sunnah, and the consensus is true, with no falsehood in it whatsoever. Whatever rational arguments are known to be true are indeed true. Yet many of their proponents treat conjecture as certainty because of specious reasoning and desire. As for them:

“They follow nothing but conjecture and what their souls desire, although guidance has certainly come to them from their Lord.”

This is demonstrated by their frequent disagreements, despite their intelligence, over questions and arguments that one considers unquestionably sound while another considers unquestionably unsound. Indeed, the same person may be certain of their soundness at one time and of their unsoundness at another. Nothing of this kind occurs in what is revealed by Allah. The utmost that may occur is that:

“When he recited, Satan cast something into his recitation. But Allah abolishes what Satan casts, then Allah makes His verses firm. And Allah is All-Knowing, All-Wise.”

At most, this amounts to a slip of the tongue that Allah corrects, so it does not persist. Everything the community has received from the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is truth containing no falsehood; guidance containing no error; light containing no darkness; and healing and salvation. Praise be to Allah, who guided us to this. We would never have been guided had Allah not guided us.

---

## Page 9526

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—also said:

Section

Everyone must know that Allah, Mighty and Majestic, sent Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, to both communities in their entirety: humans and jinn. He made it obligatory upon them to believe in him and in what he brought, to obey him, to regard as lawful whatever Allah and His Messenger declared lawful, and to regard as forbidden whatever Allah and His Messenger forbade; to regard as obligatory whatever Allah and His Messenger made obligatory, to love whatever Allah and His Messenger love, and to hate whatever Allah and His Messenger hate. Everyone must also know that any human or jinn against whom the proof of Muhammad’s messengership has been established, yet who does not believe in him, deserves the punishment of Allah Most High, just as do other disbelievers like him to whom the Messenger was sent.

This is a fundamental principle agreed upon by the Companions, those who followed them in righteousness, the imams of the Muslims, and all Muslim groups—the people of the Sunnah and the united community, and others—may

---

## Page 9527

Allah be pleased with them all. No Muslim group has disputed the existence of the jinn or that Allah sent Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, to them. Most groups of disbelievers also affirm the existence of the jinn. As for the People of the Book—the Jews and Christians—they acknowledge them just as Muslims do, although some among them deny this, just as some Muslims do. Such denial is found among mistaken individuals within Muslim groups and among the Muʿtazilah, even though the majority of that group and its leading scholars affirm it.

This is because reports from the prophets concerning the existence of the jinn have been transmitted so widely and abundantly that their truth is necessarily known. It is likewise necessarily known that the jinn are living, rational beings who act of their own volition and, moreover, are subject to commands and prohibitions. They are not attributes or accidents inhering in human beings or anything else, as some heretics claim.

Since accounts of the jinn have been transmitted from the prophets so widely and manifestly that they are known to ordinary people and scholars alike, no large community of those who believe in the messengers could deny their existence. Likewise, no large community of those who believe in the messengers could deny the angels, bodily resurrection, the worship of Allah alone without any partner, or that Allah sends a human messenger to His creation, and other such matters concerning which reports from the prophets have been transmitted so widely that they are known to ordinary people and scholars alike. Similarly, ordinary people and scholars alike have received widespread reports of Moses coming to Pharaoh, Pharaoh’s drowning, the Messiah coming to the Jews and their hostility towards him, and the emergence of Muhammad

---

## Page 9528

may Allah bless him and grant him peace, in Mecca, his migration to Medina, his bringing the Quran and the well-known religious laws, and the miracles he performed, considered collectively—such as multiplying food and drink and reporting unseen matters of the past and future that no human being could know unless Allah informed him—and other such things.

For this reason, Allah commanded His Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, to ask the People of the Book about matters established among them through widespread transmission, as in His words:

“We sent before you only men to whom We revealed. So ask the people of knowledge if you do not know.”

Some disbelievers denied that Allah could have a human messenger. Allah therefore stated that those He sent before Muhammad were human beings, and commanded those who did not know this to ask the People of the Book about it.

The same applies to asking them about the worship of Allah alone and other teachings brought by the prophets that the disbelievers rejected. Allah Most High says:

“Say, ‘Allah is sufficient as a witness between me and you, as are those who have knowledge of the Scripture.’”

Allah Most High also says:

“If you are in doubt about what We have revealed to you, then ask those who read the Scripture before you.”

And Allah Most High says:

“Say, ‘Have you considered: if it is from Allah and you disbelieve in it, while a witness from the Children of Israel testifies to its like and believes, yet you remain arrogant?’”

The same applies to the testimony of the People of the Book confirming his accounts of unseen matters that could be known only to a prophet or someone informed by a prophet. They knew that Muhammad had not learned

---

## Page 9529

anything from the People of the Book.

This is distinct from the People of the Book bearing witness to him personally on the basis of the description of him they find in their scriptures, as in Allah Most High’s words:

“Is it not a sign to them that the scholars of the Children of Israel know it?”

And His words:

“Those to whom We have given the Scripture know that it has been sent down from your Lord in truth.”

There are other similar passages. This differs from reports established through mass transmission among specialist scholars, such as the hadiths concerning seeing Allah, punishment and questioning in the grave, intercession, the bridge over Hell, and the Cistern. Some ignorant and misguided people who are unfamiliar with these reports may deny them. Thus, some of the Muʿtazilah, such as al-Jubbāʾī, Abū Bakr, and others, denied that a jinn enters the body of a person suffering seizures, but did not deny the existence of the jinn. The former was not as clearly attested in reports transmitted from the Messenger as the latter, although they were mistaken in this denial.

For this reason, al-Ashʿarī mentioned, in his account of the doctrines of the people of the Sunnah and the community, that they hold that a jinn enters the body of a person suffering seizures, as Allah Most High says:

“Those who consume usury cannot stand except as one driven to madness by Satan’s touch.”

ʿAbdullāh ibn Ahmad ibn Hanbal said: “I said to my father, ‘Some people claim that a jinn does not enter a human body.’ He replied, ‘My son, they are lying! He actually speaks through the person’s tongue.’”

This is discussed at length in its proper place.

---

## Page 9530

The point here is that all Muslim groups acknowledge the existence of the jinn, as do most unbelievers: the People of the Book in general, the Arab polytheists in general, and others among the descendants of al-Hudhayl, the Indians, and other descendants of Ham, as well as most Canaanites, Greeks, and other descendants of Japheth.

Thus, the great majority of religious communities acknowledge the existence of the jinn. Indeed, they also acknowledge the incantations and talismanic formulas by which they enlist the jinn’s assistance, whether these are permissible according to the believers or constitute polytheism. Polytheists recite incantations, talismanic formulas, and healing recitations that involve worshipping and venerating the jinn. Most of the incantations, talismanic formulas, and healing recitations in circulation whose meaning cannot be understood in Arabic contain elements of associating the jinn with Allah in worship. Muslim scholars have therefore forbidden healing recitations whose meaning is not understood, because they are liable to contain polytheism, even if the person reciting them does not recognize it as such.

In Sahīh Muslim, Awf ibn Mālik al-Ashja'ī reported:

“We used to perform healing recitations during the pre-Islamic period, so we asked, ‘O Messenger of Allah, what do you think of this?’ He replied, ‘Present your healing recitations to me. There is nothing wrong with healing recitations so long as they contain no polytheism.’”

Also in Sahīh Muslim, Jābir reported:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade healing recitations. The family of Amr ibn Hazm then came to the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, we have a healing recitation that we use for scorpion stings, but you have forbidden healing recitations.’ They presented it to him, and he said, ‘I see nothing wrong with it. Whoever among you can benefit his brother should do so.’”

---

## Page 9531

The Arabs and other nations had many such practices, a full account of which would be lengthy. Reports about the Arabs in this regard are so widely transmitted as to establish certainty among Muslim scholars familiar with their history, and likewise among others. Muslims, however, know more about the Arabs’ pre-Islamic ignorance than about that of other nations. This is because the best generations were Arabs who had themselves witnessed and heard about their former practices during the pre-Islamic period. These practices were among the circumstances that occasioned the revelation of the Quran, so they recorded them in works of Quranic commentary, hadith, biography, military history, and jurisprudence. Accounts of the Arabs’ pre-Islamic days thus became widely and reliably transmitted among Muslims. Otherwise, the other polytheistic nations were of the same kind as the Arab polytheists in this respect: some were more extreme in unbelief and misguidance, and others less so.

The verses Allah revealed to Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, address all creation, both humans and jinn, for his message embraces both. Although some verses were occasioned by circumstances found among the Arabs, Muslims unanimously agree that no verse applies exclusively to the particular circumstance that occasioned its revelation. Their disagreement concerns only whether its application is restricted to the type of circumstance about which the question was asked.

As for restricting it to that particular instance, no Muslim has ever said that the verses concerning divorce, zihār (declaring one’s wife unlawful to oneself like one’s mother), mutual imprecation, the prescribed punishments for theft and for those who wage war, and other such matters apply exclusively to the particular person whose case occasioned the verse’s revelation. This is what some call “refining the basis of the ruling,” namely, when

---

## Page 9532

the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, gave a ruling concerning a particular case, and it is known that the ruling is not exclusive to that case. One therefore seeks to refine the basis of the ruling in order to identify the category of cases to which it applies. For example, when he “ordered the Bedouin who had intercourse with his wife during Ramadan to offer expiation,” it was known that the ruling was not exclusive to him. It was also known that his being a Bedouin or an Arab, or the woman’s being his wife, had no bearing on the ruling. Thus, if a non-Arab Muslim had intercourse with his concubine, the ruling would be the same.

But is the effective cause requiring expiation his having intercourse during Ramadan, or his breaking the fast? The former is the position of al-Shāfiʿī and the position most commonly reported from Ahmad. The latter is the position of Mālik and Abū Hanīfah, and is also explicitly reported from Ahmad concerning cupping; other fast-breaking acts would therefore warrant it all the more. Mālik treats the general category of fast-breaking acts as the effective cause, whereas Abū Hanīfah differentiates according to the kind of fast-breaking act. He therefore does not require expiation for swallowing a pebble or a date stone.

They also disagreed over whether the person must have invalidated a valid fast. Ahmad does not stipulate this; rather, he requires expiation for intercourse during any period in Ramadan when abstention is obligatory, just as all four imams require something comparable in an invalidated state of ihrām (pilgrimage consecration). In his view, an invalidated fast is thus like an invalidated ihrām: both must be continued and completed. Al-Shāfiʿī and others require expiation only when the fast was valid. The disagreement concerns someone who eats and then has intercourse, someone who did not intend to fast and then has intercourse, and someone who has intercourse, offers expiation, and then has intercourse again.

Another example is his saying to someone who had entered ihrām for the lesser pilgrimage wearing a robe and liberally scented with khalūq (a saffron-based perfume): “Take off

---

## Page 9533

your robe and wash off the yellow traces.” Did he order him to wash because a person in ihrām may not retain previously applied perfume, as Mālik maintains? Or was it because he had forbidden men to apply saffron, in which case retaining previously applied perfume is not prohibited, as the other three imams maintain? On the first interpretation, was this hadith abrogated by ʿĀ’ishah’s applying perfume to him during the Farewell Pilgrimage?

Another example is his saying, when asked about a mouse that had fallen into ghee: “Remove it and the ghee surrounding it, and eat your ghee.” Is the decisive factor that the ghee was not altered by the impurity, that it was solid, or that it was specifically a mouse that fell into ghee, so that the ruling does not extend to other liquids? There are many such examples. This kind of reasoning is indispensable in religious law, and many scholars, such as Abū Hanīfah and those who reject analogy, do not call it analogy, since everyone agrees on applying it.

Likewise, they agree on ascertaining whether the basis of a ruling is present in a particular case. This means that the Lawgiver attaches a ruling to a general attribute, and one then examines whether that attribute is present in particular categories or individual cases. Examples include His command to face the Kaaba; His command to call upon two witnesses from among our men whom we find acceptable as witnesses; His prohibition of intoxicating drink and gambling; His ordaining release from an oath through expiation; and His distinction between marital separation in return for compensation and divorce, among other matters.

It thus remains to examine particular categories: do they constitute intoxicating drink, an oath, gambling, marital separation in return for compensation, or divorce? Individual cases must also be examined: does this instance belong to that category? Is this person praying facing the direction of prayer? Is this person upright and acceptable as a witness? And so forth. This kind of independent judgment is agreed upon among Muslims—indeed, among all rational people—in relation to the religious laws they follow, their obedience to those in authority over them, and their interests in this world and the Hereafter.

---

## Page 9534

In essence, this amounts to comparing a thing with its counterpart and subsuming the particular under the universal. The former is called analogical reasoning, and the latter syllogistic reasoning. The two necessarily entail one another: the feature shared by the individual instances in syllogistic reasoning, which logicians call the “middle term,” is the same shared feature in analogical reasoning that scholars of legal theory call the common factor, the basis of the ruling, the effective cause, the indicator, the prompting factor, the motivating factor, the factor calling for the ruling, the factor necessitating it, the shared attribute, and other such expressions.

As for inferring the basis of a ruling, this is analogy proper: a ruling is explicitly laid down concerning certain cases, and one might suppose that it applies exclusively to them. It is then established by inference that other cases are equivalent to them, either because there is no relevant distinction between them, or because they share the attribute to which, as the evidence establishes, the Lawgiver attached the ruling in the original case. This is the analogy accepted by the great majority of scholars and rejected by those who deny analogy.

Errors frequently arise in this because the common factor to which the Lawgiver attached the ruling is not known. This is the issue addressed by what is called the “demand for proof”: the objector requires the proponent of the argument to establish that the attribute shared by the original case and the new case is the effective cause of the ruling, or an indication of that cause. Most errors made by those who reason by analogy arise from supposing something to be the effective cause in the original case when it is not. Hence their frequent denunciations of those who employ unsound analogy.

But if evidence establishes that the distinction is irrelevant, and that there is no difference between the original case and the new case on account of which the Lawgiver would distinguish between the two cases; or if there is

---

## Page 9535

evidence that a particular attribute is the reason why the Lawgiver prescribed this ruling in the original case, and that it is present in another case, then this analogy is disputed only by someone who is unaware of these two premises. A fuller discussion of this belongs elsewhere.

The point here is that the call of Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, encompasses both mankind and jinn, in all their various kinds. It must not be supposed that he singled out the Arabs for any ruling whatsoever. Rather, he attached rulings to such designations as Muslim and disbeliever, believer and hypocrite, righteous and wicked, doer of good and wrongdoer, and other designations mentioned in the Quran and hadith. Neither the Quran nor the hadith singles out the Arabs for any ruling of Islamic law. Some scholars, however, thought this was the case with certain rulings, and the majority disagreed with them. Thus, a group of scholars, including Abū Yūsuf, thought that he had singled out the Arabs as exempt from enslavement. The majority of Muslims, however, hold that they may be enslaved, as established in authentic hadiths: he enslaved Banū al-Mustaliq, among whom was Juwayriyyah bint al-Hārith, then freed and married her and, on her account, freed those of her people who had been enslaved.

In the hadith concerning Hawāzin, he said:

“Choose one of the two: either the captives or the property.”

In the two Sahīh collections, Abū Ayyūb al-Ansārī reports that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said: “Whoever says, ‘There is no god but Allah alone

---

## Page 9536

with no partner; His is the dominion and His is the praise, and He has power over all things,’ ten times is like one who has freed four descendants of Ishmael.”

Also in the two Sahīh collections, Abū Hurayrah reports:

“A female captive from Hawāzin was with Āishah, and he said, ‘Free her, for she is a descendant of Ishmael.’”

Most of the women and children whom the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, enslaved were Arabs. A full account of this would be lengthy. However, when Umar ibn al-Khattāb saw the abundance of non-Arab captives and that people no longer needed to enslave Arabs, he considered that they should free the Arabs. This was a matter of the ruler’s counsel and directives in the public interest, not a ruling of Islamic law binding upon everyone. Some therefore adopted what they understood Umar’s words to mean.

Likewise, some assumed that jizyah (the poll tax) could not be collected from Arab polytheists, although it could be collected from other polytheists. The majority of scholars, however, hold that no distinction is made between Arabs and others. Among them, some permit its collection from every polytheist, while others collect it only from the People of the Book and the Magians. This is because the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did not collect jizyah from Arab polytheists, but did collect it from the Magians and the People of the Book.

Those who say, “It may be collected from any disbeliever,” argue: “When the verse concerning jizyah was revealed

---

## Page 9537

the Arab polytheists had embraced Islam, for it was revealed in the year of Tabūk, when no Arab polytheist remained who was engaged in hostilities. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would not have set out against the Christians in the year of Tabūk with all the Muslims—except those whom Allah had excused—and left the Hijāz with people there who were at war with him. He sent Abū Bakr in the ninth year, and he proclaimed during the pilgrimage season that no polytheist was to perform Hajj after that year and no one was to circumambulate the House naked. He renounced the open-ended treaties but upheld the fixed-term treaties as long as the other parties remained faithful to them, as Allah commanded at the beginning of Sūrat al-Tawbah. He granted those whose treaties he had renounced four months’ respite and ordered that, once those months had elapsed, war be waged against all the polytheists.”

They say: “Thus, all the polytheists embraced Islam, and they were unwilling to pay jizyah. Once the religion of Islam had prevailed, the Arab polytheists had no religious commitment for whose sake they would endure paying jizyah in submission and humiliation. Most Arabs had already embraced Islam, leaving the Arab polytheists with no strength on which to pride themselves. They therefore embraced Islam when Allah made it prevail among the Arabs through proof and clear exposition, the sword and the spear.”

The Prophet’s words, may Allah bless him and grant him peace,

“I have been commanded to fight the people until they testify that there is no god but Allah and that Muhammad is the Messenger of Allah, establish prayer and pay zakah,”

refer to fighting those engaged in hostilities whom Allah had permitted him to fight. He did not mean fighting those bound by treaties whose terms Allah had commanded him to honour. Before “Barā’ah” (Disavowal) was revealed, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would conclude treaties with those disbelievers who entered into treaties with him

---

## Page 9538

without their paying jizyah in submission. But when Allah revealed Barā’ah and commanded him to renounce open-ended treaties, he could no longer conclude treaties with them as he had previously done. Rather, he was required to fight them all, as Allah said:

“Then, when the sacred months have passed, kill the polytheists wherever you find them, capture them, besiege them, and lie in wait for them at every place of ambush. But if they repent, establish prayer and pay zakah, then let them go their way. Indeed, Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.”

The religion of the People of the Book was better than that of the polytheists. Nevertheless, the Muslims were commanded to fight them until they paid jizyah in submission and humiliation. If treaties could no longer be concluded with the People of the Book as they had been before the revelation of Barā’ah, then it was all the more impermissible to conclude treaties with the polytheists without those conditions.

They say: Singling out the People of the Book for mention thus indicated, by an argument from the stronger case, that treaties were no longer to be concluded with the polytheists without humiliation and jizyah, as he had previously concluded treaties with them, such as the truce of al-Hudaybiyah and other agreements.

They say: It is established in the Sahīh, in the hadith of Buraydah, who said: “Whenever the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, appointed a commander over an army or a detachment, he would enjoin him personally to fear Allah and to treat the Muslims under his command well. Then he would say: ‘Go forth in the name of Allah, in the cause of Allah. Fight those who disbelieve in Allah. Go forth, but do not misappropriate the spoils, act treacherously, mutilate, or kill a child. When you meet your enemy from among the polytheists, offer them three alternatives—or choices. Whichever of these they accept, accept it from them

---

## Page 9539

and refrain from attacking them. Invite them to Islam; if they accept, accept it from them and refrain from attacking them. Then invite them to move from their homeland to the land of the Emigrants, and inform them that if they do so, they will have the same rights and obligations as the Emigrants. If they refuse to move, inform them that they will be like the Muslim Bedouins: they will be subject to Allah’s law as it applies to the believers, but they will have no share in the spoils of war or property acquired without fighting unless they fight alongside the Muslims. If they refuse, ask them to pay jizyah. If they agree, accept it from them and refrain from attacking them. If they refuse, seek Allah’s help against them and fight them.

When you besiege the people of a fortress and they ask you to grant them the protection of Allah and His Prophet, do not grant them the protection of Allah or His Prophet. Rather, grant them your own protection and that of your companions, for violating your own pledges of protection and those of your companions is less grave than violating the protection of Allah and His Messenger. When you besiege the people of a fortress and they ask you to let them surrender subject to Allah’s judgment, do not let them surrender subject to Allah’s judgment. Rather, let them surrender subject to your judgment, for you do not know whether your judgment concerning them will accord with Allah’s judgment or not.’”

They say:

In this hadith, he commanded those whom he sent out to invite the disbelievers to Islam, then to migrate to the towns, and otherwise to pay jizyah. If they did not migrate, they would be like the Muslim Bedouins. The Bedouins had mostly been polytheists, so this indicates that he called upon those whom he besieged, whether polytheists or People of the Book, to pay jizyah. There were many fortresses in Yemen after the verse concerning jizyah was revealed. As for the people of Yemen, there were

---

## Page 9540

polytheists and People of the Book among them. He instructed Mu‘ādh to take from every adult male a dinar or its equivalent in Ma‘āfirī garments, without distinguishing between polytheists and People of the Book. This indicates that the Arab polytheists embraced the faith, as did those of the People of the Book who believed, while those of the People of the Book who did not believe paid jizyah.

The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, took jizyah from the people of Bahrain, who were Magians, while ‘Abd al-Qays and others among the people of Bahrain embraced Islam voluntarily. The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, had not imposed jizyah on any of the Jews in Medina or Khaybar. Rather, he fought them before the verse concerning jizyah was revealed. He allowed the Jews of Khaybar to remain as cultivators without paying jizyah until ‘Umar expelled them. They had a truce with him and cultivated the land, so he allowed them to remain because the Muslims needed them. Then, before his death, he ordered their expulsion and commanded that the Jews and Christians be removed from the Arabian Peninsula.

Some have said that this ruling applies exclusively to the Arabian Peninsula. Others have said that it applies generally to all non-Muslims living under Muslim protection: if the Muslims no longer need them, they are to expel them from Muslim lands. This is the view of Ibn Jarīr and others.

Those who hold that jizyah is not to be taken from a polytheist argue that the verse concerning jizyah was revealed while polytheists were still present, yet he did not take it from them.

The point is that he did not single out the Arabs for a distinct ruling, even if it is said that he singled out the Arabian Peninsula, which surrounds the Sacred Mosque, just as he singled out the Sacred Mosque in His words:

“The polytheists are indeed unclean, so let them not approach the Sacred Mosque after this year of theirs.”

---

## Page 9541

Likewise, some scholars have said that whatever the Arabs find repugnant has been forbidden to all Muslims, and whatever they find palatable has been made lawful for them. Most scholars disagree with this view, including Mālik, Abū Hanīfah, Ahmad, and Ahmad’s early followers. However, al-Khiraqī and a group of them agreed with al-Shāfiʿī on this point. As for Ahmad himself, the great majority of his recorded statements agree with the majority of scholars and the position held by the Companions and Successors: declaring something lawful or forbidden does not depend on whether the Arabs find it palatable or repugnant. Indeed, they found palatable things that Allah prohibited, such as blood, carrion, animals killed by strangling, beating, falling, or goring, those killed by beasts of prey, and those slaughtered in the name of anyone other than Allah.

Conversely, they—even the best among them—disliked things that Allah had not forbidden. The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, even disliked the meat of the spiny-tailed lizard, saying:

“It was not found in the land of my people, so I find it distasteful.”

Yet he also said, “It is not forbidden.” It was eaten at his table while he watched, and he said of it, “I neither eat it nor forbid it.”

The majority of scholars have said that the wholesome things Allah has made lawful are those that benefit the eater in his religion, while the unwholesome are those that harm him in his religion. The foundation of religion is justice, which Allah sent the messengers to establish. Thus, He has prohibited whatever food engenders aggression and injustice in the eater, just as He has prohibited all fanged beasts of prey, because they are aggressive and predatory. There is a resemblance between food and the one nourished by it: when a person’s flesh is formed from these animals, he develops a disposition toward aggression and hostility.

---

## Page 9542

Likewise, blood contains the soul’s powers of desire and anger; consuming it increases one’s desire and anger beyond moderation. For this reason, only blood poured out has been forbidden, unlike small amounts, which cause no harm. Pork gives rise to all manner of vile traits, since the pig, more than any other animal, eats everything and shuns nothing. Allah has not forbidden the community of Muhammad anything wholesome; He imposed such prohibitions only on the People of the Book, as He, Most High, says:

“For wrongdoing on the part of the Jews, We made unlawful to them certain wholesome foods which had been lawful to them.”

And He, Most High, says:

“To the Jews We forbade every animal with an undivided hoof; and of cattle and sheep We forbade them their fat, except what adheres to their backs or entrails, or is mixed with bone. Thus We recompensed them for their transgression, and indeed, We are truthful.”

As for Muslims, He has forbidden them only unwholesome things, such as blood poured out. Blood that has not been poured out, such as that remaining in the veins, He has not forbidden. Indeed, ʿĀ’ishah related that they would put meat in the pot and see traces of blood in it. For this reason, the majority of jurists excused small amounts of blood on the body and clothing, provided it was not blood poured out. If it is excused in food, there is all the more reason to excuse its presence on clothing or in what one carries. Likewise, a dog’s saliva on hunted game is excused according to the majority of scholars, which is

---

## Page 9543

the view of Mālik and Abū Hanīfah, and of Ahmad according to the more evident of the two opinions in his school. It is also one of the two opinions within al-Shāfi‘ī’s school, even though most of these scholars require washing a vessel from which a dog has lapped. This is because, when a dog laps, a substantial amount of saliva spreads through the liquid, and avoiding this presents no difficulty. By contrast, the saliva that reaches hunted game is a small amount absorbed into solid flesh, and avoiding it is difficult.

Likewise, most scholars do not hold that lineage gives someone precedence in leading the prayer, and there is no explicit statement from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, to that effect. Rather, it is authentically reported that he said:

“The people should be led in prayer by the one most proficient in reciting the Book of Allah. If they are equal in recitation, then by the one most knowledgeable about the Sunnah. If they are equal in knowledge of the Sunnah, then by the one who migrated earliest. If they migrated at the same time, then by the oldest.”

Thus, he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, gave precedence first on the basis of excellence in knowledge, then on the basis of excellence in deeds. He gave precedence to the one knowledgeable about the Quran over the one knowledgeable about the Sunnah, then to the one whose precedence in religion came through his own choice, and then to the one whose precedence in religion came through his age. He did not mention lineage.

Ahmad and others adopted this approach, ranking those eligible to lead the prayer in the same order as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, without mentioning lineage. Likewise, most scholars, such as Mālik and Abū Hanīfah, did not give preference on the basis of lineage. Al-Shāfi‘ī, however, did so, as did a group of Ahmad’s followers, including al-Khiraqī, Ibn Hāmid, al-Qādī, and others. They cited the statement of Salmān

---

## Page 9544

al-Fārisī:

“You Arabs have a claim upon us: that we should neither lead you in your prayers nor marry your women.”

Those who hold the first view say that Salmān said this only out of deference to the Arabs over the Persians, just as a man might say to someone of nobler standing, “You have such-and-such a claim upon me.” Salmān’s statement is not a religious ruling that everyone must follow as they must follow the rulings of Allah and His Messenger. Nevertheless, any Persian who follows Salmān’s example has a good model in him, for Salmān was foremost among the Persians in embracing Islam.

Likewise, taking lineage into account regarding the People of the Book is not a position held by any of the Companions, nor is it the position of the majority of scholars, such as Mālik, Abū Hanīfah, Ahmad ibn Hanbal, and his early followers. Some of his followers, however, reported two opinions from him, and some chose to take lineage into account, agreeing with al-Shāfi‘ī. Al-Shāfi‘ī took this view from ‘Atā’. A detailed discussion of this belongs elsewhere.

The point here is that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, based his rulings only on attributes relevant to what Allah loves and what He hates. He commanded what Allah loves and called people to it as far as possible, and he forbade what Allah hates and cut off its causes as far as possible. He did not single out the Arabs for any particular kind of religious ruling, since his call was addressed to all humanity. The Quran, however, was revealed in their language—indeed, in the language of Quraysh—as is reliably reported

---

## Page 9545

from ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb, who said to Ibn Mas‘ūd:

“Teach people to recite in the language of Quraysh, for the Quran was revealed in their language.”

Likewise, ‘Uthmān said to those from Quraysh and the Ansār who were writing out the Quran:

“If you disagree over anything, write it in the language of this tribe of Quraysh, for the Quran was revealed in their language.”

This was for the sake of conveying the message: he first conveyed it to his own people, then through them conveyed it to all other nations. Allah commanded him to convey it first to his own people, then to those nearest to him, proceeding outward, just as He commanded him to wage jihad against those nearest, then those next nearest.

As for the statement made by many scholars that non-Arabs are not suitable matches for Arabs in marriage, this is a matter of scholarly disagreement. Some recognize marital suitability only in religion. Those who also take lineage into account cite ‘Umar’s statement:

“I shall prevent women of noble lineage from marrying anyone other than suitable matches.”

For marriage aims at harmonious companionship, and if a woman is of higher social standing, her attention is diverted from her husband, so that this purpose is not fully achieved. This is the argument of those who regard such suitability as a right of Allah, going so far as to declare a marriage invalid if a woman is married to someone who is not her equal in religion or social standing. Those who regard it as an individual right say:

“This entails an affront to the woman’s guardians and to her, and the decision in this matter rests with them.”

Moreover, these scholars do not restrict marital suitability to lineage. Rather, they say:

“It concerns the qualities by which people differ in standing, such as occupation, wealth, free status, and other such things.”

These are matters of scholarly judgment that must be referred back to Allah and the Messenger. If there comes from Allah and His Messenger

---

## Page 9546

something supporting either of the two opinions, then what comes from Allah is not contradictory. Otherwise, no one’s opinion can serve as an authority against Allah and His Messenger. There is no authentic, explicit text from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, concerning these matters. Rather, he said:

“Allah has removed from you the stain of pre-Islamic ignorance and its boasting of ancestry. People are of two kinds: a pious believer or a wretched evildoer.”

In Sahīh Muslim, it is reported that he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“There are four practices of pre-Islamic ignorance that my community will not abandon: boasting of ancestral standing, disparaging lineages, wailing over the dead, and seeking rain through the stars.”

It is also authentically established that he, may Allah bless him and his family and grant him peace, said:

“Allah chose Kinānah from the descendants of Ishmael, chose Quraysh from Kinānah, chose Banū Hāshim from Quraysh, and chose me from Banū Hāshim. Thus, I am the best of you in person and the best of you in lineage.”

The majority of scholars hold that the Arabs, as a people, are better than others, just as Quraysh, as a people, are better than others, and Banū Hāshim, as a people, are better than others.

It is authentically reported in the Sahīh that he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“People are like mines of gold and silver. The best of them in pre-Islamic times are the best of them in Islam, provided they acquire understanding of the religion.”

However, the superiority of one group over another as a whole does not entail that every individual in the former is better than every individual in the latter, for among non-Arabs there are many people who are better than most Arabs.

---

## Page 9547

and among the Emigrants and the Ansār who are not from Quraysh are those who are better than most of Quraysh. Likewise, among those outside Banū Hāshim, whether from Quraysh or elsewhere, are those who are better than most of Banū Hāshim. As the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The best generation is the generation among whom I was sent, then those who follow them, then those who follow them.”

Among the later generations are people better than many of those in the second and third generations. Nevertheless, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did not single out the second and third generations for any particular religious ruling. Likewise, he did not single out the Arabs for any particular religious ruling. Indeed, he did not even distinguish some of his Companions with a ruling that did not apply to the rest of his community. Rather, he spoke of the Companions’ excellence because of the merit they possessed. Similarly, he did not single out the earliest forerunners for a particular ruling, but spoke of their excellence on account of the deeds that distinguished them. This has no connection with lineage.

The point here is that he was sent to all of humankind and the jinn. He did not single out the Arabs, to the exclusion of other peoples, for particular religious rulings. He did, however, distinguish Quraysh by stipulating that leadership should be vested in them, and Banū Hāshim by prohibiting them from receiving zakat. Since Quraysh were superior as a people, leadership had to be vested in the best group whenever possible. Leadership is not something held by every one of them; rather, only one person holds it.

As for the prohibition on receiving charity, he prohibited it for himself and his household to complete their purification and ward off suspicion concerning himself. Likewise, he left no inheritance, so his heirs receive neither a dirham nor a dinar;

---

## Page 9548

rather, he and those whom he supports receive from Allah’s wealth only what is needed for their maintenance. The remainder of Allah’s wealth is spent on what Allah and His Messenger love. His relatives receive reasonable allocations from the khums (the one-fifth share of war booty). Fayʾ revenues, from which allocations are made for the various public interests of the Muslims, are not restricted to specified categories of recipients as zakat is.

Regarding the allocation made for his relatives, one view is that their entitlement ceased upon his death, as Abū Hanīfa holds. Another is that it belongs to the relatives of whoever assumes authority after him, in accordance with the report narrated from him:

“Whatever provision Allah grants a prophet passes to whoever assumes authority after him.”

This is the view of Abū Thawr and others. It has also been said that this was the basis on which ʿUthmān made allocations to Banū Umayya. Another view is that it belongs permanently to the relatives of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

Among those who hold the latter view, some say that its amount is fixed by the revealed law at one-fifth of the khums. This is the position of al-Shāfiʿī and the well-known position reported from Ahmad.

Another view is that the khums and fayʾ are to be spent in the interests of the Muslims according to the ruler’s judgment, rather than divided into fixed, equal portions. This is the view of Mālik and others. It is also reported from Ahmad that he treated one-fifth of zakat as fayʾ. This view is supported by the Quran, the Sunnah, and the practice of the Rightly Guided Caliphs. A detailed discussion of these matters belongs elsewhere.

The point here is that although some Quranic verses were occasioned by events among the Arabs, their rulings are general, encompassing whatever falls within the wording of those verses

---

## Page 9549

and their meaning, whatever the category involved. Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was sent to humankind and the jinn.

The great majority of nations acknowledge the jinn and have had encounters with them that would take too long to describe. Only a small handful of ignorant philosophizers, physicians, and the like have denied the jinn. As for their leading figures, they are either reported to have acknowledged them, or are not known to have expressed any view on the matter.

Hippocrates is known to have said concerning certain waters:

“They are beneficial for epilepsy. I do not mean the kind treated by temple practitioners, but rather the epilepsy treated by physicians.”

He is also reported to have said:

“Our medicine, compared with that of temple practitioners, is like the medicine of old women compared with ours.”

Those who deny this have no evidence on which to base their denial. All they have is a lack of knowledge, since their discipline contains nothing that would establish it. A physician, for example, examines the body in terms of its health and the illnesses related to its constitution. This does not address what arises from the soul or from the jinn, even if he has learned outside his medical discipline that the soul has a tremendous influence on the body—greater than that of medical causes. The jinn likewise influence the body, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said in an authentic hadith:

“Indeed, Satan courses through the son of Adam as blood does.”

He courses through the blood, which is the vapor that physicians call the vital spirit, emanating from the heart and circulating through the body, by which the body lives, as

---

## Page 9550

this has been explained at length elsewhere.

The point here is that Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was sent to both humankind and the jinn. Allah has stated in the Quran that the jinn listened to the Quran and believed in it, as He, Most High, says:

“And when We directed to you a group of jinn listening to the Quran, when they attended its recitation, they said, ‘Listen attentively.’”

The passage continues up to His words:

“Those are in clear error.”

He then commanded him to inform people of this, saying:

“Say, ‘It has been revealed to me that a group of jinn listened and said, “Indeed, we have heard a wondrous Quran.”’”

And so on. He commanded him to say this so that humans would learn about the circumstances of the jinn and know that he was sent to both humans and jinn. For this provides humans and jinn with guidance concerning their duty to believe in Allah, His messengers and the Last Day, their obligation to obey His messengers, and the prohibition of associating the jinn or anyone else with Allah. As He says in the surah:

“And there were men among humankind who sought refuge in men among the jinn, but they only increased them in transgression.”

A man would stop in a valley—valleys being places where jinn are likely to dwell, since they inhabit valleys more frequently than higher ground—and say, “I seek refuge in the great one of this valley from its foolish ones.” When the jinn saw that humans sought refuge in them, their transgression—and that of others—increased. This is why they respond to someone who adjures them or recites incantations using their names and the names of their kings: he adjures them by the names of those whom they revere

---

## Page 9551

and thereby they gain such authority and prestige over humans that they are moved to grant some of their requests, especially since they know that humans are nobler and greater in standing than themselves. When humans humble themselves before them and seek refuge in them, it is like a person of high standing humbling himself before someone of low standing to have his need fulfilled. Moreover, among the devils are those who choose disbelief, associating partners with Allah, and disobedience to the Lord.

Iblīs and his armies of devils desire evil, delight in it, pursue it, and eagerly seek it because of their wicked nature, even though it brings punishment upon them and those whom they lead astray. As Iblīs said:

“By Your might, I will surely mislead them all, except Your chosen servants among them.”

And He, Most High, says:

“He said, ‘Do You see this one whom You have honoured above me? If You grant me respite until the Day of Resurrection, I will surely bring his descendants under my sway, except a few.’”

And He, Most High, says:

“Indeed, Iblīs proved his assumption about them correct, and they followed him, except a group of believers.”

When a person’s soul becomes corrupt or his bodily constitution disordered, he craves what harms him and takes pleasure in it. Indeed, he becomes so infatuated with it that it ruins his reason, religion, character, body, and wealth. The devil is himself wicked. Thus, when someone who employs incantations, adjurations, books of spirit magic, and the like seeks to draw close to the devils through the disbelief and association of partners with Allah that they love, this serves as bribes and payoffs to them. They then fulfil some of his purposes, just as someone gives another person money

---

## Page 9552

to kill someone he wants killed, help him commit an indecent act, or engage in one with him. For this reason, in many of these practices they write Allah’s words with impure substances—sometimes reversing the letters of the words of Allah, Mighty and Majestic, whether those of al-Fātihah, “Say, He is Allah, One,” or other passages—using blood or something else; or they write them without any impure substance. Alternatively, they write other things that please Satan, or utter them.

When they say or write what pleases the devils, the devils help them achieve some of their aims: making water sink underground, carrying someone through the air to certain places, or bringing him wealth belonging to other people. Thus, the devils steal property belonging to dishonest people, and property over which Allah’s name has not been mentioned, and bring it to him. They may also do other things. For each of these kinds of activity, I know specific incidents and people among my acquaintances to whom they happened. Recounting them would take a long time, for such people are very numerous.

The point is that Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was sent to both humankind and jinn. The jinn listened to his recitation and returned to their people as warners, as Allah, Mighty and Majestic, has related. This is a matter of agreement among Muslims.

Furthermore, most Muslims, including the Companions, the Successors, and others, hold that the jinn subsequently came to him, that he recited the Quran to them, and that they pledged allegiance to him. They asked him for provisions for themselves and their animals, and he said to them: “You shall have every bone

---

## Page 9553

over which Allah’s name has been mentioned, restored to its fullest covering of flesh; and every dropping shall be fodder for your animals.” The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said: “So do not cleanse yourselves with either of them, for they are provisions for your brothers among the jinn.” This is established in Sahīh Muslim and elsewhere through the hadith of Ibn Mas‘ūd.

The Prophet’s prohibition, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, against cleansing oneself with bones and dung is established in several hadiths, including the report of Abū Hurayrah in Sahīh al-Bukhārī and elsewhere. Sahīh Muslim and other collections also relate that Salmān said:

“Someone said to me, ‘Your Prophet has taught you everything—even how to defecate!’ I replied, ‘Indeed! He has forbidden us to face the direction of prayer when defecating or urinating, to cleanse ourselves with the right hand, to cleanse ourselves with fewer than three stones, or to cleanse ourselves with dung or bones.’”

Sahīh Muslim and other collections also relate that Jābir said:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade us to wipe ourselves with bones or droppings.”

This prohibition also appears in the hadith of Khuzaymah ibn Thābit and others.

The reason for this prohibition is explained in the hadith of Ibn Mas‘ūd. Sahīh Muslim and other collections relate from Ibn Mas‘ūd that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“A caller from among the jinn came to me, so I went with him and recited the Quran to them.”

He continued: “He then took us and showed us their traces and the traces of their fires. They asked him for provisions, and he said: ‘You shall have every bone over which Allah’s name has been mentioned that comes

---

## Page 9554

into your hands, clothed in flesh; and every dropping shall be fodder for your animals.’ The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said: ‘So do not cleanse yourselves with either of them, for they are provisions for your brothers.’”

Sahīh al-Bukhārī and other collections relate from Abū Hurayrah:

“I used to accompany the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, carrying a small water vessel for his ablution and personal needs. As I was following him with it, he asked, ‘Who is that?’ I replied, ‘Abū Hurayrah.’ He said, ‘Find me some stones to cleanse myself with, but do not bring me a bone or a dropping.’ I brought him some stones, carrying them in the hem of my garment, and placed them beside him. Then I withdrew. When he had finished, I walked with him and asked, ‘Why not bones and droppings?’ He replied, ‘They are food for the jinn. A delegation of the jinn of Nasībīn—and excellent jinn they were—came to me and asked me for provisions. So I prayed to Allah that they would never pass a bone or a dropping without finding food on it.’”

By forbidding cleansing oneself with anything that spoils the food of the jinn or the fodder of their animals, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, indicated that spoiling human food or the fodder of their animals is forbidden with even greater reason. Yet people’s innate disposition plainly makes them dislike and recoil from the latter, unlike using bones and droppings, since the defilement of the jinn’s food is not apparent to them. Hence the numerous authentic hadiths prohibiting it.

These authentic hadiths establish that he addressed the jinn and they addressed him, that he recited the Quran to them, and that they asked him for provisions.

It is also established in the two Sahīh collections that Ibn ‘Abbās used to say: “The Prophet

---

## Page 9555

may Allah bless him and grant him peace, neither saw the jinn nor addressed them, but was informed that they had heard the Quran.” Ibn ‘Abbās knew what the Quran indicated concerning this, but he did not know what Ibn Mas‘ūd, Abū Hurayrah and others knew about the jinn coming to him and his addressing them. Allah informed him of this in the Quran and commanded him to convey it. This occurred at the outset, when heaven was guarded, the jinn were prevented from obtaining news from heaven, and it was filled with mighty guards. This contained signs of prophethood that offer a lesson for reflection, as has been explained at length elsewhere.

After this, they came to him, and he recited the Quran to them. It is reported that he recited Sūrat al-Rahmān to them, and whenever he said:

“Which, then, of your Lord’s favors will you both deny?”

they replied: “We deny none of Your favors, our Lord; all praise belongs to You.”

Allah’s addresses to both humankind and jinn in the Quran clarify this principle, as in His words, Exalted is He:

“O company of jinn and mankind, did there not come to you messengers from among you, relating My verses to you and warning you of the meeting of this Day of yours? They will say, ‘We testify against ourselves.’”

Allah also tells us that the jinn said:

“Among us are the righteous, and among us are those otherwise; we have followed diverse paths.”

That is, they follow various ways: Muslims and disbelievers, adherents of the Sunnah and adherents of religious innovation. They also said:

“Among us are those who submit to Allah, and among us are the unjust. Whoever submits to Allah—it is they who have sought right guidance.”

“But as for the unjust, they will be firewood for Hell.”

The word qāsit means one who acts unjustly: qasata means to act unjustly, whereas aqsata means to act justly. The scholars unanimously agree that the disbelievers among them will be punished in the Hereafter. As for their believers, the majority of

---

## Page 9556

scholars hold that they will be in Paradise. It has been reported:

“They will be on the outskirts of Paradise, where humans will see them while they cannot see the humans.”

This view is transmitted from Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī, Ahmad, Abū Yūsuf, and Muhammad. Another view holds that their reward is deliverance from the Fire; this is transmitted from Abū Hanīfah. The majority cite as evidence His words:

“Whom neither man nor jinn has touched before them.”

They say: This indicates that sexual intercourse is possible for them, since intercourse with the wide-eyed maidens takes place only in Paradise.

Section

Since the jinn are living, rational beings, subject to commands and prohibitions, liable to reward and punishment, and since the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was sent to them, Muslims must deal with them as they deal with humans: enjoining what is right, forbidding what is wrong, and calling them to Allah as Allah and His Messenger have prescribed, and as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, called them. If they commit aggression, they should be treated as aggressors are treated, and their attacks repelled by the same means used to repel human attacks.

The fits they cause in humans may be motivated by lust, desire, or passionate love, just as occurs between humans. Humans and jinn may intermarry and have offspring together. This is common and well known. Scholars have mentioned and discussed this, and it was regarded with disapproval by most

---

## Page 9557

scholars, who disapproved of marrying jinn.

Possession may also arise—and this is common, or even the most common cause—from hatred and retaliation. For example, a human may harm them, or they may think that humans are deliberately harming them by urinating on some of them, pouring hot water on them, or killing some of them, even though the human is unaware of this. There is ignorance and injustice among the jinn, so they punish him more severely than he deserves. It may also arise from their mischief and wickedness, as with foolish humans.

Whatever falls into the first category is among the acts of sexual immorality that Allah Most High has forbidden, just as He has forbidden them to humans, even when the other party consents. How much worse, then, when it is against the other party’s will, making it both sexual immorality and injustice? The jinn should therefore be addressed about this, so that they recognize it as forbidden sexual immorality, or as both sexual immorality and aggression. In this way, the case against them is established, and they know that judgment is being passed upon them according to the ruling of Allah and His Messenger, whom He sent to both communities: humans and jinn.

As for the second category, if the human was unaware, they should be told that he did not know, and that someone who does not deliberately cause harm does not deserve punishment. If he did this in his own house and on his own property, they should be told: “The house belongs to him, and he is entitled to make any lawful use of it. You have no right to remain on human property without the owners’ permission. Rather, you may inhabit places where humans do not dwell, such as ruins and wilderness.” For this reason, they are frequently found among ruins

---

## Page 9558

and wilderness. They are also found in places of filth, such as bathhouses, latrines, refuse heaps, qamāmīn, and graveyards. The shaykhs whom devils accompany, and whose spiritual states are satanic rather than from the Most Merciful, often frequent these places where devils dwell. Reports have prohibited prayer in them because they are dwellings of devils. Some jurists explain the prohibition by the likelihood of impurities being present.

Others say that it is a devotional injunction whose rationale cannot be understood. The correct view is that bathhouses, camel resting places, and similar locations are prohibited because they are dwellings of devils, whereas prayer in graveyards is prohibited because it is a means leading to associating partners with Allah—although graveyards, too, are dwellings of devils.

The point is that people of misguidance and religious innovation who practise asceticism and worship in ways not prescribed by Islamic law, and who sometimes experience unveilings and exert influence, often frequent the places of devils in which prayer has been prohibited. This is because devils descend upon them there and speak to them about certain matters, just as they speak to soothsayers, and just as they used to enter idols and speak to their worshippers. They help them achieve some of their aims, just as they help sorcerers and worshippers of idols, the sun, the moon, and the heavenly bodies when they worship these things through practices they believe appropriate to them, such as glorifying them, wearing particular clothing, burning incense, and other practices. Devils whom they call the spirits of the heavenly bodies may descend upon them and fulfil some of their needs, whether by killing some of their enemies or

---

## Page 9559

making them ill, bringing them someone they desire, or procuring some money. Yet the harm they suffer as a result is greater than the benefit; indeed, it may outweigh the benefit many times over.

Many of those who employ the jinn through these practices claim that Solomon employed the jinn by the same means. More than one scholar of the early generations mentioned that, when Solomon died, the devils wrote books of sorcery and disbelief, placed them beneath his throne, and said, “Solomon used these to employ the jinn.” A group of the People of the Scripture therefore disparaged Solomon on this account.

Others said, “Had this not been right and permissible, Solomon would not have done it.” Both groups thus went astray: the former by disparaging Solomon, and the latter by practising sorcery. Concerning this, Allah Most High revealed His words:

“And when a messenger from Allah came to them, confirming what was with them, a group of those who had been given the Scripture cast the Book of Allah behind their backs,”

through to His words:

“If only they had believed and feared Allah, a reward from Allah would have been better, if only they knew.”

He, glory be to Him, explained that this neither harms nor benefits, since benefit is that which is wholly or predominantly good, while harm is that which is wholly or predominantly evil; and the evil of this is either unmitigated or predominant.

The point is that when the jinn transgress against human beings, they are informed of the ruling of Allah and His Messenger, the proof is established against them, and they are enjoined to do what is right and forbidden to do what is wrong, just as is done

---

## Page 9560

with human beings; for Allah says:

“And never would We punish until We sent a messenger.”

And He, Most High, says:

“O company of jinn and mankind, did there not come to you messengers from among you, relating to you My verses and warning you of the meeting of this Day of yours?”

For this reason, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade killing snakes found in houses until they had been given three warnings. As reported in Sahīh Muslim and elsewhere, Abū Sa'īd al-Khudrī said that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“In Medina there are some jinn who have embraced Islam. Whoever sees any of these house-dwelling snakes should give it three warnings. If it appears to him after that, he should kill it, for it is a devil.”

It is also reported in Sahīh Muslim that Abū al-Sā'ib, the freedman of Hishām ibn Zuhrah, visited Abū Sa'īd al-Khudrī in his home. He said:

“I found him praying, so I sat down to wait until he finished his prayer. I heard movement among some date-palm stalks in a corner of the house. I turned and saw a snake, so I sprang up to kill it. He gestured to me to sit down, and I sat down. When he finished praying, he pointed to a room in the house and said, ‘Do you see this room?’

“I replied, ‘Yes.’ He said, ‘A young man from among us, newly married, used to live there. We went out with the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, to the Trench. That young man would ask the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, for permission to leave around midday and return to his wife. One day he asked his permission, and the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, told him, “Take your weapons with you, for I fear for your safety because of Qurayzah.” The man took his weapons and returned home, where he found his wife standing in the doorway. Overcome by jealousy, he lunged at her with his spear to stab her. She said:

---

## Page 9561

“Hold back your spear and go inside the house to see what drove me out.” He went inside and found a huge snake coiled on the bed. He thrust his spear at it, impaling it, then went outside and planted the spear in the courtyard. The snake writhed upon it, and no one knew which died first—the snake or the young man.’

He continued, ‘We went to the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, told him what had happened, and said, “Pray to Allah to bring him back to life for us.” He said, “Ask forgiveness for your companion.” Then he said, “In Medina there are some jinn who have embraced Islam. If you see any of them, give it warning for three days. If it appears to you after that, kill it, for it is a devil.”’”

In another wording also reported by Muslim, the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“These houses have jinn dwelling in them. If you see any of them, warn it three times. If it leaves, well and good; otherwise kill it, for it is a disbeliever.”

He also told them:

“Go and bury your companion.”

This is because killing jinn without right is impermissible, just as killing human beings without right is impermissible. Injustice is forbidden in all circumstances: no one may wrong anyone, even a disbeliever. Rather, the Most High says:

“And do not let the hatred of a people prevent you from being just. Be just; that is nearer to righteousness.”

Jinn take the forms of human beings and animals. They take the forms of snakes, scorpions and other creatures; of camels, cattle, sheep, horses, mules and donkeys; of birds; and of human beings. Thus Satan came to Quraysh in the form of Surāqah ibn Mālik ibn Ju‘shum when they intended to set out for Badr. The Most High says:

“And when Satan made appealing to them

---

## Page 9562

their deeds and said, ‘No one among mankind can overcome you today, and I will protect you,’” up to His words:

“And Allah is severe in punishment.”

Likewise, it is reported that he appeared in the form of an elderly man from Najd when they gathered at Dār al-Nadwah to consider whether to kill the Messenger, imprison him, or expel him. As He, blessed and exalted, says:

“And when those who disbelieved plotted against you to imprison you or kill you or expel you. They plot, and Allah plots; and Allah is the best of planners.”

Since snakes found in houses may be jinn, they are to be warned three times. If they leave, well and good; otherwise, they are killed. If it is a snake, it is killed; if it is a jinn, it has persisted in aggression by appearing to human beings in the form of a snake and frightening them. An aggressor is an assailant who may be repelled by whatever will avert his harm, even if that means killing him. Killing them without grounds that permit it, however, is impermissible.

Practitioners of adjurations and oaths adjure some jinn to help them against others. Sometimes they honour the practitioner’s oath, but often they do not. This may be because the jinn in question is highly esteemed among them, while neither the practitioner nor his adjuration commands enough reverence to induce them to provide such help. For the practitioner may be like someone who makes another swear an oath and adjures him by someone he reveres. Circumstances differ in such matters: if someone adjures people to harm a person whom they hold in high esteem, they pay him no heed. That person may also be well protected. Thus their circumstances resemble those of

---

## Page 9563

humans. Yet humans are more rational, truthful, just, and faithful to their covenants, while jinn are more ignorant, deceitful, unjust, and treacherous.

The point is that practitioners of adjurations—quite apart from the fact that their adjurations contain polytheism and disbelief, which may not lawfully be used in adjurations or oaths—are often unable to repel a jinn. The jinn frequently mock them when they ask them to kill or imprison the jinn possessing a human being. They make them imagine that they have killed or imprisoned it, when this is merely illusion and falsehood. This assumes that the person who sees what they conjure up is truthful about what he has seen.

For they generally impart what they wish someone to know through unveilings and direct communication, if he is one of the devotees among the polytheists, the People of the Book, or the Muslim innovators whom the jinn and devils lead astray. As for what they show practitioners of adjurations and oaths, they produce a likeness of what they wish to make known. When the practitioner sees this likeness, he reports it. He may know that it is a likeness, or they may lead him to believe that it is the actual thing depicted.

When they want him to hear the words of someone calling to him from a distant place—for example, someone appealing for help to one of the misguided devotees among the polytheists, the People of the Book, or the ignorant Muslim devotees—one of his admirers may appeal to him, saying, “O my master, so-and-so!” The jinn then addresses him in a voice resembling that human’s voice. When the shaykh replies to him, that human answers in a similar voice. This has happened to a great many people, some of whom I know.

---

## Page 9564

Section

When a deceased person is invoked, called upon, and appealed to for help, the devil often assumes his form. The person may also be alive yet unaware of whoever has called upon him. Instead, the devil assumes his form, and the misguided polytheist appealing to that person for help imagines that the person himself has answered him, when it is only the devil. This happens to unbelievers who appeal for help to those, dead or living, whom they hold in high regard, such as Christians who appeal to George and other saints of theirs. It also happens to those who profess Islam but practise polytheism and follow misguidance, appealing for help to the dead and the absent. The devil appears to them in the form of the person whose help they seek, while that person remains unaware.

I know many people who have experienced this in connection with various individuals. Each of those individuals tells me, “I did not know that this person had appealed to me for help.” Yet the person seeking help had seen a being in that individual’s form and had no doubt that it was the individual himself.

More than one person has told me that they appealed to me for help, each recounting a different incident. I told each of them that I had neither answered any of them nor known of his appeal for help. It was then said:

---

## Page 9565

“This would be an angel.” I replied, “An angel does not come to the aid of a polytheist. It is only a devil seeking to lead him astray.”

Likewise, the devil assumes a person’s form and stands at Arafāt, so that someone who thinks well of that person imagines that he has stood at Arafāt. Many such people have been carried by the devil to Arafāt or elsewhere within the Sacred Precinct. They cross the prescribed boundary without entering the consecrated state or reciting the pilgrimage invocation, and neither circumambulate the House nor walk between al-Safā and al-Marwah. Some do not even pass through Mecca; others stand at Arafāt and return without throwing pebbles at the pillars. These and similar practices are ways in which the devil leads them astray: they do what Islamic law prohibits, whether forbidden or disliked, rather than anything obligatory or recommended. Yet the devil makes this appear to them to be among the miraculous gifts granted to the righteous, when it is actually his deception.

Allah is to be worshipped only through what is obligatory or recommended. Whoever performs an act of worship that is neither obligatory nor recommended, believing it to be obligatory or recommended, has merely had it made appealing to him by the devil. Even supposing that Allah pardons him because of his good intention and earnest striving, this is not something through which Allah honours His God-fearing allies. There is no honour in committing forbidden or disliked acts. Rather, honour lies in Allah protecting a person from them and preventing him from committing them, for such conduct diminishes him rather than elevates him.

Even if he is not punished with torment for it, it will inevitably lower him from his former standing, along with those followers of his who praise this condition and revere the person who experiences it. Praising forbidden and disliked acts, and revering those who practise them, is a departure from the path of Allah. The further a servant goes in

---

## Page 9566

pursuing religious innovations, the further he moves away from Allah, for they lead him away from Allah’s path—the path of those whom Allah has blessed: the prophets, the steadfastly truthful, the martyrs, and the righteous—and onto some part of the path of those who have incurred His wrath and those who have gone astray.

Section

Having established the fundamental principle concerning this matter, we say:

It is permissible—indeed, recommended, and sometimes obligatory—to defend and support someone who has been wronged. Supporting the oppressed is commanded to the extent that one is able. In the two Sahīh collections, al-Barā’ ibn ‘Āzib said:

“The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, commanded us to do seven things and forbade us seven things. He commanded us to visit the sick, follow funeral processions, invoke Allah’s mercy upon someone who sneezes, honour an oath—or help the person who swears it to fulfil it—support the oppressed, accept invitations, and spread greetings of peace.
