This distinction gives rise to the division mentioned earlier: knowledge that affects its object and knowledge that does not, and knowledge that depends on its object and knowledge that does not. This holds even though all knowledge has an effect on the knower himself, and even though

---

## Page 9649

all knowledge follows its object by corresponding and conforming to it, even though some knowledge does not follow its object in the sense of coming after it, being affected by it, depending on it, or being caused by it. This is a comprehensive principle of great benefit in many matters.

Once this is clear concerning knowledge in general, the same becomes evident concerning belief, opinion, conjecture, and the like. These may or may not constitute knowledge; rather, they may be correct or incorrect beliefs, correct or incorrect conjectures, or other forms of awareness, sensation, and perception. This general category is the foundation of spiritual and bodily movements and acts, whether love, hatred, and the like, or standing, sitting, and the like. All of these follow awareness, depend upon it, and are preceded by it. Knowledge is invariably the foundation of action, although it may itself derive from knowledge of matters other than action, as explained above.

Belief, then, sometimes derives from its object and follows it, as with beliefs concerning matters beyond a person’s agency—for example, the beliefs of believers and disbelievers concerning Allah Most High and the Last Day. At other times, belief is the basis for its object, which follows it. Such is a person’s belief or conjecture that a particular action will bring him some benefit or ward off some harm, either in this world or in the Hereafter. Examples include his belief that eating this food will satisfy his hunger, that taking this poison will kill him, that this shot will hit this target, that this blow will sever this neck, that this sale and trade will bring him profit or loss, and that

---

## Page 9650

his prayer, zakat, pilgrimage, righteous conduct, truthfulness, and other such good deeds will bring him happiness in this world and the Hereafter, and that his disbelief, wickedness, and disobedience will bring him misery in this world and the Hereafter. This is a broad field encompassing religious traditions, governance, and all other religious and worldly activities; it includes sound and corrupt forms of religion alike. Yet this practical belief must also concern matters other than action. For his belief that a particular action will benefit or harm him in this world and the Hereafter also concerns objectively established attributes that do not depend on his belief. Likewise, although theoretical belief has something other than action as its object, action follows from it. As explained above, each of the two kinds of belief both follows something else and is itself followed.

Judgments are likewise akin to beliefs, and they too fall into two categories:

Judgments concerning existing realities, which follow the subject being judged—for example, judging what praise and commendation Allah Most High deserves, and what He is exalted above, such as need and having partners.

Practical judgments, which are followed by the action being judged—for example, judging that a particular action is good or bad, sound or corrupt, good or evil, beneficial or harmful, obligatory or forbidden, commanded or prohibited, right guidance or error, justice or injustice.

Likewise, utterances fall into declarative and performative types. Declarative utterances

---

## Page 9651

conform to what they describe and follow it. They correspond to knowledge, belief, and judgment that likewise follow their objects. Performative utterances, such as commands, prohibitions, and permissions, give rise to the matters spoken of—what is commanded, prohibited, or permitted—and are a cause of their existence or nonexistence. They resemble knowledge and belief that are themselves followed by action: this is practical judgment.

Once these two types are understood, some people call knowledge, belief, judgment, and declarative speech that follow their objects “the study of fundamentals,” “the fundamentals of religion,” “speculative theology,” “the greater jurisprudence,” and other similar names, although their intended meanings and terminological conventions differ.

They call the other type “the study of subsidiary matters,” “the subsidiary matters of religion,” “the study of jurisprudence and religious law,” and similar names. This terminology is used by many later scholars of jurisprudence and speculative theology.

Others use “the fundamentals of religion” to designate everything upon which the revealed laws agree and which is neither abrogated nor changed, whether theoretical or practical, and whether belonging to the first category or the second. Thus, they include worshipping Allah alone, loving Him, fearing Him, and similar matters among the fundamentals of religion, while they may classify some matters of belief conveyed through declarative statements among its subsidiary matters. They also use the term “religious law” to encompass both beliefs and actions, and so forth. This terminology predominates among the scholars of hadith and Sufism and is also adopted by the leading jurists and a group of speculative theologians.

---

## Page 9652

Section

With this established, some people have gone to opposite extremes. It is reported that one of the Sophists held that all beliefs are themselves what determine convictions. He did not acknowledge that things possess realities fixed in themselves, with which belief sometimes agrees and sometimes disagrees. Rather, he made the truth concerning anything whatever a person believes it to be, making realities dependent on beliefs.

No person of sound reason holds this view in such unrestricted and universal terms. It is simply of a piece with the report that the Sophists denied realities, acknowledging neither any reality nor any knowledge of reality, and that they had a leader called Sūfistā, as some speculative theologians relate.

Others have maintained that no rational person is known to have expressed this view, nor is any group known by this name. Rather, it is a word Arabized from Greek, meaning “specious wisdom.” By this they mean false discourse that may resemble the truth, such as a person might imagine because of an impairment of his reason or temperament, or because the matter is unclear to him; and they regarded

---

## Page 9653

this as a kind of discourse and opinion that arises in people’s minds, not as a class of human beings. In any case, it is well known that false imaginings frequently occur to human beings—indeed, they are beset by them—and that they deny the truth, either obstinately or mistakenly, in many matters and circumstances. Yet someone who denies one truth may acknowledge another, or acknowledge that same truth at another time. Thus, the ignorance and obstinacy that constitute sophistry are particular and limited among them, not universal and unrestricted. Some may be afflicted with it in every respect, though not persistently; others may be persistently afflicted with it concerning one particular matter. An individual may be afflicted with it either alongside an impairment of reason that removes religious accountability—namely, insanity—or while possessing the sound reason required for religious accountability. But I know of no individual who is ignorant of everything and obstinately denies everything, such that he would be a Sophist.

Further evidence that this has not occurred, even according to the speculative theologians, is that many speculative theologians among the people of hadith and Sunnah, as well as others, say:

The rational capacity required for religious accountability is a kind of necessary knowledge, such as knowledge of the necessity of what is necessary, the possibility of what is possible, and the impossibility of what is impossible. They support this by arguing that no rational person is devoid of some such knowledge. This is the position of Judge Abū Bakr, Ibn al-Bāqillānī, Abū al-Tayyib al-Tabarī, Judge Abū Ya‘lā, Ibn ‘Aqīl, and others. Thus, anyone who takes this as

---

## Page 9654

his position cannot consistently claim that a rational person has denied all knowledge except out of obstinacy. It is well known that obstinacy serves some purpose, and no one has any purpose in persistently opposing and denying everything.

At the opposite extreme are people who may imagine that beliefs have no effect on their objects and that rulings do not vary with differing beliefs. Such a person imagines that, if he believes an act to be obligatory or forbidden, anyone who departs from his belief is in the wrong, committing something forbidden or neglecting an obligation, and deserves the blame and punishment due to those who neglect obligations or commit forbidden acts. If it is objected that the other person is acting on an interpretation or exercising independent judgment, he disregards this and says, “He is misguided, mistaken, and deserving of punishment.”

Taken without qualification and applied universally, this view, too, is not held by anyone sound in reason and religion. As with the first extreme, I know of no one who maintains it absolutely and universally. I know only of individuals and groups afflicted with aspects of it and its implications in certain matters. For among the majority of those who maintain that the prophets and the twelve Imams are protected from error in word and deed are some who hold that, if the Imam were to err in an action, this would constitute a fault and grounds for blame.

Between these two widely separated extremes lie other positions, which have given rise to disagreement over whether those exercising independent judgment are correct or mistaken in fundamental and subsidiary matters, as we shall explain, Allah willing.

---

## Page 9655

Section

What is established is that judgments, statements, and beliefs, as explained above, are of two kinds: objective and practical—those that depend on the object of belief and those on which that object depends; those that derive from it and those that serve as its basis.

As for the first—the objective kind, which depends on and derives from the object of belief—it is unaffected by beliefs and does not vary as they vary. The realities of existing things are established in themselves, whether people believe in them or not, and whether their beliefs about them agree or differ. When people hold two contradictory positions concerning them, not everyone exercising independent judgment is correct in the sense that his statement corresponds to and agrees with its object. No rational person maintains this, as explained above.

Anyone who reports that a Muslim scholar—whether ʿUbayd Allah ibn al-Hasan al-ʿAnbarī or someone else—said that everyone exercising independent judgment in fundamental matters is correct, meaning that two contradictory statements are both true and correspond to reality, has attributed a false view to him on the basis of his own misconception. If he refutes and disproves this view, he does well to do so, even though no one actually holds the view being refuted.

However, disputes and disagreements of this kind fall into several categories: a dispute may concern wording alone, meaning alone, each of the two, or the two taken together.

---

## Page 9656

If the disagreement concerns meaning, whether alongside wording or independently of it, the two meanings must either contradict one another or be reconcilable. If the dispute concerns two contradictory meanings, one statement is correct and the other mistaken. As for the remaining categories, both statements may be correct, all may be mistaken, or each—or one of them—may be correct in one respect and mistaken in another. Where both statements are erroneous, the error may not constitute disbelief; if it is not disbelief, it may or may not constitute grave disobedience.

Thus, if someone says that both disputants are correct, meaning that they are correct in some of the categories mentioned above, or that neither will be punished for it, this is possible. But to declare two contradictory statements correct is impossible. Disagreement over meaning is often a matter of diversity rather than opposition and contradiction: one person affirms something, while the other denies something else. They may use different terms for what one denies and the other affirms, or they may use the same term. In the latter case, the contradiction and disagreement concern the wording; as for the meaning, they neither disagree nor contradict one another about it. They may agree on that meaning and both express it; or one may have expressed it, or may express it, while the other neither affirms nor denies it. A verbal dispute may also occur where the meaning is identical rather than varied. Much of the Muslim community’s disagreement about religion falls into this category, whether concerning fundamental or subsidiary matters, the Quran, hadith, or anything else.

An example of diversity involving no disagreement over wording is for one person to say: “The path

---

## Page 9657

that is straight is Islam.” Another says: “It is the Sunnah and the community.” Another says: “It is the Quran.” Another says: “It is the way of servitude to Allah.” This is diversity in the names and descriptions used to explain the straight path, comparable to the names of Allah, His Messenger, and His Book. There is no opposition between them, either in wording or in meaning. The same applies when people offer explanations of those foremost in good deeds, those who take a middle course, and those who wrong themselves: each group mentions a particular category of Muslims, while the designation encompasses them all without contradiction.

An example of diversity involving a verbal dispute arising from a word’s multiple meanings—as has been said, “Most disagreement among rational people arises from names having multiple meanings”—is a dispute among some people over whether Muhammad saw his Lord in this world or in the Hereafter. Some said: “He saw Him in this world, because he saw Him before death.” Others said: “Rather, in the Hereafter, because he saw Him while above the heavens, not while on earth.” The precise distinction is that the term “Hereafter” is used with reference to this life and the life to come, and with reference to the abode of this world and the abode of the Hereafter. Muhammad saw his Lord during this life, in the abode of the Hereafter.

The same applies to many who dispute whether Allah is in heaven or is not in heaven. Those who affirm this state without qualification that Allah is in heaven, as the scriptural texts state and indicate, meaning that He is above the heavens, upon His Throne, distinct from His creation; while others deny

---

## Page 9658

the statement that Allah is in heaven, meaning that heaven neither contains nor confines Him, nor carries nor supports Him. There is no doubt that this meaning is also correct, for Allah is not confined by His creatures. Rather, His Footstool encompasses the heavens and the earth, and the Footstool in relation to the Throne is like a ring cast into a vast desert. Likewise, He is neither dependent upon nor in need of anything else. Rather, He is independent of His creation, the Ever-Living, the Sustainer of all, the One upon whom all depend. Thus, there is no contradiction between the two meanings. These people have, however, erred in rejecting the wording found in the Quran and Sunnah and in supposing that its unqualified use conveys a false meaning.

Some of them may be excused if they encounter someone who uses this wording to mean that heaven supports Him or covers Him from above. If someone judges a person who intends this meaning to be mistaken, he is correct. As for the former, he is correct both in his wording, since he uses the expression found in the scriptural text, and in the meaning explained above, since it is the true meaning indicated by that text. Some of them may, however, err in declaring someone who uses the latter expression a disbeliever when he intends the correct meaning. For someone who intends the correct meaning does not become a disbeliever merely by using an expression, even if he is acting improperly or doing something forbidden.

As for those who explain their statement, “He is not in heaven,” to mean that He is not above the Throne and that there is nothing above the heavens but sheer nonexistence, these are the misguided Jahmiyyah, who oppose the consensus of the prophets and the innate disposition of rational people.

---

## Page 9659

Section

We shall set out several principles concerning this.

The first concerns the effect of beliefs in averting punishment and prescribed legal penalties. We say: Certain rulings of Islamic law are supported by known, conclusive evidence, such as the Quran, the Sunnah established by mass transmission, and clear consensus. These include the obligation of prayer, zakat, pilgrimage and fasting, and the prohibition of unlawful sexual intercourse, wine and usury.

If this evidence reaches a legally accountable person in a manner that enables him to follow it, but he acts contrary to it, neglecting his duty towards Allah and transgressing His limits, then he is undoubtedly mistaken and sinful. Such conduct is grounds for Allah’s punishment in this world and the Hereafter. For Allah has established His proof against His creation through the messengers whom He sent to them as bearers of glad tidings and warners:

“So that people would have no argument against Allah after the messengers.”

The Most High says concerning the people of the Fire:

“Whenever a group is cast into it, its keepers will ask them, ‘Did no warner come to you?’”

“They will say, ‘Yes, a warner did come to us, but we denied him and said: Allah has revealed nothing; you are only in great error.’”

The Most High also says:

“Those who disbelieved will be driven to Hell in groups until, when they reach it, its gates will be opened, and its keepers will say to them, ‘Did messengers from among yourselves not come to you, reciting to you the verses of your Lord and warning you of

---

## Page 9660

the coming of this Day of yours?’ They will say, ‘Yes, but the decree of punishment has become binding upon the disbelievers.’”

However, dispute arises when an act, an occurrence or a practical legal question is addressed by a revealed text that the legally accountable person has no means of knowing or understanding. For example, the relevant Prophetic hadith may be known to someone of whom the jurist is unaware, with nothing known to him that would lead him to it; or its import may be too obscure for the jurist to understand. The same applies when there is no revealed text on the matter at all.

A group of speculative theologians, such as Abū ʿAlī, Abū Hāshim, al-Qādī Abū Bakr and al-Ghazālī, adopted an innovated position concerning matters open to juristic reasoning that resembles the position of antinomian heretics concerning matters explicitly addressed by revealed texts. They maintain that there is no objective ruling with Allah concerning such an occurrence. Rather, the ruling applicable to each legally accountable person follows his own juristic reasoning and belief: whoever believes an act to be obligatory is obliged to perform it, and whoever believes it to be forbidden is forbidden to perform it. They based this on two premises:

The first: A ruling exists only through divine address. Where there is no such address, Allah has no ruling concerning the matter. Thus, if reason establishes no ruling concerning it—either because reason never establishes rulings at all, or because it establishes none in this particular case—it follows that there is no ruling which one person correctly identifies and another mistakenly misses.

The second: It is known that whoever believes something to be obligatory must perform it, and whoever

---

## Page 9661

believes it to be forbidden must refrain from it. Thus, the ruling follows the belief.

They said: “The rulings of Islamic law vary according to the circumstances of legally accountable persons, both in their juristic reasoning and in other respects. This is demonstrated by the agreement of the jurists and Ahl al-Sunnah that juristic reasoning and belief can remove sin and punishment, as the revealed texts establish, and that obligation and prohibition vary according to residence and travel, ritual purity and menstruation, inability and ability, and other such circumstances. Rulings may therefore vary according to beliefs, and, in the absence of a revealed text, the ruling applicable to a jurist exercising independent reasoning may be whatever he believes it to be.” This summarizes their position.

The early generations, the jurists, the Sufis, ordinary people and the majority of speculative theologians reject this position, holding that it contradicts the Quran, the Sunnah and the consensus of the early generations. Indeed, it contradicts sound reason. Abū Ishāq al-Isfarāyīnī and others even said: “This doctrine begins in sophistry and ends in heresy.” What he means is that sophistry makes realities dependent upon beliefs, as we explained earlier. Whoever maintains that obligation and prohibition depend upon beliefs has thus engaged in sophistry concerning practical rulings, even if he does not do so concerning judgments about objective realities. We have already explained that no rational person ordinarily engages in sophistry about everything—whether mistakenly or deliberately, through misguidance or obstinacy, out of ignorance or feigned ignorance.

As for its ending in heresy, this is because it abolishes commands and prohibitions, obligation and prohibition, and the threat of punishment in these matters, leaving a person free to declare something obligatory if he wishes, or, if he wishes,

---

## Page 9662

declare it forbidden, so that all beliefs and actions become equivalent. This is disbelief and heresy.

The response to these people rests on two main points:

First: this position is inherently impossible and untenable, as reason establishes.

Second: even if it were rationally possible, revelation has not sanctioned it. Rather, it contradicts revelation, and its conflict with the scriptural texts and scholarly consensus can be demonstrated.

The first point can be established in several ways:

First, as explained above, every instance of knowledge, belief, or judgment must have an object that is known, believed, or judged, to which the belief conforms and corresponds, whether or not that belief contributes to bringing its object into existence. Consider beliefs concerning action that affect their objects, such as the belief that eating this bread satisfies hunger, or that consuming this poison kills. Although such a belief contributes, for example, to bringing about the act of eating, it must have an object established independently of it: namely, that eating that bread possesses the quality in question.

Although the act of eating does not exist before it occurs, its substrate—the bread—and the eater both exist. If the bread does not possess the property of satisfying hunger when eaten, and the eater does not possess the property of becoming sated upon eating it, then the belief would not be correct, but rather

---

## Page 9663

false, as when someone believes something to be a loaf of bread and eats it, only to discover that it is plaster or gypsum. Although his belief prompted him to eat it, it does not satisfy his hunger, because the belief was false. Likewise, when someone believes that something will benefit or harm him, that belief urges and motivates him to act or refrain from acting. If it corresponds to reality, the benefit is attained and the harm averted, provided that no impediments intervene. Otherwise, merely benefiting from an act or suffering harm through it does not necessitate the occurrence of benefit or harm. This is merely the claim of some ignorant disbelievers:

“If any of you thought well of a stone, it would benefit him.”

They thus make benefiting from something dependent upon believing that it is beneficial.

The polytheists believed that they benefited from the idols concerning which Allah said:

“He invokes one whose harm is closer than his benefit.”

Thus, when someone believes that an act is commanded as a recommendation, for which Allah grants the reward of a recommended act, or as an obligation, for neglecting which He punishes a person as a sinner—or believes that Allah has prohibited it in a corresponding manner—his belief concerns one of three things. It may concern solely an attribute of his Lord, namely commanding and prohibiting, which is an attribute involving a relation to the act, as maintained by a group of theologians and jurists, both among our companions and others. Or it may concern solely a quality of goodness or badness in the act itself, with the command and prohibition disclosing that quality, as maintained by another group of theologians and jurists, both among our companions and others. Or it may affirm both attributes together, pertaining respectively to the command and the act commanded, as maintained by the majority of jurists. He believes that this attribute—which is the legal ruling—exists only because he believes that it is established in

---

## Page 9664

itself and exists independently of his belief—not that, through his belief, he seeks to confer upon the command and the act an attribute they did not previously possess. No jurist exercising independent judgment aims to establish rulings for acts through his own belief, or to institute a religion that Allah has not authorized. Rather, he seeks to believe in Allah’s ruling and religion. Nor does he intend to take acts that are equal both in themselves and with respect to Allah’s command, and then regard one as obligatory upon himself and another as forbidden, without any distinguishing reason specific to those acts. This point deserves careful reflection.

If a believer seeking Allah’s ruling knows that those acts are equal in Allah’s sight, that He has not distinguished between them by any command or prohibition, and that they are equal in themselves, with no distinction between them in goodness or badness, benefit or harm, then this belief of his entails their equality and equivalence. For him subsequently to believe that one is obligatory, so that whoever omits it deserves blame, while another is forbidden, so that whoever performs it deserves punishment, is a contradiction and sophistry in reason, and disbelief and heresy in religion.

As for the former, believing in equality and equivalence is incompatible with believing that one is preferable or superior to another, let alone that one is obligatory and another forbidden. How, then, can a rational person hold these two contradictory beliefs?

Unless he is a foolish disbeliever who says:

“I make this obligatory and that forbidden without any command from Allah or any rational ground for preferring either one.”

If he does this, he is instituting as religion something Allah has not authorized. Moreover, it is a religion whose invalidity is known through reason, since he has deemed acts that are equal

---

## Page 9665

to be obligatory in some cases and forbidden in others, with no reason warranting this distinction except sheer arbitrariness of a kind in which no living creature, sane or insane, ever engages. For if one of the two acts were assumed to offer some particular gratification or pleasure, one could say:

“That provides grounds for preferring it, and constitutes an aspect of goodness according to those who hold that reason can discern goodness; the act would therefore be obligatory on that basis.”

Yet the case under consideration excludes all of this. Once all such considerations are excluded, it becomes clear that believing an act to be good or bad, obligatory or forbidden, depends upon an independently established reality with which that belief either accords or fails to accord. Accordingly, a belief that accords with that reality is correct, while one that conflicts with it is not. It is not the case that the ruling depends upon belief in every respect.

Second:

The inquirer who reasons from evidence in order to ascertain rulings seeks knowledge of what that evidence indicates. If, however, the evidence indicates nothing independently, and what it indicates comes into being only after reflection, then what he seeks is not knowledge of what is indicated, but its existence. This is not the function of evidence, which makes clear what it indicates; rather, it is the function of causes and causal factors, which bring their effects into existence.

There is a great difference between evidence that gives rise to knowledge residing in the heart and knowledge that gives rise to existence in the external world. What the former produces is belief in the mind, whereas what the latter produces is external existence. The two kinds are distinct from one another.

---

## Page 9666

Section

As for practical rulings, beliefs, and pronouncements that determine the status of the matters judged, these include commands and prohibitions, declaring things good or bad, and believing them obligatory or forbidden. Many jurists and speculative theologians call these “religious rulings”; they are also called “subsidiary matters,” “jurisprudence,” and similar names.

These exist in all religious communities and faiths, as well as in worldly affairs such as governance, crafts, transactions, and so forth. These are what we intended to discuss under this principle when we said:

Beliefs may affect religious rulings. Here too, people occupy two extremes and a middle position.

The first extreme:

This is the position of the heretical libertines who disbelieve in revealed laws, warnings of punishment, and punishment in the Hereafter. They hold that these rulings depend entirely upon belief, and that belief is what determines them. Thus, a thing is obligatory only for someone who believes it to be forbidden (*).

They hold that the threatened punishment that befalls such people consists in their souls being tormented by their beliefs concerning commands and prohibitions, obligation and prohibition, and by their belief that, if they commit forbidden acts and neglect obligatory duties, they will be tormented and punished. Thus there remains within

> **Footnotes:**
> Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd stated (p. 155):
> An omission occurred here, apparently because the scribe’s eye skipped ahead—and Allah knows best. This is evident from the context. The missing wording appears to be as follows:
> “Thus, a thing is obligatory only for [someone who believes it to be obligatory, and a thing is prohibited only for] someone who believes it to be prohibited.”

---

## Page 9667

their souls fear, suffering, and the apprehension and imagining of torment. They claim that this pain arising from such belief and imagination is their punishment and torment, and that it stems from what they believe. This is like someone who believes that a lion, a thief, or a highwayman is nearby when none actually exists, and so suffers pain and harm through fear of the danger he believes to be present. Thus, a belief that does not correspond to reality combines with an object of belief whose existence would cause pain, and the soul suffers through this belief and imagination.

The more sophisticated among these adherents—from among the Ismā‘īlīs, the Qarmatians, and certain people who profess Sufism or engage in speculative theology, who are the extremist Murji’a—may say:

“The warnings of punishment conveyed by the divine Scriptures are merely intended to frighten people into refraining from what they have been forbidden, without there being any reality behind them. This resembles sensible people frightening children and the simple-minded with things that have no reality in order to discipline them. It also resembles a warrior deceiving his enemy by making him imagine something frightening, either to deter him from attacking or to gain power over him, and other such practices.”

These people disbelieve in Allah’s messengers, His Scriptures, and the Last Day, and deny His commands and prohibitions, His promises and His warnings. The examples Allah sets forth in the Quran and the accounts He relates of nations that denied the messengers apply to these people. Sufficient evidence lies in the various exemplary punishments Allah has inflicted in this world upon those guilty of disbelief, wickedness, and disobedience. These are tangible, observable realities that cannot be denied. There is no one who has not heard of various instances of them or witnessed some of them. The people of the earth agree that the condition of a truthful, righteous, and just person is not like that of

---

## Page 9668

a lying, wicked, and unjust person. Rather, they observe rewards for good deeds and punishments for evil deeds that offer a lesson and a deterrent, just as they did in the age of ignorance before the messengers came. When the revealed message brought warnings of the Hereafter, it clarified what people had been unaware of.

The second extreme:

This is the extreme of those who are excessively strict and hold that belief has no effect on actions. Indeed, the most extreme among them, such as certain Muʿtazilī theologians, say: “Allah has a ruling for every action; whoever errs concerning it is sinful and will be punished.” Thus, they hold that a Muslim scholar exercising independent judgment is sinful and will be punished for his error whenever evidence from revelation escapes his notice, even though he has striven and exhausted his ability in seeking Allah’s ruling. Such is their position on independent scholarly judgment and belief. Then, if someone neglects an obligation or commits a forbidden act, they say that the threatened punishment must be carried out against him. They therefore hold that sinful Muslims must remain in Hell forever. This is the position of the majority of the Muʿtazila and the Khawārij. The Khawārij, however, declare a person a disbeliever for committing a major sin—or even a minor sin, according to some of them.

As for the Muʿtazila, they say:

“He occupies an intermediate position between the two: neither a believer nor a disbeliever.”

The community that follows the middle course holds that belief may or may not affect rulings, depending on the evidence and the factors involved. This is also what happens in natural matters: the effects of foods and medicines may or may not vary according to the beliefs of the physician and the patient. A person may believe that something has a beneficial or harmful property and consequently benefit from it or suffer harm, even though it has no such property. He may also hold such a belief

---

## Page 9669

without that belief having any effect. Thus, if someone believed that bread and meat were not filling, that belief would make no difference: they would still be filling even if he believed otherwise.

Section

The legal positions of the imams are established from their statements.

As for their actions, our fellow scholars hold two views on whether Imam Ahmad’s legal position may be inferred from his actions.

The first is that it may not, because he could commit a sin, act contrary to his own belief, or act inadvertently, out of habit, in deference to another’s judgment, or for some reason other than the conviction on which he bases his legal rulings. For a person to act upon his knowledge in every situation, and to act only upon knowledge that can serve as the basis for a legal ruling in each situation, he must have a considered view on the matter, recall it, and intend to act upon it without anything diverting him. For, given the ability to act, action depends upon motivation, and motivation consists of awareness and the heart’s inclination.

The second is that his legal position may indeed be inferred from his actions, given what is known of Abū ʿAbd Allāh’s piety, scrupulousness, and renunciation of worldly pleasures. He was among those least likely to sin deliberately. Although we do not claim that he was infallible, the apparent and usual case is that his actions accorded with his knowledge. Thus, what he did may presumptively be taken to represent his legal position. The same applies to anyone whose predominant quality is piety

---

## Page 9670

and scrupulousness. Some are stronger in these qualities than others: the more mindful a man is of Allah and the more he fears Him, the stronger this inference is in his case. Abū ʿAbd Allāh was among the most pious of this community and the greatest in renunciation and scrupulousness. Indeed, he was a forerunner and leader in these qualities, as his life and the lives of others testify—accounts familiar to scholars and ordinary people alike.

Likewise, al-Shāfiʿī’s followers found his explicit statement that selling green broad beans was impermissible, yet he purchased them during his illness. They therefore differed, holding two views on whether a legal position could be inferred from this and attributed to him. They discussed a similar question concerning the holding of two Friday congregational prayers in one locality when he entered Baghdad.

If we hold that an action represents Imam Ahmad’s legal position, may we also say that he regarded what he did as preferable to its alternatives? This inference is weaker than the first. His doing something indicates its permissibility when it is not among his devotional practices. If he performs it as an act of worship, it indicates that he regards it as recommended or obligatory. To establish that he regards it as preferable to something else, however, requires separate evidence. A person often turns from the better course to one that is less excellent because of obstacles to the better course, the conditions it requires, or a lack of motivation.

Moreover, even if his action is permissible, recommended, or preferable, it does not establish a general ruling covering every situation. Rather, its ruling extends only to comparable cases. This is true of all actions: they do not, in themselves, establish a general ruling. Even an action of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, does not in itself establish a general ruling.

It may further be said that the imams’ actions and abstentions fall into categories, just as the actions of the Prophet

---

## Page 9671

may Allah bless him and grant him peace, do. At times, an imam performs an action as an act of worship and religious devotion, indicating that he regards it as recommended. Whether he regards it as preferable to its alternatives, however, requires further consideration.

When he acts without intending worship, there are two views on what his action indicates. Accordingly, drawing conclusions from reports of the imams’ various practices of devotion, renunciation, and scrupulousness depends on several preliminary considerations:

First: Does he regard these practices as good, such that he would state this and issue fatwas accordingly? Or did he perform them without holding such a belief, merely following someone else’s example or out of forgetfulness? The same two views apply here as in the case of permissible acts.

Second: Does he have an inclination toward these practices that accords with his belief? A man’s natural disposition often runs counter to his belief.

Third: Does he regard that practice as better than its alternatives, or does he take the less excellent course for other permissible purposes? The former is more likely.

Fourth: Does he regard it as preferable without qualification, or only in certain circumstances? Allah knows best.

---

## Page 9672

In the name of Allah, the Most Merciful, the Especially Merciful.

The shaykh, imam, and scholar Taqī al-Dīn Ahmad ibn Taymiyyah, foremost among scholars qualified for independent legal reasoning—may Allah sanctify his soul and illuminate his grave—said:

All praise belongs to Allah. We praise Him, seek His help, guidance, and forgiveness, and seek refuge in Allah from the evil within ourselves and from our evil deeds. Whomever Allah guides, none can lead astray; and whomever He leads astray, none can guide. We bear witness that there is no deity worthy of worship except Allah alone, without partner, and we bear witness that Muhammad is His servant and Messenger. May Allah bless him and grant him peace.

Section

On the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, having explained the religion in its entirety—its foundations and branches, its inward and outward aspects, its knowledge and practice. For this principle is the foundation of

---

## Page 9673

the foundations of knowledge and faith. The more firmly a person adheres to this principle, the closer he is to the truth in knowledge and practice. Among those furthest from the truth in knowledge and practice are the Qarmatians and adherents of philosophy who suppose that the messengers did not know the truths of the divine and universal sciences. Such knowledge, they claim, belongs only to those philosophers who attain it. They say that the distinguishing feature of prophethood is imaginative representation, and accord prophethood superiority over other attainments among the masses, but not among people of knowledge. Al-Fārābī and others like him express these and similar views, as do Mubashshir ibn Fātik and his like among the Ismā'īlīs.

Others acknowledge that the Messenger knew these truths, but say that he did not explain them; rather, he addressed the masses through imaginative representation. They thus locate imaginative representation in his discourse, not in his knowledge. This is the view of Ibn Sīnā and others like him.

Still others acknowledge that the messengers knew the truth and explained it, but say that it cannot be known from their words. Rather, it must be known by another route: through reason, according to one group, or through spiritual unveiling, according to another—either philosophical syllogism or Sufi imagination. Only then are the Messenger’s words examined: whatever agrees with that is accepted, while whatever contradicts it is either left uninterpreted or reinterpreted. This is the approach of many Jahmī and Mu'tazilī speculative theologians. It is also the approach of the best among the Bātinīs and philosophers, who hold the Messenger in high esteem and regard him as above ignorance and lying, yet resort to reinterpretation.

---

## Page 9674

Abū Hāmid al-Ghazālī, in discussing people’s approaches to reinterpretation in his book, stated that the philosophers had taken it so far that they cast off all constraints; that the truth lies between the rigidity of the Hanbalīs and the philosophers’ lack of restraint; and that this cannot be known through revelation. Rather, you know the truth through a light cast into your heart. Revelation is then examined: whatever agrees with that, you accept; otherwise, you do not. By philosophers who resort to reinterpretation, he meant the best among them: those who hold the Messenger in too high an esteem to believe that he would lie for the sake of some benefit. Yet these people fell into something akin to what they sought to escape: they accused him of deception, obscuring the truth, and leading people astray—indeed, of presenting falsehood and concealing the truth.

When Ibn Sīnā and others like him recognized that the Messenger’s words could not bear these philosophical reinterpretations—and indeed recognized that he intended the understood meaning of his words—he adopted the approach of imaginative representation. He said that the Messenger addressed the masses through representations that appealed to their imagination, while knowing that the truth of the matter was otherwise. Thus, these people say that the messengers lied for the sake of some benefit. This is the approach of Ibn Rushd the Younger and others like him among the Bātinīs. Those among them who regarded the messengers as above lying accused them of deception and leading people astray, while those who acknowledged that they had explained the truth said that they lied for the sake of some benefit.

As for the people of knowledge and faith, they agree that the messengers said nothing but

---

## Page 9675

the truth and that they made it clear, while recognizing that the messengers are the most knowledgeable of all creation concerning the truth. They are truthful, and their truthfulness is attested: they knew the truth and made it clear. Whoever says that they lied for the sake of some benefit is a brother to those who reject the messengers. Yet, having seen the good and justice they brought about in the world, he could not say that they lied in pursuit of supremacy and corruption. Instead, he said that they lied for the benefit of creation, as is related of Ibn al-Tūmart and others like him.

For this reason, these people distinguish between a prophet and a sorcerer only in terms of good intent: the prophet intends good, whereas the sorcerer intends evil. Otherwise, both perform extraordinary feats which, in their view, arise from powers of the soul, and they regard both as liars. The sorcerer, however, lies in pursuit of supremacy and corruption, whereas the prophet, they claim, lies for the sake of some benefit, since he could not establish justice among people without resorting to some form of lying.

Those among them who knew that prophethood is incompatible with lying about Allah, and that a prophet can only be truthful, said that the messengers did not make the truth clear. Had they merely said that the messengers remained silent rather than explaining it, that would have been less heretical. But instead they said that the messengers made statements whose apparent meaning conveyed falsehood to people, without making the truth clear to them. In their view, the messengers thus combined two things: concealing a truth they did not explain, and presenting statements that indicated falsehood, even though they did not intend that falsehood. They therefore treated the messengers’ words as a kind of equivocal speech in which the speaker intends a true meaning, but the listener understands nothing but falsehood.

To say that they intended equivocation is less heretical than to say that they intended to lie.

---

## Page 9676

And equivocation is a kind of lying, since it conveys a falsehood to the listener’s understanding. For this reason, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Abraham told only three lies, all of them for Allah’s sake.”

These were instances of equivocation, such as his statement about Sarah, “She is my sister,” since no believers were present besides him and her.

These people say that what Abraham and the prophets generally related concerning the unseen consists of lies expressed through equivocation. The majority of speculative theologians, however, do not say this. Rather, they say that the prophets intended to make things clear, not to equivocate. Nevertheless, the Jahmiyyah and others like them say that the truth is not explained in the prophets’ discourse; rather, their discourse contains only what points to falsehood.

Those speculative theologians among the Jahmiyyah, Mu'tazilah, Ash'ariyyah, and others who follow the argument from accidents to establish the existence of the Maker say that the Companions did not explain the foundations of religion—and neither did the Messenger—whether because they were occupied with jihad or for some other reason.

We have discussed these people at length elsewhere and explained that the foundations of the true religion, with which Allah sent down His Book and sent His Messenger—namely, the proofs, demonstrations, and signs that establish it—were explained by the Messenger in the clearest manner. He directed people and guided them to the rational proofs and conclusive demonstrations through which they come to know the truths concerning Allah and establish His lordship and oneness

---

## Page 9677

and His attributes, the truthfulness of His Messenger, the Resurrection, and other matters that need to be known through rational proofs—indeed, even matters that can be demonstrated through rational proofs when such proofs are not necessary. Many matters are known through truthful reports, yet the Messenger also explained the rational proofs establishing them, thus combining both approaches: the transmitted and the rational.

We have explained that the Book and Sunnah establish the foundations of religion not merely through reports, as some among the speculative theologians, hadith scholars, jurists, Sufis, and others mistakenly suppose. Rather, the Book and Sunnah direct people and guide them to the signs, demonstrations, and proofs that clarify the foundations of religion. Those who mistakenly turn away from the rational proofs and conclusive demonstrations contained in the Quran fall into factions when writing about the foundations of religion:

One group begins its books by discussing rational inquiry, proof, and knowledge, maintaining that rational inquiry yields knowledge and is obligatory. They discuss inquiry, proof, and knowledge in general, mingling truth with falsehood. Then, when they reach what constitutes the foundation and proof for those who infer the temporal origination of bodies from the temporal origination of accidents, that argument is an innovation in religion and invalid according to reason.

The second group recognizes that this discourse is an innovation, that it necessarily entails contradicting the Book and Sunnah, and that it gives rise to the assertion that the Quran is created and that

---

## Page 9678

Allah will not be seen in the Hereafter and is not above the Throne, and similar innovations of the Jahmiyyah. They therefore compiled books, beginning them with evidence from the Quran, hadith, and statements of the early generations showing the obligation to adhere firmly to the Book and Sunnah. They mentioned sound points, but they sometimes mixed authentic reports with weak ones and sometimes cited evidence that did not establish the point at issue.

Moreover, they appeal to the Quran only for what it reports, not for the proofs it provides. Thus, they do not mention its proofs establishing Allah’s lordship and oneness, prophethood, and the Resurrection, or explain that it has set out rational proofs demonstrating these matters. This is why they gave their books titles such as Foundations of the Sunnah and the Sharia. They treated faith in the Messenger as already established, with no need to explain the proofs supporting it. The first group therefore criticized them and accused them of ignorance because they had not mentioned the foundational proofs establishing the Messenger’s truthfulness. In turn, the second group accused the first of religious innovation, and even disbelief, because they had laid down principles contrary to what the Messenger taught.

Both groups deserve blame because they turned away from the foundations Allah explained in His Book, which are the foundations, proofs, and signs of the religion. When both groups turned away from them, enmity arose between them, as Allah Most High said:

“So they forgot a portion of what they had been reminded of, and We stirred up enmity and hatred among them until the Day of Resurrection.”

---

## Page 9679

And a third group:

They recognize the negligence of the one group and the transgression and innovation of the other, and therefore censure them. They also censure the intelligent student whose soul yearns to understand the evidence and move beyond uncritical imitation if he follows their path. They say that this path is harmful, that the early generations did not follow it, and other such things that imply its condemnation. This is correct, but it establishes only a general point whose bearing on the matter at issue is unclear. Indeed, someone may accept the approach of the speculative theologians while declaring it an innovation, yet fail to open up the avenues of proof that Allah has set forth in the Quran. These proofs demonstrate that what the Messenger brought is true; by understanding them, an intelligent person moves beyond uncritical imitation, misguidance, innovation, and ignorance.

These people, in their various factions, are further astray because they have not reflected upon the Quran and have turned away from Allah’s signs, which He has explained in His Book, just as others turn away from His signs in creation. Allah Most High said:

“And how many a sign within the heavens and earth do they pass over while turning away from it.”

And He, Most High, said:

“But neither signs nor warnings benefit a people who do not believe.”

And He, Most High, said:

“Indeed, those who do not expect to meet Us, are content with the life of this world and feel secure in it, and those who are heedless of Our signs—”

“Their refuge will be the Fire because of what they used to earn.”

And He, Most High, said:

“This is a blessed Book which We have sent down to you, that they may reflect upon its verses and that people of understanding may take heed.”

And He, Most High, said:

“And We have certainly presented for people in this Quran every kind of example.”

And He, Most High, said:

“And We sent before you only men to whom We revealed—so ask the people of knowledge if you do not know—”

“With clear proofs and scriptures.”

The verse. And He said

---

## Page 9680

Most High:

“And if they deny you, messengers before you were also denied.”

And He, Most High, said:

“And if they deny you, those before them also denied. Their messengers came to them with clear proofs, scriptures, and the enlightening Book.”

There are many such passages, whose detailed discussion belongs elsewhere. The point is that these mistaken people, who have turned away from the rational arguments and conclusive proofs in the Quran, make no mention of the rational inquiry, evidence, and knowledge brought by the Messenger, although the Quran is full of these. The speculative theologians acknowledge the rational arguments in the Quran that establish the foundations of religion, yet they follow other methods, such as the argument from accidents. Some imagine that this was the method of Abraham, Allah’s intimate friend, but they are mistaken.

The philosophers say that the Quran employs rhetorical methods and persuasive premises that convince the general public. They say that the speculative theologians employ dialectical methods, while claiming that they themselves possess conclusive demonstration. Yet they are further removed from demonstration in matters of theology than the speculative theologians, who have greater demonstrative knowledge of theology and universals than they do. The philosophers do, however, engage extensively and in detail with natural philosophy, in which they have distinguished themselves. Theology is quite another matter: they are among the most ignorant of people concerning it and the furthest from knowing the truth about it. Their teacher Aristotle’s discussion of it is scant and full of errors. It is like lean camel meat atop a rugged mountain: the mountain is not easy to climb, nor is the meat fat enough to be worth seeking. This is discussed at length elsewhere.

---

## Page 9681

The Quran brings clear proofs and guidance—clear signs that constitute conclusive evidence. Allah, Most High, said to His Messenger:

“Call to the way of your Lord with wisdom and good exhortation, and debate with them in the best manner.”

The philosophers interpret this in terms of their logical methods of demonstration, rhetoric, and dialectic. This is misguided in several respects, as explained at length elsewhere. Rather, wisdom is knowing the truth and acting upon it. Hearts endowed with understanding and resolve are called through wisdom: the truth is made clear to them in matters of knowledge and action, so they accept it and act upon it. Others acknowledge the truth but have desires that prevent them from following it. These are called through good exhortation, which encourages adherence to the truth and warns against falsehood.

Exhortation consists of commands and prohibitions accompanied by encouragement and warning, as the Most High said:

“If they had done what they were exhorted to do.”

And the Most High said:

“Allah admonishes you never to repeat anything like it.”

Calling through these two methods is for those who accept the truth. Those who do not accept it are to be debated in the best manner. The Quran contains both approaches. Thus, when presenting an argument, it poses questions about clear, demonstrative premises that no one can deny, so that the addressee affirms the truth and acknowledges that falsehood must be rejected. Examples include His words:

“Were they created from nothing, or are they themselves the creators?”

And His words:

“Were We wearied by the first creation? Yet they are in doubt about a new creation.”

And His words:

“Is not He who created

---

## Page 9682

the heavens and the earth able to create their like?” And His words:

“Does man think that he will be left to himself? Was he not a drop of semen emitted? Then he became a clinging clot, and He created and fashioned him, and made from him the two sexes, male and female. Is He not able to give life to the dead?”

And His words:

“Have you considered the semen you emit? Is it you who create it, or are We the Creator?”

And His words:

“They say, ‘Why does he not bring us a sign from his Lord?’ Has there not come to them clear proof of what is in the former scriptures?”

And His words:

“Is it not enough for them that We have sent down to you the Book, which is recited to them?”

And His words:

“Is it not a sign for them that the scholars of the Children of Israel know it?”

And His words:

“Have We not given him two eyes, a tongue and two lips, and shown him the two ways?”

There are many similar passages in which He addresses them through questions that elicit their acknowledgement and admission of the demonstrative premises establishing the intended conclusion. This is among the finest forms of debate through demonstration. For dialectical argument requires only that the opponent concede the premises, even if they are not clear and known. When they are clear and known, however, they are demonstrative.

In its debates, the Quran does not invoke a premise merely because the opponent concedes it, as is the dialectical method of the logicians and others. Rather, it uses propositions and premises that people acknowledge and that are demonstrative. If some accept them while others dispute them, it presents evidence of their soundness, as in His words:

“They did not give Allah His due when they said, ‘Allah has sent down nothing to any human being.’ Say, ‘Who sent down the Book which

---

## Page 9683

Moses brought as a light and guidance for mankind, which you make into separate sheets, displaying some and concealing much? And you were taught what neither you nor your forefathers knew.’” Since this address encompasses both those among the People of the Book who acknowledge Moses’ prophethood and those among the polytheists who deny it, He mentions this in His words:

“Say, ‘Who sent down the Book which Moses brought?’”

He has explained the demonstrations establishing Moses’ truthfulness in several places.

According to the reading “they display them,” as recited by Ibn Kathīr and Abū ‘Amr, the passage is understood as addressing the polytheists, and His words:

“And you were taught what you did not know”

are understood as an argument against the polytheists based on what Muhammad brought. Thus, the proof against the former is Moses’ prophethood, and against the latter, Muhammad’s prophethood. Each has supporting demonstrations, some of which have been explained elsewhere.

According to the majority reading, with the second-person prefix t-, the address is directed to the People of the Book. His words:

“And you were taught what you did not know”

explain what the prophets brought that they had denied. The prophets thus taught them what they had neither accepted nor known. He therefore argued from both what they knew of the accounts of the prophets and what they did not know.

He, Glorified is He, has recounted the story of Moses and displayed his demonstrations and signs, which are among the clearest demonstrations and proofs. Even the magicians whom Pharaoh assembled acknowledged them—and that alone is enough. Then, when Allah made Moses’ truthfulness manifest, and he brought signs that were known with inescapable certainty to be from Allah, and his staff swallowed the ropes and staffs that had been brought

---

## Page 9684

by the magicians, after they had produced mighty magic, bewitched the people’s eyes and struck them with terror. Then, when the truth prevailed and they were humbled, they said:

“We believe in the Lord of all worlds,”

“the Lord of Moses and Aaron.”

Pharaoh said to them:

“Have you believed in him before I gave you permission? He must be your master who taught you magic! I will surely cut off your hands and feet on opposite sides and crucify you on the trunks of palm trees. You will certainly know which of us is more severe and lasting in punishment.”

“They said, ‘We will never prefer you over the clear signs that have come to us’”—that is, clear, certain and conclusive proofs—nor over Him who created us. He is our Creator and Lord, whom we cannot do without. We will not prefer you over these certain proofs and the Creator of all beings.

“So decree whatever you will; you can only decree concerning this worldly life.”

“We have believed in our Lord so that He may forgive us our sins and the magic you compelled us to practise. Allah is better and more lasting.”

Allah has related this account in several places in the Quran, bringing out in each a different kind of lesson and evidence. Likewise, Allah, His Messenger and His Book are called by several names, each conveying a meaning not conveyed by another. There is no repetition in this; rather, the signs are presented in varied ways. Consider the names of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace: Muhammad, Ahmad, the Gatherer, the Last, the Successor, the Prophet of Mercy, the Prophet of Repentance and the Prophet of Battle. Each name conveys a meaning not conveyed by another. Although the person is one, the attributes are various.

---

## Page 9685

Likewise, when the Quran is called the Recitation, the Criterion, Clarification, Guidance, Insights, Healing, Light, Mercy and Spirit, each name conveys a meaning distinct from the others.

The same applies to the names of the Lord, Exalted is He: the Sovereign, the Most Holy, the Flawless, the Granter of Security, the Guardian, the Almighty, the Compeller, the Supremely Great, the Creator, the Originator and the Fashioner. Each name conveys a meaning not conveyed by another. His essence is one, while His attributes are many.

This applies to individual names, and the same holds for complete sentences. An account is expressed in sentences conveying certain aspects of its meaning, then expressed in other sentences conveying other aspects. Although the account itself is one, its attributes are many. Thus, each sentence conveys a meaning not conveyed by the others.

There is no repetition whatsoever in the Quran. Some people, however, have claimed that He repeated the accounts, although relating each one once would have sufficed, and that the wisdom behind this was that Arab delegations would come to the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and the Muslims would teach them some of the Quran, which would suffice for them, and that he would send to the tribes

---

## Page 9686

scattered across the land different surahs. Had the verses and accounts not been repeated, the account of Moses would have reached one people, that of Jesus another, and that of Noah yet another. Allah therefore wished to make these accounts known throughout the earth and bring them to every ear. Such are the words of someone who has failed to appreciate the Quran as it deserves.

Abū al-Faraj confined himself to this answer in his discussion of “oft-repeated,” when addressing the question: “Why was it repeated?”

A fuller discussion belongs elsewhere. Such reiteration consists in presenting different kinds and grouping them into categories, covering all their divisions. Hence some of the early generations explained it as “categories and similitudes.” The point here is to draw attention to the fact that the Quran contains the foundations of religion that truly deserve that name, together with proofs, signs and evidence yielding certainty—unlike what the innovators and heretics have introduced. As al-Rāzī said, despite his familiarity with their methods:

“I have reflected on the methods of speculative theology and the approaches of philosophy, and have found that they neither heal the ailing nor quench a burning thirst. I have found the most direct path to be the way of the Quran. For affirmation, read:

‘To Him ascends good speech.’

‘The Most Merciful rose over the Throne.’

And for negation, read:

‘There is nothing like Him.’

‘They cannot encompass Him in knowledge.’”

He said: “Whoever has experienced what I have experienced will know what I know.”

Goodness, happiness, perfection and righteousness are confined to two things: beneficial knowledge and righteous deeds. Allah sent Muhammad with the best of these, namely guidance

---

## Page 9687

and the religion of truth, as He says:

“It is He who sent His Messenger with guidance and the religion of truth to make it prevail over all religion. And sufficient is Allah as Witness.”

The Most High also says:

“And remember Our servants Abraham, Isaac and Jacob—possessors of strength and insight.”

Thus He mentioned both kinds. Al-Wālibī reported from Ibn ‘Abbās that this means: “Those endowed with strength in worship.” Ibn Abī Hātim said: “Something similar has been reported from Sa‘īd ibn Jubayr, ‘Atā’ al-Khurāsānī, al-Hasan, al-Dahhāk, al-Suddī, Qatādah, Abū Sinān and Mubashshir ibn ‘Ubayd.” As for “insight,” he said: “It means understanding the religion.”

Mujāhid said: “‘Insight’ means sound judgment.” Sa‘īd ibn Jubayr said: “Insight into Allah’s religion and His Book.”

Regarding “possessors of strength and insight,” ‘Atā’ al-Khurāsānī said: “Those endowed with strength in worship, insight, and knowledge of Allah’s command.” A report is also transmitted from Mujāhid, and Qatādah is reported to have said: “They were granted strength in worship and insight into the religion.”

All the sages of the nations esteem these two kinds, including the sages of Greece, India and the Arabs. Ibn Qutaybah said: “Wisdom, among the Arabs, is knowledge and action.”

Righteous deeds consist in worshipping Allah alone, without any partner. This is the religion—the religion of Islam. Knowledge and guidance consist in affirming the Messenger’s reports concerning Allah, His angels, His Books, His messengers, the Last Day and other matters. Beneficial knowledge is therefore faith, and righteous deeds are Islam. Beneficial knowledge comes from Allah’s knowledge, while righteous deeds consist in acting according to Allah’s command. The former is to affirm what the Messenger has reported, and the latter is

---

## Page 9688

to obey him in what he commanded. The opposite of the former is to speak about Allah without knowledge, and the opposite of the latter is to associate with Allah that for which He has sent down no authority. The former is nobler: every believer is a Muslim, but not every Muslim is a believer.

“The Bedouins say, ‘We believe.’ Say, ‘You have not believed; rather, say, “We have submitted.”’”

All groups esteem these two kinds, but what the Messenger brought is the most excellent in both, as Allah says:

“Indeed, this Quran guides to that which is most upright.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would sometimes recite Surah al-Ikhlās and “Say, O disbelievers” in the two rak‘ahs before the dawn prayer. “Say, O disbelievers” concerns worshipping Allah alone, which is the religion of Islam. “Say, He is Allah, One” describes the Most Merciful and concerns speaking of Him and affirming about Him what befits Him, which is faith. This is affirming Allah’s oneness in word, whereas the former is affirming His oneness in practice.

At other times, he would recite in the first of these two rak‘ahs the following words from Surah al-Baqarah:

“Say, ‘We believe in Allah and in what has been revealed to us, and in what was revealed to Abraham, Ishmael, Isaac, Jacob and the descendants, and in what Moses and Jesus were given, and in what the prophets were given by their Lord. We make no distinction between any of them, and to Him we submit.’”

And in the second:

“Say, ‘O People of the Scripture, come to a word that is equitable between us and you’”

through to His words:

“But if they turn away, then say, ‘Bear witness that we are Muslims.’”

---

## Page 9689

Commenting on Allah’s words,

“We will surely question them all about what they used to do,”

Abū al-‘Āliyah said: “Everyone will be questioned about two matters: What did you worship? And how did you respond to the messengers?” The first concerns fulfilling the testimony that none has the right to be worshipped except Allah; the second concerns fulfilling the testimony that Muhammad is the Messenger of Allah.

The Sufis have based their approach on intent. This is indispensable, but only on condition that it be the intention to worship Allah alone in the manner He has commanded. The speculative theologians have based their approach on rational inquiry that yields knowledge. This too is indispensable, but only on condition that the knowledge concern what the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, conveyed, and that the inquiry examine the proofs to which the Messenger directed people—namely, Allah’s signs. Both are indispensable.

Whoever seeks knowledge without intent, or intent without knowledge, is astray. Whoever seeks both without following the Messenger in either is also astray. Rather, as some of the early generations said:

“Religion and faith consist of words, deeds, and following the Sunnah.”

Those versed in the jurisprudence of outward actions discuss outward acts of worship. Those devoted to Sufism and asceticism discuss a person’s purpose and intent. Those engaged in rational inquiry and speculative theology, together with those who study matters of creed among the scholars of hadith and others, discuss knowledge, understanding, and assent—the foundation of intent. They say: “Worship necessarily requires intent, and intent cannot be sound without prior knowledge of the One intended and worshipped.” This is correct.

---

## Page 9690

Thus, it is essential to know the One who is worshipped and how He is to be worshipped. Those who have gone astray among the polytheists, Christians, and their like practise worship and asceticism, but either for someone other than Allah or in ways Allah has not commanded. The only beneficial intent and will is the will to worship Allah alone, and He is to be worshipped only through what He has prescribed, not through religious innovations.

The religion of Islam revolves around these two principles: worshipping Allah alone and worshipping Him through what He has prescribed, not through religious innovations. As for knowledge, understanding, and Sufism, these revolve around knowing what the Messenger conveyed and knowing that what he conveyed is true: either because we know that he speaks nothing but the truth—this is general assent—or because we know that a particular report is true through the signs of its truth that Allah has manifested. For He sent down the Scripture and the Balance, and showed people His signs on the horizons and within themselves until it became clear to them that the Quran is the truth.

Section

As for “practical matters,” which some call subsidiary matters, religious law, and jurisprudence, the Messenger explained these with the utmost clarity. There is nothing Allah commanded, prohibited, permitted, or forbade that he did not clarify. Allah Most High said:

“Today I have perfected your religion for you.”

And Allah Most High said:

“It is not a fabricated account, but a confirmation of

---

## Page 9691

what came before it, a detailed explanation of all things, and guidance and mercy for people who believe.”

Allah Most High said:

“And We have sent down to you the Book as a clarification of all things, and as guidance, mercy, and good tidings for the Muslims.”

Allah Most High said:

“Mankind was one community. Then Allah sent the prophets as bearers of good tidings and warnings, and sent down with them the Scripture in truth to judge between people concerning their differences.”

Allah Most High said:

“By Allah, We certainly sent messengers to nations before you, but Satan made their deeds seem fair to them. He is their ally today, and they will have a painful punishment.”

“We have sent down the Book to you only so that you may clarify for them what they differed over, and as guidance and mercy for people who believe.”

Thus, He—glorified be He—has explained that He sent down the Book to him only so that he might clarify for them what they differed over. Likewise, He has explained that He sent down Scripture with the prophets to judge between people concerning their differences.

Allah Most High said:

“Whatever you differ over, its judgment rests with Allah. Such is Allah, my Lord; in Him I put my trust, and to Him I turn.”

Allah Most High said:

“Allah would not cause a people to go astray after guiding them until He has made clear to them what they must avoid.”

He has therefore made clear to the Muslims everything they must avoid, as He said:

“He has already explained to you in detail what He has forbidden you, except that which you are compelled to eat.”

Allah Most High said:

“If you disagree over anything, refer it to Allah and the Messenger.”

This means referring it to the Book of Allah or to the Messenger’s Sunnah after his death. His words, “If you disagree,” express a condition, and the verb is indefinite within a conditional construction. Thus, whatever they disagree over, they must refer it to Allah and the Messenger. And if the clarification from Allah were not

---

## Page 9692

and the Messenger decisive in settling disputes, they would not have been commanded to refer matters to it.

Allah sent down to the Messenger the Book and wisdom, as He mentions in several places. The Messenger taught his community the Book and wisdom, as Allah said:

“And teaches them the Book and wisdom.”

He would speak of the Book and wisdom in his home, and Allah commanded His Prophet’s wives to remember this, saying:

“And remember what is recited in your homes of Allah’s verses and wisdom.”

Allah’s verses are the Quran, for the Quran itself demonstrates that it was sent down by Allah: it is a sign and evidence pointing to the One who sent it down. As for “wisdom,” several of the early generations said that it is the Sunnah. Others, including Mālik, said that it is knowledge of the religion and acting upon it. Other explanations have also been given, and all of them are true. Wisdom encompasses distinguishing between what is commanded and what is forbidden, and between truth and falsehood, and teaching truth rather than falsehood. This is the Sunnah by which he distinguished truth from falsehood, good deeds from evil deeds, and good from evil. It is reported that he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“I have left you upon a clear path whose night is like its day. None will deviate from it after me except one who is doomed.”

Words to this effect are also reported from ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb, may Allah be pleased with him. Such statements occur frequently in hadiths and reports from the early generations, and are cited in the books that collect these reports. Similar statements are cited by numerous authors in their accounts of the Sunnah, including Ibn Battah, al-Lālakā’ī, and al-Talamankī, as well as earlier authors who compiled works on the Sunnah, such as the associates of

---

## Page 9693

Ahmad, such as ʿAbd Allāh, al-Athram, Harb al-Kirmānī and others, as well as al-Khallāl and others. The purpose here is to establish this point: the Book and the Sunnah are sufficient to address every matter of religion.

As for the consensus of the Muslim community, it is true in itself: the community does not agree upon error. Sound analogical reasoning is likewise true, for Allah sent His messengers with justice and sent down the Balance along with the Book. The Balance encompasses justice and the means by which justice is known. Its being sent down has been explained as Allah inspiring His servants with knowledge of it. Allah and His Messenger treat like cases alike and distinguish between unlike cases.

This is sound analogical reasoning. Allah has set forth every kind of example in the Quran and has explained the truth through sound analogies—that is, the examples He sets forth. Yet sound analogical reasoning accords with the revealed text, just as the Balance accords with the Book. Allah commanded His Prophet to judge according to what He revealed, and commanded him to judge with justice. It is He who sent down the Book, and He sent it down only with justice. Allah Most High said:

“And judge between them by what Allah has revealed.”

“If you judge, judge between them with justice.”

As for the consensus of the Muslim community, it is true: all praise belongs to Allah, the community does not agree upon error. This is how Allah has described it in the Book and the Sunnah. Allah Most High said:

“You are the best nation brought forth for mankind: you enjoin what is right, forbid what is wrong, and believe

---

## Page 9694

in Allah.” This describes them as enjoining everything that is right and forbidding everything that is wrong, just as their Prophet is described in His words:

“Whom they find mentioned in the Torah and the Gospel in their possession: he enjoins upon them what is right and forbids them what is wrong.”

He likewise describes the believers in His words:

“The believing men and believing women are allies of one another. They enjoin what is right and forbid what is wrong.”

If the community were to affirm something in religion that constitutes misguidance, it would not have enjoined what is right or forbidden what is wrong in that matter. Allah Most High also said:

“Thus We have made you a middle nation, so that you may be witnesses over mankind and the Messenger may be a witness over you.”

“Middle” means just and the best. Allah has made them witnesses over mankind and accorded their testimony the standing of the Messenger’s testimony. It is authentically reported that:

“A funeral procession passed by the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and the people spoke well of the deceased. He said, ‘It is assured! It is assured!’ Then another funeral procession passed by him, and they spoke ill of the deceased. He said, ‘It is assured! It is assured!’ They asked, ‘O Messenger of Allah, what do you mean by saying, “It is assured! It is assured!”?’
