“He replied, ‘You spoke well of this deceased person, so I said that Paradise was assured for them. You spoke ill of the other deceased person, so I said that the Fire was assured for them. You are Allah’s witnesses on earth.’”

Since the Lord has made them witnesses, they do not bear false testimony. Thus, if they testify that Allah has commanded something, then He has indeed commanded it; and if they testify that Allah has forbidden something, then He has indeed forbidden it. Were they to testify falsely or mistakenly, they would not be Allah’s witnesses

---

## Page 9695

on earth. Rather, Allah has vouched for the reliability of their testimony, just as He has vouched for the prophets in what they convey from Him: they say nothing about Him but the truth. Likewise, the community testifies concerning Allah only with truth. Allah Most High said:

“Follow the way of those who turn to Me.”

The community turns to Allah, so following its way is obligatory. Allah Most High also said:

“The first and foremost among the Emigrants and the Helpers, and those who followed them in goodness—Allah is pleased with them, and they are pleased with Him.”

He is thus pleased with those who follow the foremost believers until the Day of Resurrection. This indicates that whoever follows them acts in a manner pleasing to Allah, and Allah is pleased only with truth, not falsehood. Allah Most High also said:

“Whoever opposes the Messenger after guidance has become clear to him and follows a way other than that of the believers, We will leave him to what he has chosen and admit him into Hell—what an evil destination!”

ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz used to make a statement that Mālik frequently related from him. He said:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and those in authority after him established practices. Adhering to them affirms the Book of Allah, puts obedience to Allah into practice, and supports the religion of Allah. No one has the right to alter them or to entertain the opinion of anyone who opposes them. Whoever opposes them and follows a way other than that of the believers, Allah Most High will leave him to what he has chosen and admit him into Hell—what an evil destination!”

When al-Shāfiʿī, may Allah be pleased with him, devoted a separate treatment to the principles of jurisprudence, he cited this verse as evidence for consensus, as he and others had done, and as Mālik had related from ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz. The verse indicates that whoever follows a way other than that of the believers is deserving

---

## Page 9696

of the threatened punishment, just as whoever opposes the Messenger after guidance has become clear to him deserves the threatened punishment. It is well known that this description alone warrants that punishment. If the other description were not also covered by the threat, mentioning it would serve no purpose.

There are three views on this matter:

One view is that following a way other than that of the believers consists simply in the opposition to the Messenger mentioned in the verse.

Another view is that opposing the Messenger is independently blameworthy, and likewise, following a way other than that of the believers is independently blameworthy.

A third view is that following a way other than that of the believers warrants blame, as the verse indicates, but this does not entail its being separate from opposing the Messenger; rather, it may necessarily entail it. Thus, everyone who follows a way other than that of the believers is, in reality, opposing the Messenger. Likewise, whoever opposes the Messenger follows a way other than that of the believers.

This is comparable to obeying Allah and the Messenger. Obedience to Allah is obligatory, and obedience to the Messenger is obligatory. Disobedience to either Allah or the Messenger warrants blame, and the two are inseparable: whoever obeys the Messenger has indeed obeyed Allah.

In an authentic hadith, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever obeys me has obeyed Allah; whoever obeys the commander I appoint has obeyed me. Whoever disobeys me has disobeyed Allah; whoever disobeys the commander I appoint has disobeyed me.”

He also said:

“Obedience is only in what is right.”

This means: when the commander I appoint commands what is right, obeying him is part of obeying me. Everyone who disobeys Allah has disobeyed the Messenger, for the Messenger commands what Allah has commanded

---

## Page 9697

—indeed, whoever obeys one messenger has obeyed all the messengers; whoever believes in one of them has believed in them all; whoever disobeys one of them has disobeyed them all; and whoever rejects one of them as false has rejected them all. For each messenger affirms the truthfulness of the others, declares each to be a truthful messenger, and commands obedience to him. Thus, whoever rejects a messenger has rejected the one who affirmed his truthfulness, and whoever disobeys him has disobeyed the one who commanded obedience to him.

For this reason, the religion of the prophets is one, as related in the two Sahīh collections from Abū Hurayrah, may Allah be pleased with him, that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“We prophets share one religion.”

Allah Most High says:

“He has ordained for you the religion He enjoined upon Noah, and that which We have revealed to you, and what We enjoined upon Abraham, Moses, and Jesus: uphold the religion and do not become divided over it.”

Allah Most High also says:

“O messengers, eat of good things and do righteous deeds. Indeed, I know all that you do.”

“And indeed, this religion of yours is one religion, and I am your Lord, so fear Me.”

“But they divided their religion among themselves into sects, each faction rejoicing in what it had.”

Allah Most High also says:

“So set your face toward the religion, inclining to the truth—the natural disposition Allah has instilled in mankind. There is no altering Allah’s creation. That is the upright religion, but most people do not know.”

“Turn to Him in repentance, fear Him, establish prayer, and do not be among those who associate partners with Allah—”

“Those who divided their religion and became sects, each faction rejoicing in what it had.”

The religion of all the prophets is Islam, as Allah has stated in numerous places.

---

## Page 9698

And it is submission to Allah alone. This is achieved only by obeying Him in what He commands at the time. Thus, obeying each prophet was part of the religion of Islam in his time. Facing Jerusalem was part of the religion of Islam before that command was abrogated. Then, when Allah commanded that the Kaaba be faced, facing it became part of the religion of Islam, and facing the Rock was no longer part of it. This is why the Jews and Christians departed from the religion of Islam: they abandoned obedience to Allah and belief in His Messenger, replacing these with what had been altered or abrogated.

Likewise, anyone who invents a religion contrary to the Messenger’s sunnah follows only an altered or abrogated religion. Whatever a person follows in opposition to what the Messenger brought either had been prescribed for a prophet and was then abrogated through Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, or had never been prescribed at all. The latter is like the religions that the devils ordained through their allies. Allah Most High says:

“Or do they have partners who have ordained for them a religion that Allah has not permitted?”

He also says:

“Indeed, the devils inspire their allies to dispute with you. If you obey them, you will indeed be associating partners with Allah.”

And He says:

“Thus We have appointed for every prophet enemies—devils from among mankind and jinn—who inspire one another with embellished speech to deceive. Had your Lord willed, they would not have done so. So leave them and what they fabricate.”

For this reason, whenever one of the Companions expressed an opinion of his own, he would say:

“If it is correct, it is from Allah; if it is mistaken, it is from me and from Satan. Allah and His Messenger are innocent

---

## Page 9699

of it.” This was said by Ibn Mas‘ūd and is also reported from Abū Bakr and ‘Umar.

There are thus three categories. A statement either agrees with what the Messenger said or it does not. If it does not, it either agrees with the revealed law of another prophet or it does not. This third category belongs to altered religion, like the religions of the polytheists and the Magians. Whatever belonged to another prophet’s revealed law but does not agree with his law has been abrogated—for example, the Sabbath and the prohibition of every animal with claws, the fat covering the stomach and intestines, and the fat of the kidneys. Observing the Sabbath as a festival and prohibiting these wholesome foods were prescribed in the law of Moses, but were later abrogated. Indeed, the Messiah said:

“And to make lawful for you some of what was forbidden to you.”

Thus, through the Messiah, Allah abrogated some of the prohibitions in the law of Moses. As for Muhammad, Allah said of him:

“Whom they find described in the Torah and the Gospel they possess. He commands them to do what is right and forbids them from doing what is wrong, makes wholesome things lawful for them and foul things unlawful, and relieves them of their burdens and the shackles that bound them. Those who believe in him, honour him, support him, and follow the light sent down with him—it is they who will prosper.”

All association of partners with Allah belongs to altered religion; Allah has never prescribed it. As He said:

“Ask those of Our messengers whom We sent before you: Did We appoint any gods besides the Most Merciful to be worshipped?”

And Allah Most High said:

“We never sent a messenger before you without revealing to him: There is no god but Me, so worship Me.”

Likewise, the things that the people of the pre-Islamic age of ignorance used to prohibit, which Allah mentions in the Quran,

---

## Page 9700

such as the animals designated sā’iba, wasīla, and hām, and the like, belong to altered religion. Thus, when Allah mentioned these practices in Sūrat al-Mā’ida, He explained that whoever prohibited such things had lied about Allah. In Sūrat al-An‘ām, the Most High mentioned what He had forbidden through Muhammad and through Moses, saying:

“Say: I find nothing in what has been revealed to me that is forbidden for anyone to eat, unless it is carrion, flowing blood, or swine flesh—for it is impure—or a sinful offering over which a name other than Allah’s has been invoked. But whoever is compelled by necessity, neither desiring it nor exceeding the limit—surely your Lord is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.”

“To the Jews We forbade every animal with claws; and of cattle and sheep We forbade them their fat, except what is on their backs or entrails or is attached to a bone. Thus We recompensed them for their transgression, and We are indeed truthful.”

Likewise, He said later:

“To the Jews We forbade what We have already related to you.”

He thus made clear that what the polytheists prohibited had not been forbidden through either Moses or Muhammad. These two were the ones who brought scriptures containing what is lawful and unlawful, as the Most High said:

“Say: Then bring a scripture from Allah that gives better guidance than these two, and I will follow it.”

The Most High also said:

“And before it was the Scripture of Moses, a guide and a mercy.”

And the Most High said:

“Say: Who sent down the Scripture that Moses brought?”

Up to His words:

“And this is a blessed Scripture which We have sent down, confirming what came before it.”

When the jinn heard the Quran, they said:

“We have heard a Scripture sent down after Moses, confirming what came before it, guiding to the truth and to a straight path.”

And Waraqa ibn Nawfal said:

---

## Page 9701

“Indeed, this and what Moses brought come from the same source.” Al-Najāshī said likewise.

The Quran and the Torah are thus two scriptures from Allah; no scripture giving better guidance than these two has come from Him. Each is an independent foundation, and the religion contained in them is one and the same. Each affirms the attributes of Allah Most High and commands worship of Him alone, without partner. Both therefore teach Allah’s oneness in profession and practice, as do the two surahs of pure devotion:

“Say: O disbelievers,”

and

“Say: He is Allah, One.”

As for the Psalms, David brought no law other than that of the Torah. The Psalms contain only praise of Allah, supplication, and general commands and prohibitions concerning His religion, obedience to Him, and worship of Him.

As for the Messiah, he said:

“And to make lawful for you some of what was forbidden to you.”

He thus permitted some forbidden things for them, while largely following the law of the Torah. For this reason, anyone who followed the Messiah had to read the Torah and follow its teachings, since the Gospel was supplementary to it.

The Quran, however, is self-sufficient: it has not left its followers in need of any other scripture. Rather, it encompasses all the good contained in the other scriptures, along with many additional teachings not found in them. For this reason, it confirms the Scripture that came before it

---

## Page 9702

and stands as a guardian over it, affirming the truth contained in those scriptures, invalidating what has been distorted in them, and abrogating what Allah has abrogated. It thus affirms the true religion, which constitutes most of their contents, and invalidates the altered religion that was not originally in them, as well as the small portion of their contents that has been abrogated. What has been abrogated is indeed very little compared with what remains in force and is reaffirmed.

All the prophets share one religion. Believing in any of them necessarily entails believing in all the others, and obeying any of them entails obeying all the others. The same applies to denial and disobedience.

No prophet may deny another prophet. Rather, if he knows of him, he affirms him; otherwise, he believes without qualification in everything Allah has revealed. He also commands obedience to whomever Allah has commanded people to obey. Thus, whoever believes in Muhammad has believed in every prophet; whoever obeys him has obeyed every prophet; whoever denies him has denied every prophet; and whoever disobeys him has disobeyed every prophet. Allah Most High says:

“Indeed, those who disbelieve in Allah and His messengers, wish to make a distinction between Allah and His messengers, say, ‘We believe in some and disbelieve in others,’ and seek to take a path in between—it is they who are truly disbelievers.”

Allah Most High also says:

“Do you then believe in part of the Scripture and disbelieve in another part? What recompense is there for those among you who do this except disgrace in this worldly life? And on the Day of Resurrection they will be consigned to the severest punishment. Allah is not unaware of what you do.”

Whoever denies these messengers by rejecting messengership itself has explicitly declared that he denies them all. Hence Allah Most High says:

“The people of Noah denied the messengers,”

although He had not

---

## Page 9703

sent anyone to them before Noah. Allah Most High also says:

“And the people of Noah—when they denied the messengers, We drowned them.”

Likewise, any heretic or adherent of philosophy who disparages the messengers as a whole, as discussed above, by claiming that they did not know the truth or did not explain it, thereby denies all the messengers. Such people are like those of whom He says:

“Those who deny the Book and that with which We sent Our messengers will come to know, when, with shackles around their necks and in chains, they are dragged through scalding water, then burned in the Fire.”

Allah Most High also says:

“When their messengers came to them with clear proofs, they rejoiced in the knowledge they possessed, but they were overwhelmed by what they used to ridicule. Then, when they saw Our punishment, they said, ‘We believe in Allah alone and reject what we used to associate with Him.’ But their faith could not benefit them once they had seen Our punishment. Such has been Allah’s established way with His servants. And there the disbelievers lost everything.”

Allah Most High says concerning al-Walīd:

“He thought and deliberated. May he be destroyed! How he deliberated! Again, may he be destroyed! How he deliberated! Then he looked, then frowned and scowled, then turned away arrogantly and said, ‘This is nothing but magic handed down. This is nothing but the speech of a human being.’”

Among the People of the Book are those who believe in messengership in principle but deny some messengers, such as the Messiah and Muhammad. Because they believed in some and disbelieved in others, they were truly disbelievers.

Many philosophers and esotericists, and many practitioners of speculative theology and Sufism, neither explicitly deny the messengers nor believe in the reality of prophethood and messengership. Rather, they acknowledge their excellence in general, while maintaining that others are more knowledgeable

---

## Page 9704

than they are; or that they did not explain the truth, or obscured it; or that prophethood is an emanation flowing into souls from the Active Intellect, akin to what a sleeper sees in a dream. Nor do they acknowledge exalted angels, jinn, and the like. These people acknowledge some attributes of the prophets but not others, and some of what they were given but not the rest. They do not acknowledge everything the prophets were given.

An individual among them may be worse than those Jews and Christians who acknowledged all the attributes of prophethood but denied some of the prophets. For what these latter acknowledge of what the prophets brought is greater in significance and extent: they acknowledge that Allah created the heavens and the earth in six days; they acknowledge the coming of the Resurrection; they acknowledge that He alone must be worshipped, without any partner; and they acknowledge the revealed laws upon which the prophets agreed. The former, however, deny all this and acknowledge only part of the law of Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

Thus, the Jews and Christians are less disbelieving than the heretical esotericists, adherents of philosophy, and their like. Yet any Jews or Christians who have joined these people combine both kinds of disbelief, since they believe neither in all the prophets’ attributes nor in every prophet individually.

Such people are frequently found in lands ruled by disbelievers, just as people of both kinds are also found among those who profess Islam. When living under the rule of Muslims and the People of the Book, they were hypocrites, with hypocrisy proportionate to what they harbored

---

## Page 9705

of disbelief. Hypocrisy can be partial, and disbelief can be partial and can increase and decrease, just as faith can be partial and can increase and decrease. Allah Most High says:

“Postponement of the sacred months is only an increase in disbelief.”

And He says:

“Whenever a surah is revealed, some of them say, ‘Which of you has this increased in faith?’ As for those who believe, it increases them in faith, and they rejoice.”

“But as for those in whose hearts is disease, it adds impurity to their impurity, and they die as disbelievers.”

And He says:

“We send down of the Quran that which is healing and mercy for the believers, but it only increases the wrongdoers in loss.”

And He says:

“What has been sent down to you from your Lord will surely increase many of them in transgression and disbelief.”

And He says:

“Allah increases those who are guided in guidance.”

And He says:

“In their hearts is a disease, so Allah has increased their disease.”

And He says:

“Indeed, those who believed, then disbelieved, then believed, then disbelieved, and then increased in disbelief.”

Many authors on speculative theology refute the People of the Book only on matters they say can be known through reason, such as the Christian doctrine of the Trinity and their rejection of Muhammad. They do not debate them concerning other fundamentals of religion. This is a shortcoming on their part and a departure from the Quran’s approach. For Allah explains in the Quran how they contradicted the prophets and censures them for doing so; the Quran is filled with such passages. Since disbelief and faith concern messengership and prophethood, once the ways in which they contradicted the prophets become clear, their disbelief becomes evident.

---

## Page 9706

When those speculative theologians established a religion for themselves through the speculative theology they had innovated—such as inferring the temporal origination of bodies from accidents—they supposed that this constituted the fundamentals of religion. Even if what they said were true, it would be only a part of religion; what, then, if it is false?

In my refutation of the Christians, I have described their opposition to all the prophets, as well as their opposition to sound reason, in ways that make their disbelief plain. Hence its title: “The Correct Response to Those Who Altered the Religion of the Messiah.” The argument against them concerns two matters: first, their alteration of the Messiah’s religion; and second, their rejection of Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

The argument against the Jews concerns their rejection of those who came after Moses, down to the Messiah, and then their rejection of Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Allah mentions this in Surah al-Baqarah, saying:

“We certainly gave Moses the Scripture and sent messengers after him in succession. We gave Jesus, son of Mary, clear proofs and strengthened him with the Holy Spirit. Is it that whenever a messenger came to you with what your souls did not desire, you grew arrogant, rejecting some and killing others?”

“They say, ‘Our hearts are covered.’ Rather, Allah has cursed them for their disbelief, so little do they believe.”

Then He says:

“When a Book came to them from Allah, confirming what they already had—although previously they had prayed for victory over those who disbelieved—when there came to them what they recognized, they disbelieved in it. So Allah’s curse is upon the disbelievers.”

And so on, until He mentioned that they turned away

---

## Page 9707

from Allah’s Book altogether and followed sorcery. He said:

“When a messenger from Allah came to them, confirming what they already had, a group of those who had been given the Scripture cast Allah’s Book behind their backs as though they did not know.”

“And they followed what the devils recited during the reign of Solomon.”

Up to His words:

“They certainly knew that whoever purchased it would have no share in the Hereafter. Wretched indeed was that for which they sold themselves, if only they knew.”

“Had they believed and feared Allah, a reward from Allah would have been better, if only they knew.”

We condemn the Christians for the religious excess and association of partners with Allah that they introduced, for rejecting the Messenger, and for the monasticism they invented. We do not praise them for it, since they invented it, and every religious innovation is misguidance. However, if someone who practices such an innovation seeks the truth, he may be pardoned, leaving his deed wasted and of no benefit. This is the kind of misguidance for which a person is excused, receiving neither punishment nor reward.

For this reason, He said:

“Not of those who have incurred Your wrath, nor of those who have gone astray.”

The one who incurs wrath is punished by that very wrath, whereas the one who goes astray misses the intended goal: mercy and reward. Yet he may not be punished as the former is; rather, he is cursed and cast out. Thus, the account of Zayd ibn ‘Amr ibn Nufayl relates that the Jews said to him, “You will not enter our religion until you take your share of Allah’s wrath.” The Christians said to him, “Until you take your share of Allah’s curse.”

Al-Dahhāk and a group of others said, “Hell has levels: the uppermost is for the sinners of this community, the next for the Christians, and the next for the Jews.” Thus they placed the Jews below the Christians.

---

## Page 9708

And the Quran testifies that the polytheists and the Jews are more hostile towards the believers than those who say, “We are Christians.” Greater hostility means greater disbelief. Thus, the Jews’ disbelief is more severe than that of the Christians, even though the Christians are more ignorant and further astray. The former are punished for their deeds because they knew the truth and stubbornly abandoned it, thereby incurring Allah’s wrath. The latter, through their misguidance, were deprived of the reward of the rightly guided, cursed, and excluded from what the rightly guided deserve. Then, if the proof is established against them and they do not believe, they deserve punishment, for the designation “misguidance” is broad in scope.

As recorded in an authentic hadith, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to say in his Friday sermon:

“The best speech is Allah’s speech, and the best guidance is the guidance of Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. The worst matters are those newly introduced into religion, and every religious innovation is misguidance.”

He did not say, “Every misguidance is in the Fire.” Rather, someone may stray from the truth despite intending to reach it and striving earnestly to find it, yet being unable to do so; such a person is not punished. He may also carry out some of what he has been commanded to do, earning a reward for his efforts, while being forgiven for the error through which he strayed from the truth of the matter.

Many scholars of the early and later generations who exercised independent judgment said and did things that were religious innovations without knowing that they were innovations. This was either because they considered weak hadiths authentic, because they understood Quranic verses in ways that were not intended, or because they adopted an opinion while scriptural texts concerning the matter had not reached them.

---

## Page 9709

When a person fears his Lord as much as he can, he is included in His words:

“Our Lord, do not hold us accountable if we forget or make a mistake.”

An authentic hadith records that Allah said, “I have done so.” A fuller discussion of this belongs elsewhere.

The point here is that the Messenger explained the whole religion through the Quran and Sunnah, and that consensus—the consensus of the Muslim community—is valid, for this community does not agree upon misguidance. Sound analogical reasoning is likewise valid and accords with the Quran and Sunnah.

The well-known verse cited as evidence for consensus is His statement:

“Whoever opposes the Messenger after guidance has become clear to him and follows a path other than that of the believers—We will leave him to what he has chosen.”

Some say that this verse does not establish the point at issue. Its censure, they argue, applies to someone who combines both acts, and this is undisputed. Alternatively, it applies to someone who follows a path other than the believers’ path by virtue of which they are believers, namely following the Messenger; this too is undisputed. Or they argue that the believers’ path means reasoning from the Quran and Sunnah, which is likewise undisputed. These and similar arguments are advanced by those who say that the verse does not establish the point at issue.

Others say that it does indeed establish an unqualified obligation to follow the believers. They have resorted to strained arguments to support this, as is well known from their discussions, yet have not given satisfactory answers to the objections raised by the former group.

---

## Page 9710

The third, middle position is that the verse establishes the obligation to follow the believers’ path and the prohibition of following any other path, alongside the prohibition of opposing the Messenger after guidance has become clear to a person. It indicates that each of these two acts is blameworthy, as explained above, but does not rule out their being inseparable, as was discussed regarding obedience to Allah and the Messenger.

We therefore say: censure must attach either to opposing the Messenger alone; to following a path other than the believers’ path alone; to neither act individually, but only to the two together; to each act even when it occurs independently of the other; or to each because it necessarily entails the other.

The first two possibilities are invalid, for if only one of the two were the ground for censure, mentioning the other would serve no purpose. The claim that neither act individually incurs censure is certainly invalid, since opposing the Messenger warrants the threatened punishment irrespective of those who follow him. Nor does the verse establish that each act incurs censure even when it occurs independently of the other, for the threat in it concerns the two together.

The remaining possibility is that each of the two acts warrants the threatened punishment because it necessarily entails the other. The same may be said of disobeying Allah and the Messenger, or of contravening the Quran and Islam. Thus one may say: “Whoever contravenes the Quran and Islam,” or “Whoever departs from the Quran and Islam, is among the people of the Fire.” Similar to this is His statement:

“Whoever disbelieves in Allah, His angels, His Books, His messengers, and the Last Day has certainly gone far astray.”

Disbelief in any one of these foundations necessarily entails disbelief in the others. Thus, whoever disbelieves in Allah disbelieves

---

## Page 9711

in them all. Whoever disbelieves in the angels disbelieves in the Books and the messengers, and thus disbelieves in Allah, since he has denied His messengers and His Books. Likewise, whoever disbelieves in the Last Day denies the Books and the messengers, and is therefore a disbeliever.

The same applies to His words:

“O People of the Book, why do you mix truth with falsehood and conceal the truth knowingly?”

He censures them for both qualities. Each warrants censure, and the two are inseparable. Thus He forbids both together in His words:

“Do not mix truth with falsehood or conceal the truth knowingly.”

For whoever confounds truth with falsehood, covering it with falsehood and thereby falling into error, necessarily conceals the truth that has come to appear false. Were he to make it clear, the falsehood with which the truth was obscured would disappear.

The same applies to opposing the Messenger and following a path other than that of the believers. Whoever opposes him has followed a path other than theirs—this is evident. Whoever follows a path other than theirs has likewise opposed him. For He has made this a ground for the threatened punishment, showing that it is a factor warranting censure. Whoever departs from their consensus has unquestionably followed a path other than theirs, and the verse establishes that this deserves censure.

If it is said, “The verse only censures this when it is accompanied by opposition to the Messenger,” we reply: “That is because the two are inseparable.” Everything upon which the Muslims have reached consensus has been explicitly stated by the Messenger. Whoever opposes them therefore opposes the Messenger, just as whoever opposes the Messenger opposes Allah. This, however, entails that everything upon which consensus has been reached has been explained by the Messenger—and this is indeed correct.

---

## Page 9712

Thus, there is never a matter on which consensus has been reached without an explanation from the Messenger concerning it. Some people, however, may be unaware of that explanation while knowing of the consensus. They therefore cite the consensus as evidence, just as someone who does not understand a text’s import may cite that text as evidence. This constitutes a second proof alongside the text, like the parables set forth in the Quran. Consensus likewise provides another proof, as when it is said: “This is established by the Book, the Sunnah, and consensus.”

Each of these foundations points to the truth, and they are inseparably connected. Whatever consensus establishes is also established by the Book and the Sunnah. Whatever the Quran establishes has been received from the Messenger; thus, both the Book and the Sunnah are received from him. There is no matter on which consensus has been reached without a text concerning it.

Some people have cited matters on which there is consensus but supposedly no text, such as mudāraba (profit-sharing investment partnerships). This is not so. Rather, mudāraba was widely known among them in the pre-Islamic era, especially among Quraysh, whose predominant occupation was trade. Owners of capital would entrust their wealth to those who conducted business with it. Before his prophethood, the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, travelled on trading journeys with other people’s wealth, including that of Khadījah. Most of the goods in the caravan accompanied by Abū Sufyān were held under mudāraba arrangements with Abū Sufyān and others.

When Islam came, the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, approved this practice. His Companions would travel with other people’s wealth under mudāraba arrangements, and he did not prohibit it. The Sunnah comprises his sayings, actions, and tacit approvals. Since he approved this practice, it was established by the Sunnah.

---

## Page 9713

The well-known report from ʿUmar concerning this, narrated by Mālik in al-Muwattaʾ and relied upon by the jurists, relates that Abū Mūsā sent some money which he had lent to ʿUmar’s two sons. They traded with it and made a profit. ʿUmar demanded that the entire profit be taken for the Muslims, because Abū Mūsā had singled them out for this loan rather than the rest of the army. One of them said to him:

“Had the money been lost, we would have borne the loss. How, then, can you have the profit while we bear the liability?”

One of the Companions said to him:

“Treat him as the managing partner in a mudāraba.”

So he treated the transaction as a mudāraba. That Companion said this only because mudāraba was already familiar among them, and the Messenger’s time was still recent; it had not arisen after him. He therefore knew that it had been familiar among them during the Messenger’s lifetime, just as farming and other occupations, such as tailoring and butchery, had been.

Accordingly, in matters on which consensus has been reached, some jurists exercising independent judgment may have known of no relevant text and arrived at their conclusions through reasoning that agreed with the text, while other scholars possessed that text.

Ibn Jarīr and a group of scholars say:

“Consensus can only be established on the basis of a text they have transmitted from the Messenger.”

They maintain this while also affirming the validity of analogical reasoning.

We do not stipulate that they must all have known the text and conveyed its meaning, as reports are transmitted. Rather, we have surveyed the matters on which consensus has been reached and found that all of them are addressed by texts. Many scholars were unaware of the relevant text yet agreed with the body of scholars. Likewise, a scholar may argue by analogy in a matter on which there is a consensus unknown to him, and thus agree with that consensus. Similarly, a matter may be addressed by a specific text, while some scholars argue for it on the basis of a text’s general wording, as Ibn Masʿūd and others did when citing His words:

“For those who are pregnant, their waiting period is until they give birth.”

Ibn Masʿūd said:

---

## Page 9714

“The shorter surah on women was revealed after the longer one”—that is, after al-Baqara.

His words,

“their waiting period is until they give birth,”

mean that the waiting period ends at childbirth alone. If she were required to observe the longer of the two waiting periods, her waiting period would not end simply upon giving birth. ʿAlī, Ibn ʿAbbās and others included her within the general scope of both verses. Yet the specific text concerning the case of Subayʿa al-Aslamiyya supported Ibn Masʿūd’s position.

Likewise, when they disagreed over whether a woman married without a stipulated dowry was entitled to the customary dowry for a woman of her standing if her husband died, Ibn Masʿūd ruled, on the basis of his own judgment, that she was entitled to it. They subsequently narrated the hadith of Birwaʿ bint Wāshiq, which supported that ruling. ʿAlī, Zayd and others, however, disagreed with him, saying that she was entitled to no dowry.

It is thus established that a jurist exercising independent judgment may issue a ruling based on a text’s general wording or on analogy, while a specific text concerning the case, unknown to him, agrees with his ruling. No single case is known in which they agreed that there was no relevant text. Rather, in most matters over which they disagreed, some of them cited scriptural texts as evidence. Some appealed to a text, as in the case of a pregnant widow; others argued that both verses encompassed her. The opposing group maintained that she came under the verse concerning pregnancy alone, and that the verse specifying months applied to women who were not pregnant, just as the verse specifying menstrual cycles applied to women who were not pregnant.

Likewise, when they disagreed over declaring something forbidden, those who regarded such a declaration as an oath cited His words:

“Why do you forbid

---

## Page 9715

what Allah has made lawful for you, seeking to please your wives? And Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.”

“Allah has prescribed for you a way to release yourselves from your oaths.”

Likewise, when they disagreed over whether an irrevocably divorced woman was entitled to maintenance or accommodation, one group cited the hadith of Fātima and argued that the accommodation mentioned in the Quran concerned a revocably divorced woman. The other group maintained that it applied to both.

The implications of scriptural texts may be subtle, and Allah grants some people a special understanding of them. As ʿAlī said: “Except an understanding of His Book that Allah grants a servant.”

A text may also be clear yet escape the attention of a jurist exercising independent judgment. Such is the case with dry ablution for someone in a state of major ritual impurity, which is clearly addressed in two Quranic verses. When Abū Mūsā cited this evidence to Ibn Masʿūd, someone present said: “ʿAbd Allāh did not know what to say, except that he said: ‘If we allowed them this concession, one of them would soon resort to dry ablution whenever he felt the cold.’”

Ibn ʿAbbās, Fātima bint Qays and Jābir stated that the divorced woman mentioned in the Quran is one whose divorce is revocable, citing His words:

“You do not know; perhaps Allah will bring about a new situation after that.”

What new situation could He bring about after three divorces?

A group of scholars cited His words as evidence that ʿumra is obligatory:

“Complete Hajj and ʿumra for Allah.”

Those who prohibited converting Hajj into ʿumra also cited this verse. Others say: “He was commanded only

---

## Page 9716

to complete it. Likewise, the Lawgiver commanded that it be completed.” Regarding such a conversion, they also said:

“Whoever cancels his ʿumra without proceeding to Hajj has not completed it. But if he cancels it in order to perform Hajj that same year, he has completed what he began: he began Hajj alone, then performed ʿumra in conjunction with Hajj. Had this not constituted completion, the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, would not have commanded his Companions to do it in the year of the Farewell Pilgrimage.”

They also disagreed over who is meant by “the one in whose hand is the marriage tie,” and over His words:

“Or you have touched women,”

and other such matters, which this is not the place to examine exhaustively. But as for any particular question on which they agreed that no scriptural text, whether clear or subtle in its implications, could be cited as evidence, I know of none.

When most of them maintained that a grandfather counts as a father, they cited the Quran as evidence, namely His words:

“As he drove your parents out of Paradise.”

Ibn ʿAbbās said: “Had the jinn thought that humans called a father’s father a jadd (grandfather), they would not have said:

‘Exalted is the majesty (jadd) of our Lord.’”

He means that he is simply a father, though a more distant father than another.

It is also reported that ʿAlī and Zayd argued by analogy. Whoever claims that they unanimously abandoned all reliance on reasoned opinion and analogy is therefore mistaken. Likewise, whoever claims that there were questions on which none of them spoke except on the basis of reasoned opinion and analogy is mistaken. Rather,

---

## Page 9717

each of them spoke according to the knowledge he possessed. Whoever discerned evidence in the Book cited it, and whoever discerned evidence in the Balance cited it. Sound proofs do not contradict one another, but some scholars may fail to perceive how they agree, or fail to recognize the weakness of one of them.

The Companions had an understanding of the Quran that eludes most later scholars, just as they knew aspects of the Sunnah and the Messenger’s circumstances that most later scholars do not know. They witnessed the Messenger and the revelation, saw him firsthand, and knew things about his words, deeds, and circumstances from which they could establish their conclusions—things unknown to most later scholars. Lacking that knowledge, these later scholars sought rulings through what they regarded as consensus or analogy.

Any later scholar who says, “Consensus is the basis of most of the Sharia,” is merely describing his own situation: his deficient knowledge of the Book and the Sunnah has made him dependent on it. This resembles their statement, “Most cases require analogy because the scriptural texts provide no indication of their rulings.” Such a statement comes only from someone unfamiliar with the Book and the Sunnah and how they indicate rulings.

Imam Ahmad—may Allah be pleased with him—said that there is no question that the Companions did not address, either directly or through a comparable case. When lands were conquered and Islam spread, every category of human activity presented itself, and they addressed these matters on the basis of the Book and the Sunnah. Only in a few questions did some of them speak on the basis of reasoned opinion. Most of them did not invoke consensus as evidence, nor did they need it, for they themselves were the people whose agreement constituted consensus; there was no consensus before them. But when the Successors came, ʿUmar wrote to Shurayh: “Judge according to what is in the Book of

---

## Page 9718

Allah. If you find nothing there, then judge according to the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah. If you find nothing there, then judge according to the judgments of the righteous before you.”

Another version reads: “Then according to what the people have agreed upon.” Umar gave precedence to the Book, then the Sunnah. Likewise, Ibn Masʿūd said the same as he did. Umar gave precedence to the Book, then the Sunnah, then consensus. Similarly, Ibn Abbās would issue fatwas according to the Book, then the Sunnah, then the practice of Abū Bakr and Umar, in accordance with the Prophet’s words:

“Follow the two who come after me: Abū Bakr and Umar.”

These reports are established from Umar, Ibn Masʿūd, and Ibn Abbās, who were among the Companions best known for issuing fatwas and judgments. This is the correct approach.

However, a group of later scholars said: “The scholar exercising independent judgment should begin by examining consensus. If he finds it, he should pay no attention to anything else. If he finds a scriptural text that contradicts it, he should believe that it was abrogated by a text that has not reached him.” Some of them said: “Consensus abrogated it.”

The correct approach, however, is that of the early generations. For if a scriptural text contradicts consensus, there must be a text supporting that consensus which establishes that the former text has been abrogated. As for the possibility that the Muslim community has lost the unabrogated text while preserving the abrogated one, this never occurs. It would mean attributing to the community the preservation of what it was forbidden to follow and the loss of what it was commanded to follow, whereas it is protected from that. Moreover, ascertaining consensus may frequently—or usually—be impossible. For who is there who

---

## Page 9719

knows all the opinions of the scholars who exercise independent judgment? Scriptural texts, by contrast, can readily be known.

They judged according to the Book first because the Sunnah does not abrogate the Book. Nothing in the Quran, therefore, is abrogated by the Sunnah; rather, if anything in it is abrogated, the text that abrogates it is also in the Quran. One therefore gives nothing else precedence over the Quran. If one does not find the ruling there, one then seeks it in the Sunnah. Nothing in the Sunnah is abrogated except by the Sunnah itself: neither consensus nor anything else abrogates the Sunnah, nor may consensus be set against it.

Most versions of these reports say, “If he does not find it.” A person seeking a ruling may not find it in the Sunnah even though it is there; the same applies to the Quran. Thus, if one does not find it in the Quran, one may seek it in the Sunnah. If it is in the Sunnah, what the Sunnah contains will not contradict what is in the Quran. Likewise, sound consensus contradicts neither the Book nor the Sunnah.

Completed, with praise to Allah and by His help. May His blessings and peace be upon the best of His creation, Muhammad, and his family.

He also said—may Allah have mercy on him—after an earlier discussion:

We shall set out a “comprehensive principle” concerning this subject for the entire Muslim community. We say:

A person must have general principles to which particular cases are referred, so that he may speak with knowledge and justice. He must then understand how the particular cases actually occurred. (*) Otherwise, he will remain mired in falsehood and ignorance concerning particular cases, and in ignorance and injustice concerning general principles, giving rise to great corruption.

We say: People have discussed whether scholars exercising independent judgment are correct or mistaken, and whether they incur sin or not, in subsidiary and foundational questions. We shall set out some comprehensive and beneficial principles.

The first principle:

Can everyone, through independent judgment, ascertain the truth in every disputed question? If someone cannot, but exercises independent judgment and expends his utmost effort without reaching the truth, instead stating what he believes to be the actual truth, although it is not in fact the truth: does he

## Page 9721

deserve to be punished or not? This is the fundamental issue underlying this question.

There are three views on this fundamental issue, each held by a group of theoretical thinkers:

The first is the view that Allah has established evidence by which the truth can be known in every question, so that anyone who exercises independent judgment and expends his utmost effort can ascertain the truth. Anyone who fails to ascertain the truth in a foundational or subsidiary question does so only because he has neglected his duty, not because he is incapable.

This is the well-known position of the Qadariyyah and the Mu‘tazilah. It is also the position of a group of speculative theologians outside these groups. They further say:

As for theoretical questions, there is conclusive evidence by which their truth can be known. Anyone who fails to ascertain it has therefore not expended his utmost effort in seeking the truth and thus incurs sin.

As for practical questions of religious law, they hold two positions:

The first is that these are like theoretical questions: there is conclusive evidence concerning every question, and anyone who goes against it incurs sin. Those who hold this position say that only one person is correct in any foundational or subsidiary question, and everyone other than the person who is correct incurs sin because he is mistaken. In their view, error and sin necessarily go together. This is the position of Bishr al-Marīsī and many of the Mu‘tazilah of Baghdad.

The second is that, if there is conclusive evidence concerning practical questions, then anyone who goes against it

---

## Page 9722

incurs sin and is mistaken, just as in theoretical questions. If, however, there is no conclusive evidence concerning them, then Allah has no intrinsic ruling on them; rather, Allah’s ruling for each person exercising independent judgment is whatever conclusion his judgment leads him to. Those who hold this position agree with the first group that error and sin necessarily go together and that everyone who is mistaken incurs sin.

They disagree with them, however, concerning questions open to independent judgment, saying that there is no conclusive evidence concerning them. In their view, probable opinion rests on no evidence; it is merely a matter of people’s inclinations towards one thing rather than another. They thus treat beliefs based on probability as akin to acts of will. They claim that there is, in reality, no ruling to be sought through independent judgment, and that sin is, in reality, an indication that outweighs another indication. This is the position of Abū al-Hudhayl al-‘Allāf and his followers, such as al-Jubbā’ī and his son. It is also one of al-Ash‘arī’s two positions, and the better-known of them. It is the position adopted by Judge al-Bāqillānī, Abū Hāmid al-Ghazālī, Abū Bakr ibn al-‘Arabī, and their followers. We have discussed this at considerable length elsewhere.

Their opponents, such as Abū Ishāq al-Isfarāyīnī and others among the Ash‘arīs and beyond, say: “This position begins in sophistry and ends in heresy.” This is the position of those who say that everyone exercising independent judgment on practical questions open to such judgment is correct both in reality and outwardly. For, in their view, a person cannot exercise independent judgment and be mistaken, except in the sense that certain matters remain hidden from him. What remains hidden from him, however, is not Allah’s ruling, either

---

## Page 9723

for him or for others like him. As for someone who is mistaken—that is, mistaken concerning matters established by conclusive evidence—they hold that he incurs sin.

The second position on the underlying issue is that someone exercising independent judgment on the basis of evidence may be able to discover the truth or may be unable to do so. If he is unable, however, Allah Most High may punish him or may leave him unpunished, for He may punish whomever He wills and forgive whomever He wills without any cause whatsoever, solely by His will. This is the position of the Jahmiyyah, the Ashʿarīs, and many jurists and followers of the four imams and others.

They then say:

“It is known through revelation that every disbeliever will be in the Fire. We therefore know that Allah will punish every disbeliever, whether he exercised independent judgment but was unable to recognize the religion of Islam, or did not exercise independent judgment at all. As for Muslims who disagree, if their disagreement concerns subsidiary questions, most of them say that these entail no punishment. Some say this is because the Lawgiver has pardoned error in such matters, and that this is known through the consensus of the early generations that one who errs in them incurs sin. Others say it is because error in matters of probability is impossible, as previously mentioned concerning some of the Jahmiyyah and Ashʿarīs.

“As for matters established by conclusive evidence, most of them hold that whoever errs in them incurs sin, saying that revelation indicates this. Some, however, do not hold him to be sinful.”

This is the meaning of the position attributed to ʿUbayd Allah ibn al-Hasan al-ʿAnbarī: he did not deem sinful those who erred in exercising independent judgment from among

---

## Page 9724

this community, whether in fundamental or subsidiary matters. The majority of both groups—the speculative theologians and the proponents of juristic opinion—rejected this position of ʿUbayd Allah. Others, however, say:

“This is the position of the early generations and the leading authorities in issuing fatwas, such as Abū Hanīfah, al-Shāfiʿī, al-Thawrī, Dāwūd ibn ʿAlī, and others. They do not deem someone sinful for an error in independent judgment, whether in fundamental or subsidiary matters, as Ibn Hazm and others have reported from them. This is why Abū Hanīfah, al-Shāfiʿī, and others accepted the testimony of adherents of deviant doctrines, except the Khattābiyyah, and regarded prayer behind them as valid. A disbeliever’s testimony against Muslims, however, is not accepted, nor may prayer be performed behind him.”

They said:

“This is the position known from the Companions, those who followed them in righteousness, and the imams of the religion: they do not declare anyone who errs in exercising independent judgment to be a disbeliever, an evildoer, or sinful, whether in a matter of practice or of knowledge.”

They said: “The distinction between subsidiary and fundamental matters originated only among the proponents of religious innovation: the speculative theologians, the Muʿtazilah, the Jahmiyyah, and those who followed their path. This position then passed to people who discussed it in works on the principles of jurisprudence without understanding its true nature or its deeper implications.”

They said:

“Besides being an innovation introduced into Islam, unsupported by the Quran, the Sunnah, or consensus—and never advocated by any of the early generations or the imams—this distinction between fundamental and subsidiary matters is also rationally invalid. For those who distinguish between what they designate as questions of

---

## Page 9725

fundamentals and subsidiary matters have not drawn a valid distinction between these two categories. Rather, they have proposed three or four distinctions, all of them invalid.”

Some of them have said:

“Fundamental matters concern knowledge and belief, requiring only knowledge and belief, whereas subsidiary matters concern practice and require action.”

They said:

“This distinction is invalid. Some practical matters are such that denying them constitutes disbelief—for example, the obligation of the five daily prayers, zakat, and fasting the month of Ramadan, and the prohibition of unlawful sexual intercourse, usury, injustice, and indecencies.

“Conversely, there are matters of knowledge in which those who disagree incur no sin. Examples include the Companions’ disagreement over whether Muhammad saw his Lord; their disagreement concerning certain reports—whether the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, actually said something, and what he meant by it; their disagreement over whether certain words belong to the Quran; and their disagreement concerning certain meanings of the Quran and Sunnah—whether Allah and His Messenger intended such-and-such. Likewise, people disagree over the subtleties of speculative theology, such as the indivisible atom, the likeness of bodies, the persistence of accidents, and similar matters. Such disagreements do not warrant declaring anyone a disbeliever or an evildoer.”

They said:

“Practical matters involve both action and knowledge. If errors in these matters are forgiven, then errors in matters involving knowledge alone, without action, are all the more deserving of forgiveness.”

---

## Page 9726

Others have said:

“Fundamental matters are those supported by conclusive evidence, while subsidiary matters are those not supported by conclusive evidence.”

The others replied:

“This distinction is also mistaken. Many practical matters are supported by evidence that is conclusive to those acquainted with it, while others are unaware of it. Some are conclusive by consensus, such as the prohibition of forbidden acts and the binding nature of evident obligations. Yet if someone denies them out of ignorance and on the basis of a mistaken interpretation, he is not declared a disbeliever until the proof has been established against him. Thus, during the time of ʿUmar, a group of people, including Qudāma, considered drinking wine lawful and believed it was permissible for them. The Companions did not declare them disbelievers before explaining their error to them, whereupon they repented and recanted. During the Prophet’s lifetime, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, some people ate after dawn had broken until they could distinguish a white thread from a black thread. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did not deem them sinful, let alone declare them disbelievers, although their error was beyond doubt.

“Likewise, Usāma ibn Zayd killed a Muslim man, and his error was beyond doubt. The same applies to those who found a man among his sheep. He said, ‘I am a Muslim,’ but they killed him and took his property. Their error was beyond doubt. Likewise, Khālid ibn al-Walīd killed members of Banū Jadhīma and took their property; he was unquestionably mistaken. The same applies to those who performed dry ablution up to their armpits, and to ʿAmmār, who rolled in the dust because he was in a state of major ritual impurity, just as an animal rolls. Indeed, those who entered a state of major ritual impurity and neither performed dry ablution nor prayed were also unquestionably mistaken. In our own time, if some people in a remote region embraced Islam and did not

---

## Page 9727

know that Hajj is obligatory, or did not know that wine is forbidden, they would not be subjected to the prescribed punishment for that. The same applies if they had grown up somewhere where this was unknown.

During the time of ʿUmar, a woman committed fornication. When she confessed, ʿUthmān said, “She speaks of it as openly as someone who does not know that it is forbidden.” When it became clear to the Companions that she knew it was forbidden, they did not subject her to the prescribed punishment. Yet considering fornication lawful is unquestionably an error.

If a man swears to something, believing it to be as he has sworn, and it then proves otherwise, he is unquestionably mistaken, but there is consensus that he bears no sin. Likewise, according to the majority, no expiation is required of him. Someone who eats believing that dawn still remains is unquestionably mistaken if it becomes clear to him that he ate after dawn. He bears no sin, though there is disagreement over whether he must make up the fast. The same applies to someone who believes that the sun has set, only for it to prove otherwise. There are many such examples.

Concerning Allah Most High’s words in the Quran:

“Our Lord, do not hold us accountable if we forget or make a mistake,”

Allah Most High said:

“I have done so.”

He made no distinction between an unquestionable error in a matter established conclusively and one in a matter established on probable evidence. As for a probable error, one cannot be certain that it is an error unless the person has unquestionably erred.

They said: “Whoever says that someone who makes a mistake in a matter established conclusively or on probable evidence thereby sins has contradicted the Quran, the Sunnah, and the consensus of the early generations.”

---

## Page 9728

They said:

“Furthermore, whether a matter is certain or probable is relative to the circumstances of those who hold the belief; it is not a property of the proposition itself. A person may be certain of things that he knows self-evidently, or through reports whose truth is established for him, while someone else has neither certain nor probable knowledge of them. A person may also be intelligent, sharp-minded, and quick to understand, grasping truths with certainty that another person cannot even conceive of and has neither certain nor probable knowledge of.

“Certainty and probability thus depend on the evidence that has reached a person and his ability to reason from it. People differ in both respects. Whether a matter is certain or probable is therefore not an inherent property of the disputed proposition, such that one could say, ‘Everyone who disagrees with it has contradicted something certain.’ Rather, it describes the condition of the person examining the matter, reasoning about it, and holding the belief. This varies from one person to another. It is thus clear that this distinction does not hold consistently in either direction.”

Some of them drew a third distinction, saying:

“Fundamental matters are those known through reason. Every question concerning knowledge that reason can apprehend independently is therefore a fundamental matter, disagreement with which renders a person an unbeliever or a transgressor. Subsidiary matters are those known through revelation.”

They said: “The first category includes questions concerning the divine attributes and decree; the second includes questions concerning intercession and the departure from Hell of those who committed major sins.”

---

## Page 9729

They may therefore be told:

“The reverse of what you have stated is more appropriate, for classifying someone as an unbeliever or a transgressor is a matter of revealed law, not a judgment that reason can reach independently.”

Later, he said:

“Accordingly, if error in rational questions said to constitute the fundamentals of religion amounts to unbelief, then those who follow these methods—which are rationally unsound and religious innovations—are the unbelievers, not those who disagree with them. If error in these questions does not amount to unbelief, then those who disagree with them are not unbelievers. Thus, on either assumption, their opponent is not an unbeliever according to the judgment of Allah and His Messenger.

“Yet it is characteristic of the people of innovation to invent doctrines that they make obligatory in religion. Indeed, they make them an indispensable part of faith, declare anyone who disagrees with them an unbeliever, and deem it lawful to shed his blood. Such is the conduct of the Khawārij, the Jahmiyyah, the Rāfidah, the Muʿtazilah, and others.

“The people of the Sunnah, however, do not invent doctrines, nor do they declare someone an unbeliever for exercising independent judgment and reaching an erroneous conclusion, even if he opposes them and considers it lawful to shed their blood. Likewise, the Companions did not declare the Khawārij unbelievers, despite the latter’s declaring ʿUthmān, ʿAlī, and their supporters unbelievers and considering it lawful to shed the blood of Muslims who disagreed with them.

“What these speculative theologians say concerning these questions—judging people correct or mistaken, sinful or free of sin, unbelievers or not unbelievers—stems from their reliance on the two positions mentioned earlier: the position of the Qadariyyah, who hold that everyone who reasons from evidence is capable of knowing the truth

---

## Page 9730

and therefore everyone who fails to know it is punished; and the position of the Jahmiyyah Jabriyyah, who say that the servant has no power over anything whatsoever. Rather, Allah punishes solely by His will: He punishes someone who has never committed any sin and grants bliss to someone who has disbelieved and transgressed. Many later scholars have agreed with them on this.

“They say that Allah may punish children and the insane, even though they have never committed any sin. Some of them assert categorically that the children of unbelievers will be punished in the Hereafter, while others regard this as possible and say, ‘I do not know what will happen.’ They also regard it as possible that the most sinful of those who face the qiblah in prayer may be forgiven without any cause whatsoever, while a righteous man may be punished for a minor sin, even though his good deeds are like mountains—without any cause whatsoever, but solely by Allah’s will. The underlying principle shared by both groups is that an agent endowed with power and choice gives preference to one of two identical alternatives over the other without any determining factor.” And so on, to the end of what he quoted—may Allah have mercy on him.

He then said:

“This makes clear the third position on this fundamental question: not everyone who exercises independent judgment and reasons from evidence is able to know the truth, and only someone who fails to do what is commanded or does what is forbidden deserves the threatened punishment. This is the position of the jurists and imams, the position known to have been held by the early generations of the Muslim community, and the position of the majority of Muslims. It combines what is correct in the two positions.”

---

## Page 9731

Thus, what is correct in the first position, that of the Jahmiyyah, is the point on which they agreed with the early generations and the majority: not everyone who seeks the truth, exercises independent judgment, and reasons from evidence is able to know the truth in the matter. Rather, people’s capacities in this regard vary.

The Qadariyyah say that Allah Most High has given all those accountable before Him equal capacity. He has not singled out the believers for anything that gave them an advantage over the unbelievers and enabled them to believe, nor singled out the obedient for anything that gave them an advantage over the disobedient and enabled them to obey. This is among the views of the Qadariyyah, the Muʿtazilah, and others in which they have contradicted the Quran, the Sunnah, the consensus of the early generations, and sound reason, as has been explained at length elsewhere.

For this reason, they say that everyone who reasons from evidence possesses the full capacity needed to attain knowledge of the truth. Yet it is well known that when people are uncertain of the direction of the qiblah while travelling, they are all commanded to exercise their judgment and use the available evidence to determine its direction. Some can ascertain its direction, while others cannot, and so make a mistake, supposing that a particular direction is that of the qiblah when they are mistaken. Nevertheless, such a person is obeying Allah and incurs no sin by praying in that direction, for Allah Most High does not burden any soul beyond its capacity. Their inability to ascertain its direction is like a person’s inability to face it—as with someone who is bound, someone in fear, a prisoner, or a sick person unable to turn towards it.

For this reason, the correct view concerning the second fundamental question is that of those who say that Allah punishes no one in the Hereafter except someone who has disobeyed Him by neglecting

---

## Page 9732

what He commanded or by doing what He prohibited. On this point, the Muʿtazilah agreed with the main body of Muslims, unlike the Jahmiyyah and their followers among the Ashʿariyyah and others, who said instead that He punishes those who have committed no sin, or words to that effect.

These people then argue against the Muʿtazilah, denying that reason establishes obligations and prohibitions, by citing the words of Allah Most High:

“We never punish until We have sent a messenger.”

Yet this is also evidence against them, ruling out punishment altogether before messengers have been sent, whereas they hold that punishment before the sending of messengers is possible. The former say that He punishes someone to whom He has sent no messenger because that person has committed acts whose evil is known through reason. The latter say that He even punishes those who have never done anything evil, such as children.

This contradicts the Quran, the Sunnah, and reason as well. Allah Most High says:

“We never punish until We have sent a messenger.”

And Allah Most High says of the people of the Fire:

“Whenever a group is cast into it, its keepers ask them, ‘Did no warner come to you?’”

“They will say, ‘Yes, a warner did come to us, but we denied him and said, “Allah has revealed nothing; you are only in great error.”’”

Thus, He—glorified and exalted be He—has stated in universally inclusive terms that whenever a group is cast into the Fire, its keepers ask whether a warner came to them, and they acknowledge that a warner did indeed come to them. Hence, no group enters the Fire without a warner having come to them. Whoever has received no warner will therefore not enter the Fire.

And He says:

“That is because your Lord would not destroy towns unjustly while their people were unaware.”

That is, this is for this reason. Thus, it is known that He does not punish someone who is unaware unless there has come to him

---

## Page 9733

a warner. This also indicates that such punishment is an injustice from which He—glorified be He—is far exalted.

Moreover, Allah Most High has stated in several places that He does not burden any soul beyond its capacity, as in His words:

“Allah does not burden any soul beyond its capacity.”

And His words:

“As for those who believe and do righteous deeds—We do not burden any soul beyond its capacity.”

And His words:

“No soul is burdened beyond its capacity.”

And His words:

“Allah does not burden any soul beyond what He has given it.”

He commanded people to fear Him to the best of their ability, saying:

“So fear Allah as much as you are able.”

The believers supplicated to Him, saying:

“Our Lord, do not lay upon us a burden like that which You laid upon those before us. Our Lord, do not burden us with what we have no strength to bear.”

He replied:

“I have granted this.”

These texts thus indicate that He does not require any soul to do what it is incapable of doing, contrary to the determinist Jahmiyyah. They also indicate that He does not hold accountable those who err or forget, contrary to the Qadariyyah and the Muʿtazilah. This is the decisive word on this matter.

Thus, whoever strives to reach a judgment by examining the evidence—whether a leader, judge, scholar, one engaged in inquiry, mufti, or anyone else—if he exerts himself and examines the evidence, fearing Allah to the best of his ability, then this is precisely what Allah has required of him. He is obedient to Allah and deserving

---

## Page 9734

of reward if he fears Him to the best of his ability. Allah will not punish him at all, contrary to the determinist Jahmiyyah. He is correct in the sense that he is obedient to Allah, though he may or may not know the actual truth. This is contrary to the Qadariyyah and the Muʿtazilah, who say that everyone who exerts his utmost comes to know the truth. This is false, as explained above. Rather, everyone who exerts his utmost deserves reward.

The same applies to unbelievers: if someone in a land of unbelief receives the call of the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and knows that he is Allah’s Messenger, believes in him and in what was revealed to him, and fears Allah to the best of his ability, as al-Najāshī and others did, yet cannot emigrate to the land of Islam or observe all the ordinances of Islam—because he is prevented from emigrating and openly practising his religion, and has no one to teach him all the ordinances of Islam—then he is a believer and one of the people of Paradise.

Such was the believing man from Pharaoh’s household among Pharaoh’s people, and such was Pharaoh’s wife. Indeed, such was Joseph, the man of truth—peace be upon him—among the people of Egypt. They were unbelievers, and he could not put into practice among them everything he knew of the religion of Islam. He called them to worship Allah alone and to believe, but they did not respond to him. Allah Most High relates the words of the believing man from Pharaoh’s household:

“Joseph had already come to you before with clear proofs, yet you remained in doubt about what he brought you, until, when he died, you said, ‘Allah will never send a messenger after him.’”

Likewise, although al-Najāshī was king of the Christians, his people did not obey him in

---

## Page 9735

embracing Islam; rather, only a few of them embraced it with him. For this reason,

“When he died, there was no one there to offer the funeral prayer for him, so the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—offered it for him in Medina. He took the Muslims out to the prayer ground, arranged them in rows, and offered the funeral prayer for him. He informed them of his death on the very day he died, saying, ‘A righteous brother of yours from among the people of Abyssinia has died.’”

He had not observed many, or even most, of the ordinances of Islam because he was unable to do so. He did not emigrate, take part in jihad, or perform the pilgrimage to the House. Indeed, it has been reported that he did not perform the five daily prayers, fast the month of Ramadan, or pay the prescribed zakat, because these practices would have become apparent to his people, who would have objected, and he was unable to oppose them.

We know with certainty that he could not judge among them according to the Quran. Yet Allah had enjoined His Prophet in Medina, whenever the People of the Book came to him, to judge among them only according to what Allah had revealed to him. He also warned him against their luring him away from any part of what Allah had revealed to him. This includes imposing the prescribed penalty of stoning for unlawful sexual intercourse on a person of the requisite marital status, judging fairly in matters of blood money, and treating the highborn and the lowly equally in matters of bloodshed: a life for a life, an eye for an eye, and so forth.

Al-Najāshī could not judge according to the Quran, for his people would not allow him to do so. Often a man assumes office as a judge—or even a ruler—among the Muslims and the Tatars, wishing to implement certain measures of justice, but is unable to do so because others prevent him. Allah does not burden any soul beyond its capacity.

---

## Page 9736

And ‘Umar ibn ‘Abd al-‘Azīz faced hostility and harm because of some of the justice he established; it has even been said that he was poisoned for that reason. Al-Najāshī and others like him will enjoy bliss in Paradise, even though they did not observe those ordinances of Islam that were beyond their ability. Rather, they judged according to such laws as they were able to apply.

This is why Allah counted these people among the People of the Book. Allah Most High says:

“Indeed, among the People of the Book are those who believe in Allah, in what has been revealed to you, and in what was revealed to them, humbly submitting to Allah. They do not exchange Allah’s revelations for a small price. They will have their reward with their Lord. Indeed, Allah is swift in reckoning.”

A group of the early generations said that this verse was revealed concerning al-Najāshī. This is reported from Jābir, Ibn ‘Abbās, and Anas. Others said that it concerned him and his companions, as al-Hasan and Qatādah stated. This is also what the Companions meant, though he was the one whom the others obeyed, for the wording of the verse is plural; it was not intended to refer to just one person.

It is reported that ‘Atā’ said: “It was revealed concerning forty people from Najrān, thirty from Abyssinia, and eight from among the Byzantines. They followed the religion of Jesus, then believed in Muhammad—may Allah bless him and grant him peace.”

These commentators did not mention those who believed in the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—in Medina, such as ‘Abd Allāh ibn Salām and others who had been Jews, or Salmān al-Fārisī and others who had been Christians. These people, however, had become numbered among the believers, so it is not said of them:

“Indeed, among the People of the Book are those who believe in Allah and in what has been revealed to you

---

## Page 9737

and in what was revealed to them.” Nor does anyone say that Jews and Christians, after embracing Islam, emigrating, and joining the ranks of the Muslims who emigrated and strove in Allah’s cause, are described as belonging to the People of the Book—that is, as still numbered among them despite having believed in the Messenger. As Allah Most High says concerning someone killed by mistake:

“Hostile to you, while he is a believer, then a believing slave must be freed.”

And His words:

“And if he belongs to a people with whom you have a treaty.”

Thus, he belongs to the enemy people, but he had believed and was unable to emigrate, openly profess his faith, or observe its ordinances. Allah therefore called him a believer because he had fulfilled as much of faith as he could.

Likewise, there was a group of believers in Mecca who concealed their faith and were unable to emigrate. Allah Most High says:

“Indeed, when the angels take the souls of those who have wronged themselves, they ask, ‘What condition were you in?’ They reply, ‘We were oppressed in the land.’ The angels say, ‘Was Allah’s earth not spacious enough for you to emigrate?’ Their refuge will be Hell—what an evil destination!

“Except the helpless among men, women, and children who can devise no means and find no way.

“For these, there is hope that Allah will pardon them. Allah is ever Pardoning, All-Forgiving.”

Thus He, glorified be He, excused those who were helpless and unable to emigrate.

Allah Most High also says:

“And why do you not fight in the cause of Allah and for the oppressed among men, women, and children who say, ‘Our Lord, bring us out of this town whose people are oppressors, and appoint for us from Yourself a protector, and appoint for us from Yourself a helper’?”

Those people were unable to practise their religion, so they were relieved of whatever lay beyond their ability. So if

---

## Page 9738

this applies to someone who was a polytheist and then believed, what, then, of someone who belonged to the People of the Book and then believed?

As for His words:

“If he belongs to a people hostile to you, while he is a believer,”

it has been said that this refers to someone who bears the outward appearance of the enemy—for example, by standing in their ranks. The person who kills him is therefore excused, since he is commanded to fight him: no blood money is due from him, but expiation is obligatory. This is the view of al-Shāfiʿī and one of the two opinions reported from Ahmad. It has also been said that this instead refers to someone who embraced Islam but did not emigrate, as Abū Hanīfa maintains. Yet expiation is required here. Another view is that, if he belonged to the enemy people, he would have no heir; his blood money is therefore not given to the enemy people, and only expiation is obligatory. This applies whether he was known to be a believer and was killed by mistake, or was thought to be an unbeliever. This is the apparent meaning of the verse.
