Some Quran commentators have said that this verse was revealed concerning ʿAbd Allāh ibn Salām and his companions, as reported from Ibn Jurayj, Muqātil, and Ibn Zayd—meaning His words:

“And indeed, among the People of the Book.”

Others have said that it concerns the believers among the People of the Book. This amounts to the first view; if a general meaning is intended, however, it amounts to the second. This is Mujāhid’s view, and Abū Sālih also related it from Ibn ʿAbbās. The view that includes Ibn Salām and others like him within its scope is weak, for these people are believers outwardly and inwardly in every respect. It is not permissible to say of them:

“And indeed

---

## Page 9739

among the People of the Book are those who believe in Allah and in what was revealed to you and what was revealed to them, humbling themselves before Allah. They do not exchange Allah’s verses for a small price. Those will have their reward with their Lord. Indeed, Allah is swift in reckoning.”

First, Ibn Salām embraced Islam as soon as the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, arrived in Medina. He said: “When I saw his face, I knew that his was not the face of a liar.” The passage concerning the People of the Book in Surah Āl ʿImrān, however, was only revealed when the delegation from Najrān arrived in the year 9 or 10 AH.

Second, Ibn Salām and others like him belong to the ranks of the Companions and believers, and he is among the best of them. The same applies to Salmān al-Fārisī: he is not described as belonging to the People of the Book.

These people have rewards like those of the other believers; indeed, they are given their reward twice over. They observe all the ordinances of Islam, so their reward is greater than is conveyed by saying:

“Those will have their reward with their Lord.”

Moreover, their circumstances were plain and well known, and no one had any doubts about them. What benefit, then, would there be in making such a statement about them?

This would be no different from saying: “People who were polytheists or belonged to the People of the Book have entered Islam.” Everyone knows this, since Islam is a religion that was not known before Muhammad, peace and blessings be upon him. Thus, everyone who entered it had previously been either a polytheist or one of the People of the Book—either a person of the Book or

---

## Page 9740

a gentile. What benefit, then, is there in stating this? The case of al-Najāshī and his companions was different: they outwardly adhered to many Christian practices, so their situation could be unclear.

For this reason, accounts of the occasion of this verse’s revelation mention that when al-Najāshī died, the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, offered the funeral prayer for him. Someone said: “Are you praying for this Christian foreigner while he is in his own land?” This verse was then revealed. This is reported from Jābir, Anas ibn Mālik, and Ibn ʿAbbās, who were among the Companions who participated in the funeral prayer for al-Najāshī. This differs from the case of Ibn Salām and Salmān al-Fārisī: had the Prophet offered the funeral prayer for either of them, no one would have objected.

This helps show that among those who outwardly profess Islam are hypocrites for whom the funeral prayer must not be offered, as was revealed concerning Ibn Ubayy and others like him. Conversely, someone living in a land of disbelief may be a believer for whom the funeral prayer is offered, as in the case of al-Najāshī.

A similar passage occurs when Allah Most High mentions the People of the Book, saying:

“If the People of the Book had believed, it would have been better for them. Among them are believers, but most of them are defiantly disobedient.”

“They will cause you no more than annoyance. If they fight you, they will turn their backs and flee; then they will receive no help.”

“Humiliation has been laid upon them wherever they are found, except under a covenant from Allah and a covenant from people. They have incurred Allah’s anger, and destitution has been laid upon them. That is because they used to reject Allah’s signs and kill the prophets without right. That is because they disobeyed and continually transgressed.”

---

## Page 9741

“They are not all alike. Among the People of the Book is an upright community who recite Allah’s verses during the night and prostrate themselves.”

“They believe in Allah and the Last Day, enjoin what is right and forbid what is wrong, and hasten to do good deeds. They are among the righteous.”

It has been said that this verse was revealed concerning ‘Abd Allāh ibn Salām and his companions. It has also been said that His words, “Among them are believers, but most of them are defiantly disobedient,” refer to ‘Abd Allāh ibn Salām and his companions. This interpretation, and Allah knows best, is of the same kind as the preceding one: these men no longer belonged to the People of the Book. Rather, what is meant is someone who outwardly belongs to them and yet is a believer, but cannot do what the believers who emigrate and fight in Allah’s cause are able to do. Such was the believer from Pharaoh’s household: he belonged to Pharaoh’s household, yet he was a believer. Hence Allah Most High said:

“A believing man from Pharaoh’s household, who concealed his faith, said, ‘Would you kill a man merely for saying, “My Lord is Allah,” when he has brought you clear proofs from your Lord?’”

He belonged to Pharaoh’s household, yet he was a believer. Likewise, among these people there are believers. Hence He said:

“But most of them are defiantly disobedient.”

Before this, He had said:

“If the People of the Book had believed, it would have been better for them. Among them are believers, but most of them are defiantly disobedient.”

Then He said:

“They will cause you no more than annoyance.”

This refers to all of them, not merely to the majority of them. Hence He said:

“If they fight you, they will turn their backs and flee; then they will receive no help.”

They may fight while among them is a believer who conceals his faith, joins them on the battlefield, is unable to emigrate, is compelled to fight, and will be resurrected

---

## Page 9742

on the Day of Resurrection according to his intention, as related in an authentic hadith from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“An army will march against this House, and while they are in an open tract of land, the earth will swallow them up.” He was asked, “O Messenger of Allah, what about those among them who were compelled?” He replied, “They will be resurrected according to their intentions.”

This concerns his outward status. Even if he is killed and subjected to the rulings applicable to disbelievers, Allah will resurrect him according to his intention. Likewise, the hypocrites among us are outwardly subject to the rulings of Islam, yet they will be resurrected according to their intentions. Recompense on the Day of Resurrection is based on what is in people’s hearts, not merely on outward appearances. Hence it is narrated that al-‘Abbās said, “O Messenger of Allah, I was compelled.” He replied, “Outwardly, you were against us; as for what was in your heart, that is left to Allah.”

In short, there is no disagreement among Muslims that someone who has embraced faith while living in a land of disbelief and is unable to emigrate is not required to fulfil religious obligations beyond his ability. Rather, obligation is proportionate to ability. The same applies to matters whose rulings he does not know. If he did not know that prayer was obligatory for him and went for some time without praying, he is not required to make up those prayers according to the stronger of the scholars’ two opinions. This is the position of Abū Hanīfa and the Zāhirīs, and one of the two positions within Ahmad’s school. The same applies to all other obligations, such as fasting the month of Ramadan, paying zakat, and so forth. If he did not know that wine was forbidden and drank it, he is not subject to the prescribed punishment, by the consensus of the Muslims. Rather,

---

## Page 9743

they differed over making up the prayers.

Likewise, if someone engaged in transactions involving usury or gambling that he believed permissible, then learned of their prohibition after taking possession of the proceeds, should he annul the contract or not, just as we would not annul it if he had done this before embracing Islam?

Similarly, suppose he contracted a marriage that he believed valid according to their customs, but when the laws of Islam reached him, he realized that he had failed to fulfil some of its conditions—for example, he had married a woman during her waiting period, which has since expired. Is this marriage invalid, or is he allowed to remain in it, as he would if he had contracted it before embracing Islam and then become Muslim?

Underlying all of this is the question: Are revealed rulings binding upon those who do not know them, or do they become binding only after one learns of them? Or should a distinction be drawn between abrogating rulings and rulings introduced for the first time?

There are three opinions on this, corresponding to three positions within Ahmad’s school. Judge Abū Yaʿlā mentioned the two positions that make no such distinction in one of his books. He and others also mentioned, in their discussions of the principles of jurisprudence, the position that distinguishes between the two: abrogation does not take effect for a legally accountable person until the abrogating ruling reaches him. Abū al-Khattāb also derived a position holding that it does take effect before then.

Another instance is someone who omitted the required purification without knowing that it was obligatory, or prayed in a prohibited place before learning of the prohibition: must he repeat the prayer? There are two explicitly transmitted reports from Ahmad on this.

The correct position throughout this entire matter is that a ruling becomes binding only when one is able to learn of it, and that one need not make up what one did not know was obligatory. For it is established in an authentic report that

---

## Page 9744

some of the Companions ate after dawn in Ramadan until they could distinguish the white thread from the black thread, yet the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) did not command them to make up their fasts. Some of them would remain in a state of major ritual impurity for a time without praying, not knowing that they could pray after performing dry ablution. This happened to Abū Dharr, ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb, and ʿAmmār when in that state, yet the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) did not command any of them to make up the missed prayers. Undoubtedly, many Muslims in Mecca and the desert regions continued praying towards Jerusalem until news of the abrogation reached them, and they were not commanded to repeat their prayers. There are many such examples.

This accords with the principle upheld by the early generations and the majority of scholars: Allah does not burden any soul beyond its capacity. Obligation is therefore conditional upon ability, and punishment is incurred only for neglecting a duty or committing a forbidden act after the proof has been established.

May Allah bless Muhammad, his family, and his Companions, and grant them peace.

---

## Page 9745

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah sanctify his soul—also said:

Section

In common usage, the terms “religious sciences” and “rational sciences” may stand in a relationship of broader and narrower scope, or they may denote two parallel categories. In some contexts, the correct distinction is between sciences based on transmitted revelation and those based on reason.

This is because “religious sciences” may mean what the Lawgiver has commanded, what the Lawgiver has communicated, what He has prescribed should be known, or what He has taught.

The first is knowledge prescribed by the revealed law—just as we speak of action prescribed by the revealed law. This includes what is obligatory or recommended, and may also include what is permissible under the revealed law.

The second is knowledge derived from the Lawgiver: what the Messenger taught his community through the faith, the Quran, the Book, and wisdom with which he was sent. It is what is indicated by the Book, the Sunnah, consensus, or their derivative sources.

The first designation thus relates knowledge to its ruling under the revealed law, while the second relates it to

---

## Page 9746

the way it is acquired and the evidence on which it rests. Thus, in the first sense, we speak of “knowledge prescribed by the revealed law,” just as we speak of “action prescribed by the revealed law.” The second usage is analogous to speaking of “rational knowledge” and “knowledge based on transmitted revelation.” In the first case, knowledge is considered in terms of praise and blame, reward and punishment, and command and prohibition: this belongs to prescriptive discourse. In the second, it is considered in terms of how it is acquired, its evidence, its soundness or unsoundness, and its correspondence or lack of correspondence to reality: this belongs to informative discourse.

Each of these two categories is, in turn, divided into two. Once it is understood that what is termed religious may be either what the Lawgiver has communicated or what He has commanded, then, regarding what He has communicated, He may either explain a rational proof for it or mention none. What He has commanded may either be intended by the Lawgiver in its own right or be necessary for His intended purpose—that without which His obligatory or recommended purpose cannot be achieved. These, then, are four categories.

If you wish, you may also divide what is commanded into what is known through reason alone and what is also known through revelation; it is then religious in respect of both communication and command.

For “known through revelation” must mean either that the Lawgiver has communicated it or that He has pointed to evidence for it. When it means what the Lawgiver has pointed to—as when He points to the signs of divine lordship, the evidence for messengership, and the like—then something can be both religious and rational. For when the Lawgiver drew people’s minds to the signs, proofs, and lessons, their minds were guided and came to know what He had guided them to.

---

## Page 9747

Know that the Lawgiver has indicated the rational proofs for most of the major questions concerning the foundations of religion: acknowledging the Creator’s existence, His oneness, knowledge, power, will, and greatness; acknowledging reward and the messengership of Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace; and other matters knowable through reason.

These principles, which the speculative theologians call rational matters because they are known through reason, are also known through revelation. I do not mean merely through what the Lawgiver communicates, for that yields knowledge only after the truthfulness of the one communicating it has been established. Knowledge of these matters through this means therefore depends on the rationally established acknowledgment of divine lordship and messengership. Rather, I mean through His indications and guidance, just as students acquire knowledge through teachers’ explanations and authors’ writings because of the evidence they have made clear to their minds.

This is a point that must be carefully understood. Many who fall into error—whether speculative theologians, hadith scholars, jurists, laypeople, or others—suppose that knowledge derived from revelation comes solely from accepting what the Lawgiver communicates as true. That is not so. Rather, through its indications, its drawing attention to evidence, and its guidance, revelation provides all religious knowledge that can be acquired in this way.

The second category of what is known through revelation is that which is known through the Lawgiver’s communication. This must be either something that can also be known through reason or something that cannot. If it cannot, then it is known solely through the Lawgiver’s communication. If it can also be known through reason, does such a case actually exist? That is, could there be something which the Lawgiver has communicated and which can also be known through reason, yet for which He has not indicated any rational proof? This is possible, and there is no deficiency if something like

---

## Page 9748

this occurs within the revealed Law; for once someone knows that the one conveying it is truthful, he can learn through his reports everything he needs to know. Undoubtedly, many people acquire such knowledge only through what the Lawgiver communicates, and they have done well to believe him. But does such a case actually occur at all? Many philosophers, speculative theologians, jurists, Sufis, laypeople, and others have held that it does: among the matters communicated by the Lawgiver are things that can also be known through reason, even if the Lawgiver has not mentioned their rational proof. This calls for scrutiny.

For someone who reflects on the various ways in which the Quran and Sunnah provide evidence—whether clear or subtle, outward or inward—may say that, concerning everything knowable through reason, the Lawgiver has drawn attention to a rational proof, just as it is agreed that He has done so concerning the major questions of the foundations of religion. This too calls for scrutiny.

From this perspective, knowledge thus falls into the following categories:

It may be known through revelation alone: this is what is known solely through revelation’s communication, concerning matters to which reason can find no way whatsoever. Such matters may, however, be known through reports other than those of our Lawgiver Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

Or it may be known through reason alone, such as the transmitted teachings of medicine, arithmetic, and the crafts.

Or it may be known through both. In this case, either the Lawgiver has guided people to its proof as well as communicated it, or He has not. In the former case, these are the rational truths within revealed teachings, or the rational teaching of the

---

## Page 9749

Lawgiver; or that which revelation prescribes one to understand rationally; or reasoning sanctioned by revelation. Alternatively, the Lawgiver may merely have communicated these truths, in which case their rational understanding comes from sources other than the Lawgiver. This distinction must be carefully noted. A rational truth may, however, be understood through the Lawgiver—as is the case with most of the foundations of religion—or it may be understood through another source without being understood through him. Whether this latter case actually exists calls for scrutiny.

This clarification makes it evident that most philosophers and the majority of speculative theologians are ignorant of the full scope of revealed knowledge and of the Lawgiver’s guidance to it. This leads them to imagine that rational knowledge is superior to it. Their ignorance rests on two premises born of ignorance:

The first is that revealed knowledge consists of what the Lawgiver has communicated.

The second is that whatever is learned through his communication is secondary to rational truths, which constitute the foundations. From this follows their elevation of rational knowledge above revealed knowledge. Both premises are false.

For revealed knowledge includes both what the Lawgiver has communicated and what he has furnished evidence for. What the Lawgiver has furnished evidence for encompasses everything that needs to be known through reason, all proofs and demonstrations, the foundations of religion, and matters of belief. Indeed, I have reflected on most of what the philosophers and speculative theologians discuss, and on rational proofs, and have found that the proofs of the Quran and Sunnah convey their pure essence, free of impurities, and bring forth things

---

## Page 9750

to which they had not found their way, while omitting the specious arguments and falsehoods into which they have fallen, numerous and inconsistent as these are. I have explained this general point in detail elsewhere.

If, however, “revealed knowledge” means knowledge whose acquisition is prescribed by revelation, this includes all knowledge that is recommended or obligatory, and may also include permissible knowledge. On this understanding, the foundations of religion also belong to revealed knowledge. Even what is known through reason alone belongs to revealed knowledge if revelation commands that it be known.

Accordingly, revealed knowledge falls into two categories: rational and transmitted. Here, “transmitted” replaces “revealed” as used in the first classification. It is thus clear that any rational knowledge which revelation commands us to acquire, or for which it provides evidence, is also revealed knowledge—whether by virtue of that command, that evidence, or both.

This clarification shows that rational knowledge falling outside the category of revealed knowledge—that which the Lawgiver neither commands us to acquire nor provides evidence for—is on a par with crafts such as farming, building, and weaving. This applies only to branches of knowledge of lesser merit and inferior rank. It also becomes clear that the category of revealed knowledge is nobler and broader, and that rational and revealed knowledge overlap in scope: neither constitutes a mutually exclusive alternative to the other. Rather, transmitted knowledge is the counterpart of rational knowledge. A body of knowledge can thus be rational while also being revealed in all three respects: the Lawgiver has communicated it, commanded its acquisition, and provided evidence for it. Reflect, then, that attribution

---

## Page 9751

to revelation is understood in these three respects.

As for the knowledge the Lawgiver commands us to acquire, His command concerns either [what the Lawgiver directly intends] (1) or what is necessarily required, because what is prescribed cannot be accomplished without it.

Likewise, by a “ruling of the revealed Law,” the Muʿtazilah mean only what the Lawgiver has communicated, whereas the Ashʿarīs mean what the Lawgiver has established. Each group has been supported by some of our companions and others. The correct view is that a ruling of the revealed Law sometimes consists of what the Lawgiver has communicated, sometimes of what He has established, and sometimes combines both. And Allah knows best.

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) The text in square brackets is absent from the printed edition.
> Usāma ibn al-Zahrā’ — book coordinator for al-Mawsū‘a al-Shāmila.

---

## Page 9752

Shaykh al-Islām also said:

A Comprehensive and Beneficial Section

The terms to which Allah has attached rulings in the Quran and Sunnah include the following:

Some have definitions and meanings known through revealed Law, as Allah and His Messenger have explained them. Examples include prayer, zakat, fasting, Hajj, faith, Islam, disbelief, and hypocrisy.

Others are defined by the language itself, such as the sun and moon, heaven and earth, and land and sea.

Still others are defined by people’s customary practices and usage, and thus vary with their customs. Examples include sale, marriage, taking possession, the dirham, the dinar, and similar terms for which the Lawgiver has prescribed no definition, nor is there a single definition shared by all speakers of the language. Rather, their measure and characteristics differ according to people’s customs.

Allah and His Messenger have explained whatever belongs to the first category. As for the second and third categories, the Companions and Successors addressed by the Quran and Sunnah understood what was intended, because they knew what these terms denoted, whether delimited by the language or left to people’s customary usage

---

## Page 9753

and practice, without any definition prescribed by revealed Law or fixed by language. This is how a sound understanding of the Quran and Sunnah is attained.

When the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, defines what a term denotes, this does not necessarily mean that he has departed from its linguistic meaning or broadened it. Rather, the point is that his intended meaning is known through his own explanation, peace and blessings be upon him, whatever the case may be; this is what matters. An example is the term khamr (wine): he explained that every intoxicant is khamr, thereby making clear what is meant by it in the Quran. Whether the Arabs had previously applied the word khamr to every intoxicant or restricted it to grape juice need not be determined. What is required is to know what Allah and His Messenger intended by this term. This is known through the Messenger’s explanation, peace and blessings be upon him, and through the fact that, in the language of those addressed by the Quran, khamr included date wine and other varieties. In Medina, they had no wine other than these.

Since this is so, no one may restrict the scope of terms that Allah has left unrestricted and to which He has attached rulings—commands, prohibitions, and declarations of lawfulness or unlawfulness—except on the basis of evidence from Allah and His Messenger.

One example is the term water, which is unrestricted in the Quran and Sunnah. The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, did not divide it into two categories: water that purifies and water that does not. Such a division therefore contradicts the Quran and Sunnah. Allah simply said:

“and find no water”

We have discussed this at length elsewhere and explained that anything properly called water is pure and purifying, whether it has been used for obligatory or recommended purification

---

## Page 9754

or for purification that is not recommended; and whether or not an impurity has fallen into it, provided that any such impurity is known to have undergone transformation and been wholly absorbed into it. If, however, traces of the impurity remain apparent in the water, using it is forbidden, because that amounts to using something forbidden.

Section

Another example is the term menstruation. Allah has attached numerous rulings to it in the Quran and Sunnah, yet neither its minimum nor its maximum duration has been specified, nor the duration of the interval of purity between two menstrual periods, despite how commonly this affects the Muslim community and their need to know its rulings. Nor does the language distinguish between one duration and another. Whoever fixes a limit in this matter has therefore contradicted the Quran and Sunnah. Some scholars set both a maximum and a minimum duration, though they disagree over those limits.

Others set a maximum but no minimum. The third view is the most correct: there is neither a minimum nor a maximum duration. Rather, whatever bleeding a woman experiences as a consistent, recurring pattern is menstruation. Even if it lasts less than a day, if that remains her consistent pattern, it is menstruation. Likewise, if its maximum duration is seventeen days and that remains her consistent pattern, it is menstruation.

If, however, she bleeds continuously without interruption, this is known not to be menstruation. For both revealed Law and language establish that a woman is sometimes free of menstruation and sometimes menstruating, and that her state of purity has its rulings, while her menstruation has its rulings.

---

## Page 9755

The usual pattern is for a woman to menstruate for a quarter of the time—six or seven days. This is the pattern to which the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, directed a woman experiencing non-menstrual bleeding who had neither an established cycle nor a way to distinguish menstrual from non-menstrual blood.

The scholars agree that there is no maximum duration for the interval of purity between two menstrual periods, since some women do not menstruate at all. If a woman has lengthy intervals between her menstrual periods, does she calculate her waiting period by one-third of a menstrual period, or is she treated like a woman whose condition is uncertain, menstruating for a year? The jurists hold two views on this.

Likewise, according to the correct view, there is no minimum duration for the interval of purity. A woman may have three menstrual periods in a month; even three periods in less time than that are possible. However, if she claims that her waiting period has ended within a duration contrary to the usual known pattern, members of her family familiar with her condition must testify on her behalf. This accords with what is reported from ʿAlī, may Allah be pleased with him, concerning a woman who claimed to have had three menstrual periods in one month.

The basic presumption is that everything discharged from the womb is menstruation unless evidence establishes that it is non-menstrual bleeding. Menstrual blood is the normal, natural blood released by the womb, whereas abnormal bleeding comes from a ruptured vein. The latter is like illness, whereas the presumption is health, not illness. Thus, whenever a woman sees blood flowing from her womb, it is menstruation, on account of which she refrains from prayer.

The view that she should take a ritual bath after one day and one night contradicts what is known from the Sunnah and the consensus of the early generations. We know that women menstruated during the Prophet’s lifetime, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and every woman initially experiences menstruation for the first time. Yet despite this, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did not instruct

---

## Page 9756

any of them to take a ritual bath after one day and one night. Had such an instruction been transmitted, it would have established a minimum duration for menstruation. Yet the hadith scholars agree that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, set no minimum duration for it. The reports transmitted on this matter specify three days, but the scholars versed in his hadith agree that these reports are falsely attributed to him. This is the view of the vast majority of scholars and one of the two positions within Ahmad’s school.

Likewise, if a woman’s established menstrual pattern changes—whether her period becomes longer or shorter, or shifts in timing—the bleeding is menstruation until its persistence establishes that it is non-menstrual bleeding. Her case is like that of a woman menstruating for the first time.

A woman experiencing non-menstrual bleeding should refer first to her established cycle, then to her ability to distinguish between the types of blood, and then to the usual menstrual pattern among women. A Sunnah ruling has been transmitted from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, for each of these three cases. Imam Ahmad adopted all three Sunnah rulings. Some scholars adopted two of the hadiths, while others adopted only one, according to what had reached them and the conclusions of their independent reasoning. May Allah be pleased with them all.

If a pregnant woman sees blood following her familiar pattern, it is menstrual blood, in accordance with the basic presumption.

Postpartum bleeding has neither a minimum nor a maximum duration. Thus, if a woman were to see

---

## Page 9757

blood for more than forty, sixty, or seventy days and it then stopped, it would be postpartum bleeding. If, however, it continued without interruption, it would be abnormal bleeding. In that case, the limit is forty days, since this is the upper end of the usual duration, as indicated by the transmitted reports. Nor is there an age limit beyond which a woman cannot menstruate. If, after sixty or seventy years of age, her familiar uterine bleeding increased, it would be menstruation.

The loss of expectation mentioned in His words,

“And those who no longer expect menstruation,”

does not mean reaching a particular age. [For] (1) if it meant reaching a particular age, Allah and His Messenger would have specified it. Rather, it means that the woman herself no longer expects to menstruate. If her bleeding stops and she no longer expects it to return, she has ceased to expect menstruation, even if she is forty years old. If she then waits and the bleeding returns, it becomes clear that she had not ceased menstruating permanently. If it returns after the three months, her case is like that of other women whose menstruation returns after they had ceased to expect it, or whose menstrual status is uncertain.

Anyone who does not understand this cessation of expectation in this way adopts an inconsistent position if he defines it by age. His position is likewise inconsistent if he defines it neither by age nor by the woman’s loss of hope that she will menstruate. Nor can it be known simply from the person herself.

Since postpartum bleeding has no fixed duration, whether a woman gives birth to twins or more, she remains in the state of postpartum bleeding as long as she continues to see blood. Whatever blood she sees from the onset of labour is postpartum bleeding. The rulings governing postpartum bleeding are the same as those governing menstrual bleeding.

Anyone who does not adopt this view, but instead sets the minimum duration of menstruation at one day, one day and one night, or three days, has no reliable basis for doing so. For the reports on this matter transmitted from

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) Not found in the printed edition.
> Usāma ibn al-Zahrā’ — book formatter for the Comprehensive Encyclopedia.

---

## Page 9758

the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) and his Companions are deemed invalid by hadith specialists. Nor do actual occurrences conform to a fixed rule. If someone knows only of menstruation lasting three days, another says that he knows of it lasting a day and a night. If someone knows only of it lasting a day and a night, another knows of it lasting a day. We cannot deny what we do not know. If we make what we know the limit prescribed by religious law, saying, “There is no menstruation lasting less than three days, a day and a night, or a day,” merely because that is all we know, we would be devising religious law ourselves on the basis of that knowledge. For lack of knowledge is not knowledge of nonexistence.

If this were truly a limit prescribed by religious law, the Messenger (peace and blessings be upon him) would have been better placed than us to know and explain it, just as he specified for the community the limits Allah had prescribed for them: the times of prayer, Hajj, and fasting; the designated places for Hajj; the minimum thresholds and prescribed dues of zakat; and the number of prayers and their bowings and prostrations. Had Allah and His Messenger prescribed a limit for menstruation, or for anything else whose limits the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) did not specify, the Messenger (peace and blessings be upon him) would have explained it. His not specifying a limit therefore indicates that he left the matter to what women recognize and what is called menstruation in ordinary language.

For this reason, when asked about menstruation, many of the early generations would say, “Ask the women, for they know more about it.” That is, they know what does and does not occur in menstruation. The religious ruling is attached to the term that denotes the actual occurrence. Thus, any bleeding that occurs is menstruation unless it is known to be blood from a vein or a wound. For the blood that emerges is either discharged by the womb, or bursts from one of the veins, or comes from the woman’s skin or

---

## Page 9759

flesh, from which it flows. This too comes from small veins, but blood from a small wound does not flow continuously like blood from a large vein. Hence the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) said to the woman experiencing non-menstrual bleeding:

“This is blood from a vein, not menstruation.”

A wound bleeds only when a vein opens, as we mentioned in discussing bloodletting, for blood is present in both small and large veins.

Section

The Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) commanded his community to wipe over leather socks. Safwān ibn ‘Assāl said:

“The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) commanded us, when we were on a journey—or travelling—not to remove our leather socks for three days and nights, except because of major ritual impurity, but not because of defecation, urination or sleep.”

He did not qualify this according to whether the leather sock stays in place on its own or not, or whether it is free of holes and splits or not. Whatever is called a leather sock and is worn and walked in may be wiped over in the manner permitted by Allah and His Messenger. Anything equivalent to it may likewise be wiped over. Being called a leather sock is not itself a determining factor; rather, the ruling concerns what is worn and walked in. Hence wiping over socks is mentioned in the hadith.

---

## Page 9760

Section

Allah and His Messenger made shortening the prayers and breaking the fast conditional upon what is called travel, without specifying a distance or distinguishing between long and short journeys. Had travel been defined by a particular distance, Allah and His Messenger would have explained it. Nor does the language assign it a fixed distance. Thus, whatever speakers of the language call travel permits shortening the prayers and breaking the fast, as indicated by the Quran and Sunnah. The people of Mecca shortened their prayers with the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) on their journey to ‘Arafāt, which is one barīd (a postal stage) from Mecca. This shows that setting a limit of one, two or three days is not a universally applicable limit prescribed by Islamic law.

What has been reported from the Companions concerning this may relate to particular circumstances in which travel would necessarily have been of that extent. This explains why differing reports have been transmitted from individual Companions, such as Ibn ‘Umar, Ibn ‘Abbās and others. It is therefore clear that they did not establish a universally applicable legal limit for the traveller or the duration, comparable to the prescribed times for fasting and prayer. Rather, they specified it for particular people according to what they considered travel for someone in those circumstances—just as one defines deviation on the part of the rich and the poor in particular cases according to one’s judgment.

This is not because Islamic law has assigned a single amount of wealth by which everyone alike is classified as rich or poor. Rather, a little may suffice for one man, while many times that amount may not suffice for another because of his many dependants and needs; and the reverse may also be true.

Some people may cover a great distance without being travellers, such as a courier

---

## Page 9761

when he leaves the town to deliver a message or fetch something he needs, then turns straight back without stopping to stay. Such a person is not called a traveller, unlike someone who takes a traveller’s provisions and spends the night there, for he is called a traveller. Someone else may cover that same distance and be a traveller, needing to take provisions for it and spend the night in that village, returning only after a day or two. People call this person a traveller, whereas they do not call the one who went straight there and immediately retraced his steps a traveller, although the distance is the same.

Travel, then, is a particular form of going from place to place; it is not defined by distance or duration. The Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) would go to Qubā’ every Saturday, sometimes riding and sometimes walking, without being a traveller. People would come to Friday prayer from al-‘Awālī and al-‘Aqīq, then be back with their families by nightfall, without being travellers. When the people of Mecca went out to Minā and ‘Arafah, however, they were travellers: they took provisions, spent the night outside the town, and made the preparations appropriate to travel. This differs from someone who goes out for Friday prayer or some other need and returns that same day: even if he covers a barīd, he may not be called a traveller.

People have always gone out from their homes to the orchards surrounding their town, each tending his orchard by planting, watering and doing other work, just as the Ansār used to work in their orchards. They are not called travellers, even if one of them stays all day, or spends the night in his orchard and remains there for several days, and even if the orchard is more than a barīd away. For they regard the orchard as part of the town’s outlying dependencies, and going out

---

## Page 9762

to it is like going out to another part of the town. In a large town extending more than a barīd, someone going from one end to the other is not a traveller. People distinguish between someone moving about among dwellings and their dependencies and a traveller departing from all of them. Thus, the inhabitants of the Prophet’s city (peace and blessings be upon him) would go to their orchards without being travellers. Medina had no wall; rather, it consisted of separate tribal quarters and clusters of houses, with a considerable distance between its two sides. Someone going from one tribal quarter to another was not a traveller, even if each tribe had its orchards and fields around it, for the name Medina encompassed all of this.

Hence Allah Most High says:

“Among the Bedouins around you are hypocrites, and among the people of Medina are those entrenched in hypocrisy.”

People are thus divided into two groups: desert dwellers, who are the Bedouins, and the people of Medina. All those living in mud-brick dwellings were therefore people of Medina. This includes Qubā’ and other places, showing that the name Medina encompassed all of them. For it did not have a wall as it does today, with gates that are opened and closed. It merely had approaches. Even if those approaches lay inward of Qubā’ and other places, the name Medina could encompass the town’s settled area.

This is familiar in every city. Someone says, “I went to Damascus, Misr, Baghdad, or elsewhere; I lived there and stayed there for a time,” or something similar, although he was living outside the walls. The city’s name encompasses all those dwellings, even though the interior

---

## Page 9763

within the walls is more specifically denoted by the name than the area outside. Likewise, the city of the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) had an inner and an outer area, separated by its approaches, and the name Medina encompasses all of this in the Book of Allah Most High. Thus, all these inhabitants used to offer the Friday prayer and the two Eid prayers behind the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) and his successors. No separate Friday or Eid prayers were held in Qubā’ or elsewhere, whereas the five daily prayers were offered in every tribal quarter. The Prophet’s statement (peace and blessings be upon him), “There are indeed men in Medina,” is another example of this: it encompasses all the dwellings.

Likewise, the term qurā (towns) includes cities, as in His words:

“How many towns We have destroyed!”

And His words:

“So that you may warn the Mother of Towns and those around it.”

And His words:

“Your Lord would never destroy towns until He had sent a messenger to their principal town, reciting Our revelations to them. Nor would We ever destroy towns unless their people were wrongdoers.”

And His words:

“These are some of the accounts of the towns which We relate to you; some are still standing, while others have been mown down.”

This encompasses dwellings both within and outside the town, even if a wall or something similar separates them. For the sending of messengers, destruction, and the like did not apply to some inhabitants to the exclusion of others. Most cities have both an inner and an outer area.

---

## Page 9764

The word “Kaaba” originally names the structure itself, but in the Quran it is also used to include the surrounding area, as in His words:

“An offering to be brought to the Kaaba.”

Likewise, “the Sacred Mosque” denotes both the mosque and the surrounding sanctuary. Similarly, Badr is the name of a well, but the surrounding area is also called by that name. Uhud, too, is the name of the mountain, yet it includes the surrounding area. Thus, people say, “The battle took place at Uhud,” although it actually took place below the mountain.

The same applies to places named after a mountain pass or a palace. Al-‘Uqayba is the diminutive of al-‘Aqaba (the mountain pass), and al-Qusayr is the diminutive of qasr (palace). There may originally have been a small palace or a small pass there, after which the name came to encompass the surrounding area, however extensive. This is very common in place names.

The point is that someone moving about among the dwellings is not called a traveler. If people customarily spend the night in their orchards and have dwellings there, going out to them is like going to another part of their residential area. A traveler is therefore not a traveler until he emerges, becoming exposed and visible in the open country beyond the dwellings, where the wayfarer does not travel but ordinarily becomes visible and exposed. The point is that what constitutes travel is determined by the meaning of the word in language and customary usage.

---

## Page 9765

Section

Likewise, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“No zakat is due on less than five wasqs, less than five ūqiyyas, or fewer than five camels.”

He also said:

“No zakat is due on silver until it reaches two hundred dirhams.”

Regarding the thief, he said:

“His hand is to be cut off if he steals something worth the price of a shield.”

He also said:

“The hand is to be cut off for stealing a quarter of a dinar.”

In his usage, an ūqiyya was forty dirhams. Yet he specified no fixed measure for either the dirham or the dinar. He did not mint any dirhams himself, nor were dirhams minted in his land; rather, they were imported, already minted by unbelievers.

These included both large and small coins. People sometimes transacted with them by number and sometimes by weight, as he said:

“Weigh it out and give extra, for the best people are those who repay their debts most generously.”

There was a man there who weighed coins for a fee. Clearly, when they weighed them, they needed a standard weight by which to determine the weight of the dirhams. But the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, neither fixed nor specified this standard. It has been related that dirhams were of three kinds: eight dāniqs, six, and four. Perhaps the seller would specify one of these kinds, and the buyer would pay him according to its weight.

Nevertheless, he used the terms dinar and dirham without qualification and prescribed no fixed measure for them. This indicates that the terms encompass all these kinds, and that whoever possesses some of

---

## Page 9766

the small dirhams amounting to five ūqiyyas—two hundred dirhams—must pay zakat. The same applies to medium-sized and large dirhams. Accordingly, people follow their customary practices regarding the weights of dirhams and dinars: whatever they agree upon and designate as a dirham is a dirham, and whatever they designate as a dinar is a dinar. The Lawgiver’s words encompass what they customarily use, whether small or large. If their customary dirhams are large and they know no others, zakat is not due from a person until he possesses two hundred of them. If they are small and they know no others, zakat becomes due when he possesses two hundred of them. If they use a mixture, zakat becomes due when he possesses that number in total. This applies whether the coins are of a single issue or different issues, and whether they are pure or debased, so long as they are called dirhams without qualification. This is the view of several scholars.

If, however, a coin is called a dirham only with a qualification—for example, if it consists mostly of copper and is called a “black dirham,” falling outside the unqualified term “dirham”—then this requires further consideration. Accordingly, the correct view is that zakat is obligatory on two hundred debased dirhams, as held by Abū Hanīfa and in one of the two positions within Ahmad’s school. If a thief steals three dirhams, whether large, small, or a mixture of both, his hand is to be cut off.

As for the wasq, it was known among them to equal sixty sāʿs, and the sāʿ

---

## Page 9767

was known to them: it was a single measure of uniform capacity, which they made themselves rather than importing it. Thus, when the Lawgiver made the obligation conditional upon possessing five wasqs, He tied it to a fixed quantity that was the same for everyone. This was unlike the five ūqiyyas, which did not constitute a fixed quantity common to everyone; rather, their customary amount was greater among some people than among others. This resembles the terms “mosque,” “house,” “residence,” “city,” and “village”: their customary sizes vary from one people to another, yet the Lawgiver’s wording encompasses them all.

If someone were to say that the sāʿ and the mudd should be determined by people’s customary usage, arguing that ʿUmar’s sāʿ was larger and that he used it to collect the land tax—being eight ratls, as the scholars of Iraq maintain—this could be conceivable where the inhabitants of a locality have two measures, one large and one small. The charity due at the end of Ramadan would then be measured by the larger one, and a wasq would equal sixty of those larger measures. For the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, specified both the zakat threshold for produce measured in wasqs and the amount of that charity in terms of the sāʿ; he did not specify any zakat threshold or obligatory amount in terms of the mudd.

However, I know of no one who held this view. Nor may we advance any position other than what our predecessors stated, for they certainly knew what the Messenger intended. If any of the Companions or Successors held that the sāʿ was not fixed by religious law, then this would become a matter of scholarly judgment. As for the dirham and the dinar, you have already learned of people’s disagreement concerning them and the inconsistency of

---

## Page 9768

most of them, since they relied on no evidence from religious law. Rather, they took the weight intended by the Messenger to be that of the dirhams minted by ʿAbd al-Malik, because he combined the large, small, and medium dirhams and set their average weight at six dāniqs.

They may therefore be told: Granted that this was so, when the Messenger, peace and blessings be upon him, addressed his Companions and his community using the terms “dirham” and “dinar,” while they had coins of differing weights, as you have mentioned, he did not define the dirham for them by an average weight as ʿAbd al-Malik did. Rather, he used the terms “dirham” and “dinar” without qualification, just as he used the terms “shirt,” “trousers,” “waist-wrapper,” “cloak,” “residence,” “village,” “city,” “house,” and other things made by human beings. Had he intended a fixed measure for what these terms designated, he would have specified it, knowing that their sizes varied. People’s agreement upon a particular weight for the dirham and dinar is therefore a matter of custom.

The cubit is more closely connected to the natural constitution of created beings than these are. Originally, a cubit is a human forearm, and a human being is created. One forearm exceeds another in length only by a difference determined at creation, over which people have no choice. This differs from things people make by choice, such as dirhams, cities, and residences, which have no fixed measure. Rather, clothing follows people’s bodily dimensions, while residences and cities are proportioned to their needs.

As for the dirham and dinar, no fixed measure is known for them, whether by nature or religious law. Rather, their measure is determined by custom and convention. This is because, fundamentally, they are not themselves the object sought; their purpose is to serve as a standard for what people exchange. Dirhams and dinars are not sought for their own sake; rather, they are a means of conducting transactions

---

## Page 9769

with them. This is why they serve as money, unlike other forms of wealth, which are intended to be used for their own sake. Hence, the latter are measured according to natural standards or those prescribed by religious law. A mere means, whose substance and form are not themselves sought, achieves its purpose whatever form it takes.

Moreover, the prescribed quantity was five wasqs, that is, five loads. If no uniform standard were recognized here, one would have to reckon five loads according to the loads customary among each people.

Furthermore, people do not all use the name “sāʿ” for their measures, so the Lawgiver’s wording does not encompass them as it encompasses the dirham and dinar—unless, of course, it is argued that sāʿ is a name for anything used to measure capacity, on the evidence of His words:

“the king’s measuring cup.”

In that case, it would be like the term “dirham.”

Section

Likewise, religious law has not specified a fixed quantity for feeding ten needy people. Rather, it is as Allah says:

“from the average food you feed your families.”

In every locality, people are to provide others with enough of the average food they themselves eat, as we have explained at length elsewhere.

---

## Page 9770

The same applies to the terms “jizyah” (poll tax) and “diyah” (blood money). The former follows the pattern fi‘lah, from jazā–yajzī, meaning to discharge or pay an obligation. This is the sense of the Prophet’s words, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“It suffices for you, but it will not suffice for anyone after you.”

The underlying construction is jazā, jizyah (“he paid; payment”), just as one says wa‘ada, ‘idah (“he promised; a promise”), and wazana, zinah (“he weighed; weight”). Likewise, diyah derives from wadā–yadī–diyah (“to pay blood money; blood money”), just as one says wa‘ada–ya‘idu–‘idah (“to promise; a promise”).

A verbal noun is often used to name the object of its action. Thus, what is paid is called diyah, and what is discharged and settled is called jizyah, just as what is promised is called a “promise” in His words:

“They say, ‘When will this promise come to pass, if you are truthful?’ Say, ‘Knowledge is with Allah alone, and I am only a clear warner.’ Then, when they see it close at hand…”

What they see is the punishment they were promised. Similarly, such a payment is called itāwah (tribute) because it is given. The same applies to darībah (a levy), which denotes what is imposed upon people. None of these expressions has a fixed limit inherent in its linguistic meaning; rather, reference is made to people’s customs. If, however, religious law has fixed a limit for any of them, that limit must be observed.

For this reason, the jurists differed over jizyah: is its amount fixed by religious law, or is it left to the independent judgment of the rulers? The same applies to kharāj (land tax). The correct view is that their amounts are not fixed by religious law. The Prophet’s instruction to Mu‘ādh, may Allah bless the Prophet and grant him peace,

“to take from every male who had reached puberty one dinar or its equivalent in Ma‘āfirī cloth,”

was a ruling for a particular case. He did not make it a general rule applying until the Day of Resurrection to everyone from whom jizyah is collected. This is shown by the fact that he concluded an agreement with the people of Bahrain on the basis of

---

## Page 9771

a levy on each male who had reached puberty, without fixing it at that amount; this, too, was jizyah. Likewise, he reached an agreement with the people of Najrān for payments different from these and not fixed at that amount. This shows that the matter rests on what the ruler considers beneficial and what those entering into the treaty agree to. It then becomes an obligation upon them which they discharge—that is, they undertake and pay it.

As for diyah, in cases of intentional killing it depends on the agreement of the two parties. In cases of accidental killing, however, religious law makes it a definite obligation, so it cannot depend on their mutual agreement. Rather, it may be said that religious law has fixed it generally for the entire Muslim community, just as it has prescribed prayer and zakah. It may also vary according to people’s differing views concerning its kind and amount. This is the more plausible of the two views, and the transmitted reports support it: the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, fixed it at one hundred camels only for people whose wealth consisted of camels. Accordingly, he prescribed gold for those whose wealth was in gold, silver for those whose wealth was in silver, sheep for those whose wealth was in sheep, and garments for those whose wealth was in garments. This was also the practice of ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb and others.

Section

Allah Most High says:

“And those who guard their private parts, except with their wives or those their right hands possess.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Guard your private parts except from your wife or those your right hand possesses.”

The Quran also indicates that anyone with whom intercourse is forbidden through marriage

---

## Page 9772

is likewise forbidden through slave ownership. Thus, a man may not take as concubines women whom he is permanently forbidden to marry. Nor may he have intercourse with a concubine while in the pilgrim state of consecration, while fasting, during her menstruation, or in any other circumstances in which intercourse with a wife is prohibited; the prohibition applies all the more to a concubine.

As for the waiting period to establish that the womb is free of pregnancy, the Sunnah has not prescribed it unconditionally for every female slave. Rather, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade a man to water another man’s crop with his own fluid.

He also said concerning the female captives of Awtās:

“No pregnant woman is to be approached sexually until she gives birth, nor a woman who is not pregnant until she has completed the waiting period to establish that her womb is free of pregnancy.”

This concerned slaves taken as captives. He did not say the same concerning women acquired by inheritance, purchase, or other means. The requirement, then, is that if a female slave has been having intercourse, intercourse with her is forbidden until she has completed that waiting period, lest a man water another man’s crop with his own fluid.

But if it is known that her owner was not having intercourse with her—because she is a virgin, because the owner is a woman or a child, or because he truthfully states, “I was not having intercourse with her”—then neither a revealed text nor analogy provides any basis for prohibiting intercourse with her until she has completed that waiting period.

Section

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, ruled that blood money was payable by the ʿāqilah: those who support a man and come to his aid. In his time, a man’s ʿāqilah consisted of his agnatic kin. When ʿUmar’s time came, he placed this liability upon those enrolled in the dīwān (state register). Hence the jurists differed concerning it.

---

## Page 9773

and it may be said:

The fundamental question is whether the membership of the ʿāqilah is fixed by the revealed law, or whether it comprises whoever supports a person and comes to his aid, without specifying particular people. Those who adopt the first view restrict it to relatives, for they constituted the ʿāqilah in the Prophet’s time. Those who adopt the second regard a man’s ʿāqilah, in every time and place, as those who support him and come to his aid in that time and place. In the time of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, it was a man’s relatives who supported him and came to his aid, so they constituted his ʿāqilah. There was then neither a state register nor stipends. When ʿUmar established the register, it was well known that the soldiers of each city supported and assisted one another, even if they were unrelated, so they constituted the ʿāqilah. This is the sounder of the two views: its composition varies according to circumstances.

Otherwise, consider a man who has settled in the West, where there are people who support him and come to his aid. How could his ʿāqilah consist of people in the East, in another kingdom, who may no longer even receive news of him? An inheritance, however, can be preserved for an absent heir. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace,

“ruled concerning a woman who had killed someone that the blood money payable on her behalf was the responsibility of her agnatic kin, while her inheritance belonged to her husband and sons.”

Thus, the heirs are distinct from the ʿāqilah. The same applies to allowing a three-year term for payment of the blood money. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did not defer its payment, but ruled that it was payable immediately, whereas ʿUmar allowed three years for its payment. Many jurists therefore say that payment must be deferred, as ʿUmar ruled; some even consider this a matter of consensus. Others say that it must be paid immediately.

The correct view is that immediate or deferred payment depends on the circumstances and the interests served. If those liable are well-to-do and immediate payment would cause them no harm, it is collected

---

## Page 9774

immediately; if doing so would cause hardship, payment is deferred.

This is Ahmad’s explicit position: deferral is not obligatory, contrary to the view of many of his followers, who deemed it obligatory, agreeing with those among the followers of Abū Hanīfah, al-Shāfiʿī, Mālik and others who held that view. This opinion is exceedingly weak. It resembles the view that the Muslim community may abrogate the law of its Prophet, as some people maintain when they say that consensus can be abrogated. This is among the views Ahmad most strongly rejected. An established Sunnah cannot be set aside except by another established Sunnah. Nor can a consensus contrary to a Sunnah arise unless that consensus is accompanied by a known Sunnah which we know abrogates the earlier one.

Section

Allah Most High says in the verse concerning the one-fifth share of war spoils:

“One-fifth of it belongs to Allah, the Messenger, near relatives, orphans and the needy.”

The same appears in the verse concerning spoils acquired without fighting. In the verse concerning zakat, He says:

“For the poor, the needy, and those employed to administer it,”

and so on to the end of the verse. Allah thus mentions the categories without further restriction. Nothing in the wording indicates that they must receive equal shares; rather, it indicates the opposite. Whoever maintains that the wording requires equal distribution has therefore said something contrary to the Book and the Sunnah. Do you not see that, when Allah said:

“But the truly righteous are those who believe in Allah, the Last Day, the angels, the Scripture and the prophets, and give wealth, despite their love for it, to relatives, orphans and the needy

---

## Page 9775

and the wayfarer.” He Most High also says:

“Give near relatives their due, as well as the needy and the wayfarer.”

He Most High says:

“If relatives, orphans and the needy are present at the division, give them something from it.”

He Most High says:

“And those in whose wealth there is a known right for the beggar and the deprived.”

He Most High says:

“So eat of them and feed those who are content and those who come seeking help.”

Equal distribution was not obligatory in any of these or similar cases? Indeed, in most of these cases it is not even recommended, whether giving itself is obligatory or recommended. Rather, distribution should serve the interests at hand.

When we say concerning pilgrimage offerings and Eid sacrifices that it is recommended to eat a third and give a third in charity, this applies only when there is no reason to favour one allocation over another. Otherwise, if the poor were numerous, we would recommend giving more than a third in charity. The same principle applies if those to whom one gives gifts outnumber the poor, and likewise to the portion one eats.

Where receipt is based on need or benefit, consideration must be given to need and benefit according to the actual circumstances. Inheritance is different: its shares are apportioned according to relationships of kinship that apply equally to those who possess them. The term “son” includes the old and the young, the strong and the weak. A son receives neither because of his need nor because of his benefit, but solely because of his kinship. For this reason, those within the same category receive equal shares.

In these other cases, however, receipt is based on need and benefit. Equal distribution among the categories therefore cannot be either obligatory or recommended. Rather, giving must be proportionate to need and benefit, just as the underlying entitlement depends on them. The conjunction “and” entails

---

## Page 9776

that both terms it joins share the stated ruling. What is stated is that none but these are entitled to receive charity; thus, they share in its being lawful for them. Their sharing in the stated ruling—namely, its permissibility in principle—does not mean that they receive equal shares, for the wording in no way indicates this.

The same applies to the wording of someone establishing an endowment or making a bequest. Someone once established an endowment for the instructor, the assistant instructor, the caretaker, the jurists and the students of jurisprudence. When its distribution was discussed, we said: “It should be distributed according to the interests served.” The instructor claimed a fifth on the basis of this assumption. He was told: “Then give the caretaker a fifth as well, since he is on the same footing as the instructor.” Thus, the invalidity of his argument became apparent.

Here it ends. Praise be to Allah, Lord of all worlds.

---

## Page 9777

And Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—said:

Section

Allah Most High has condemned in the Quran those who turn away from following the messengers to follow the religion of their forefathers in which they were raised. This is the imitation that Allah and His Messenger have forbidden: following someone other than the Messenger in matters in which that person opposes the Messenger. By the consensus of the Muslims, this is forbidden for everyone, for no created being may be obeyed in disobedience to the Creator. Obeying the Messenger is obligatory upon everyone, elite and common people alike, at every time and in every place, privately and publicly, and in all circumstances.

This is part of faith. Allah Most High says:

“But no, by your Lord, they will not believe until they make you judge concerning that over which they dispute among themselves, then find within themselves no discomfort over what you have decided, and submit fully.”

He also says:

“The only response of the believers, when they are called to Allah and His Messenger for him to judge between them, is to say, ‘We hear and we obey.’”

He also says:

“It is not for a believing man or a believing woman, when Allah and His Messenger have decided a matter, to have any choice in their affair.”

He also says:

“So let those who oppose his command beware lest a trial befall them

---

## Page 9778

or a painful punishment afflict them.” He also says:

“Say, ‘If you love Allah, then follow me; Allah will love you.’”

Allah has made obedience to the Messenger obligatory upon everyone in nearly forty places in the Quran. Obedience to him is obedience to Allah: it is worshipping Allah alone, without partner. That is Allah’s religion—Islam. Whoever Allah has commanded people to obey—whether a scholar, ruler, parent or husband—is to be obeyed because obeying him constitutes obedience to Allah. If, however, he commands something contrary to obedience to Allah, he must not be obeyed.

A parent or husband may command something permissible and thus be obeyed. The same applies to a ruler when he gives an order to a scholar who knows that it constitutes disobedience to Allah, and to a scholar when he gives someone a ruling that the questioner does not know to be contrary to Allah’s command: the person obeying them is not disobeying Allah. But if he knows that it is contrary to Allah’s command, then obeying them in that matter constitutes disobedience to Allah.

For this reason, several scholars have reported a consensus that a scholar may not follow another scholar’s opinion once he has exercised independent judgment, examined the evidence, and clearly recognized the truth brought by the Messenger. In that case, he may not follow someone whose view contradicts it; there is no disagreement about this. But may he follow another scholar’s opinion when he is himself capable of examining the evidence?

There are two views on this. The position of al-Shāfi‘ī, Ahmad and others is that he may not. Permission to do so has been attributed to Muhammad ibn al-Hasan. This is a well-known issue, and some have also attributed this view to Ahmad.

---

## Page 9779

but the person who reported this did not understand Ahmad’s position, as explained elsewhere. According to the majority of scholars, someone unable to examine the evidence may follow a scholar’s opinion. The qualifications of those permitted to do so involve distinctions and disagreements that are beyond the scope of this discussion.

The point here is that the following of others forbidden by the revealed texts and scholarly consensus consists in setting a contrary opinion against the word of Allah and His Messenger, whoever holds that opinion. Allah Most High says:

“On the Day the wrongdoer bites his hands, saying, ‘If only I had taken a path with the Messenger!’”

“‘Woe to me! If only I had not taken so-and-so as a close friend!’”

“‘He led me away from the Reminder after it had come to me.’ Satan has always been a betrayer of mankind.”

“And the Messenger will say, ‘My Lord, my people have abandoned this Quran.’”

He Most High also says:

“On the Day their faces are turned about in the Fire, they will say, ‘If only we had obeyed Allah and obeyed the Messenger!’”

And the passage continues as far as His words:

“and curse them with a great curse.”

He Most High also says:

“When those who were followed disown those who followed them, and they see the punishment, and all ties between them are severed.”

And the passage continues as far as His words:

“The likeness of those who disbelieve is that of one who calls out to what hears nothing but cries and shouts. Deaf, dumb and blind, they do not understand.”

Thus, He mentions how those who were followed in disobedience to Allah will disown their followers. He mentions this after saying:

“Your God is one God.”

The one God is the One who is worshipped and obeyed. Whoever obeys

---

## Page 9780

someone he follows in contravention of this has a share in this condemnation. Allah Most High says:

“We have enjoined upon man care for his parents. His mother carried him in weakness upon weakness.”

And the passage continues as far as His words:

“But if they strive to make you associate with Me that of which you have no knowledge, do not obey them. Yet keep their company courteously in this world, and follow the way of those who turn to Me.”

Then He addresses mankind, telling them to eat what is lawful and good on earth and not to follow Satan’s footsteps in contravention of this. Satan commands only evil and indecency, and that they say about Allah what they do not know, declaring, “This is forbidden and that is lawful,” or making other statements about Allah without knowledge, concerning matters of fact and conduct. As Allah Most High says:

“Do not falsely declare with your tongues, ‘This is lawful and that is forbidden.’”

Then, when these people who speak about Allah without knowledge are told:

“Follow what Allah has revealed,” they say, “Rather, we follow what we found our fathers following.”

They have no knowledge; they merely follow their predecessors, adhering to what they have grown accustomed to and been brought up upon.

Then He addresses the believers specifically, saying:

“O you who believe, eat of the good things We have provided for you, and give thanks to Allah, if it is Him that you worship.”

“He has forbidden you only carrion, blood, the flesh of swine, and that over which a name other than Allah’s has been invoked.”

Thus, He commands them to eat the good things He has provided for them, because they are the intended recipients of His provision. Here He does not stipulate that it be lawful

---

## Page 9781

because He has forbidden only what He mentioned, so everything else is lawful for them. As for mankind generally, He has commanded them to eat what is lawful and good on earth. Yet He has made things lawful only for believers; He has made nothing lawful for disbelievers. Lawfulness is therefore conditional upon faith. Whoever does not use His provision to help him worship Him has had nothing made lawful for him, even though He has not forbidden it either. Thus, it cannot be said that Allah has either made it lawful for them or forbidden it. He has only forbidden the Jews what He mentioned in Surah al-An‘ām.

For this reason, in Surah al-An‘ām and elsewhere, He condemns those who forbid what He has not forbidden, as when He says:

“Say, ‘Has He forbidden the two males or the two females?’”

Then He says:

“And to the Jews We forbade every animal with claws.”

Then He, Most High, says:

“Say, ‘Come, I will recite what your Lord has forbidden to you,’”

and the verses that follow.

In Surah al-Nahl, He says:

“And to the Jews We forbade what We related to you before,”

and the rest of the verse. He states that He forbade these things because of their transgression, saying:

“For the wrongdoing of the Jews, We forbade them good things which had been lawful for them.”

He also says:

“Thus We recompensed them for their transgression.”

All of this supports the more correct of the scholars’ two views: this prohibition remains binding upon them after Muhammad was sent, and is lifted only by following him. It is a punitive prohibition imposed for their wrongdoing and transgression. Their wrongdoing and transgression have not ceased; rather, they have increased and become more grievous, making them still more deserving of punishment.

Moreover, Allah Most High spoke of this prohibition after Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was sent, to make clear that He had forbidden only the things He specified. Had that prohibition been lifted, He would not have mentioned it as an exception.

---

## Page 9782

Moreover, a prohibition can be lifted only by His making the thing lawful, and He has made the eating of good things lawful only for believers, saying:

“There is no blame upon those who believe and do righteous deeds for what they have eaten,”

and the rest of the verse. He also says:

“Livestock have been made lawful for you, except what is recited to you, without your being permitted to hunt.”

And He says:

“They ask you what has been made lawful for them. Say, ‘Good things have been made lawful for you,’”

through to His words:

“The food of those who were given the Scripture is lawful for you, and your food is lawful for them.”

This addresses the believers. Hence He says:

“The food of those who were given the Scripture is lawful for you,”

then says:

“And your food is lawful for them.”

If whatever has been made lawful for us were also lawful for them, there would have been no need for this statement. His words, “And your food is lawful for them,” do not include what has been forbidden to them, just as His words, “The food of those who were given the Scripture is lawful for you,” do not include things forbidden to us which they regard as lawful, such as game hunted within the Sacred Precinct and animals slaughtered with the invocation of a name other than Allah’s.

Does their food which has been made lawful for us include what has been forbidden to them but not to us—for example, when they slaughter camels? There is a well-known disagreement over this. The established position in Mālik’s school—which is also one of the two positions in Ahmad’s school—is that it is forbidden. The position of Abū Hanīfa and al-Shāfiʿī, and the other position in Ahmad’s school, is that it is lawful.

Is the legal basis that they did not intend a valid ritual slaughter, or that it is not part of their food? There is disagreement over this.

---

## Page 9783

If they slaughter an animal for a Muslim, is the ruling the same as when they slaughter it for themselves? There is disagreement over this. There are also two opinions on whether they may slaughter sacrificial animals, provided they belong to those whose slaughtered meat is lawful. Both opinions are reported from Ahmad: prohibition is Mālik’s position, while permissibility is the position of Abū Hanīfa and al-Shāfiʿī. If the slaughterer is Jewish, two grounds for prohibition then arise concerning the slaughter. This, however, is not the place to discuss these questions.

Then, after describing those who speak about Allah without knowledge, merely following their predecessors, He, glorified be He, described those who conceal the clear proofs and guidance Allah has revealed after He has made them clear to people in the Book. He said:

“Indeed, those who conceal what Allah has revealed of the Book and exchange it for a small price consume nothing but fire into their bellies. Allah will neither speak to them on the Day of Resurrection nor purify them, and they will have a painful punishment.”

This is the condition of those who conceal the Messenger’s teaching; the former is the condition of those who turn away from it to what contradicts it. Those who turn away from it to what contradicts it include anyone who follows an earlier or later authority in something he knows contradicts the Messenger’s words, whether that authority is a Companion, a Successor, one of the four renowned jurists, or anyone else.

As for someone who believes that the authorities he follows agree with the Messenger in what they say, if he has reached that conclusion through a scholarly method, he is exercising independent scholarly judgment and is subject to the same ruling as others like him. But if he speaks without knowledge, he is among those who are blameworthy.

---

## Page 9784

Anyone who claims a consensus that contradicts a text from the Messenger, without a text supporting his claim, and believes that those forming the consensus may oppose the Messenger on the basis of their own judgment, and that consensus abrogates a text—as some speculative theologians and proponents of personal opinion maintain—is of the same kind as these people.

If, however, he believes that the consensus indicates the existence of a text that has not reached us and that abrogates the earlier text, then, although his position is unsound, he is exercising independent scholarly judgment in this matter.
