The error in his position should be explained to him, just as with someone who counters an authentic hadith with a weak hadith that he believes to be authentic. Although his assertion is not true, its weakness should be explained to him.

This is done by showing him that no consensus contrary to the text exists, or by explaining that the Muslim community has never agreed to depart from a text unless it possessed a known text which its members knew abrogated the earlier one. Thus, the claim that a text and consensus conflict is false. It should also be explained to him that such a thing is inadmissible: the texts are known and preserved, and the community is commanded to seek them out and follow them. As for establishing a consensus contrary to them without a supporting text, it is impossible to know that every single Muslim scholar has opposed that text.

Consensus is of two kinds:

Definitive consensus: there is no way to establish a definitive consensus contrary to a text.

As for presumptive consensus, this comprises consensus established through tacit acknowledgment and consensus inferred from surveying scholarly opinions: one surveys the scholars’ statements and finds no disagreement on the matter, or a statement in the Quran becomes widely known and one knows of no one who has objected to it. Such a consensus, although it may be cited as evidence,

---

## Page 9785

cannot be used to set aside known texts, because it is presumptive evidence whose validity one cannot affirm with certainty: one cannot be certain that no dissenting opinion exists. Where the absence of dissent is certain, the consensus is definitive. But where one merely considers dissent unlikely, without certainty, the evidence is presumptive. Presumptive evidence cannot override a known text. It may, however, be cited as evidence and given precedence over evidence of lesser probability, while stronger presumptive evidence takes precedence over it. Thus, whenever one considers the meaning indicated by the text more probable than the existence of consensus, one gives precedence to the text’s meaning. Whenever one considers the consensus more probable, one gives precedence to it. In reality, only one position is correct.

If subsidiary rulings on the matter have been reported to him, but the authenticity of the report has not been established, he must refrain from presuming a consensus unless he considers that report unsound. Otherwise, whenever he allows that the person reporting disagreement might be truthful or might be lying, he remains uncertain whether consensus exists. With such uncertainty, he has neither certain nor presumptive knowledge of a consensus. Scriptural evidence cannot be set aside on the basis of something so uncertain.

Yet such a situation does not actually arise: there can never be a consensus that must be followed while conflicting with another, otherwise unopposed text. Nor can there ever be a text that must be followed without anyone in the Muslim community upholding it. Rather, those who uphold it may be unknown to many people.

Al-Tirmidhī said:

“Every hadith in my book has been acted upon by some scholars, except two: the hadith on combining prayers and the hadith on putting the wine-drinker to death.”

Nevertheless, both hadiths have been acted upon by a group of scholars. The hadith on combining prayers has been acted upon by Ahmad and others.

---

## Page 9786

But someone who has established the authenticity of a text, yet knows of no one who upholds it and does not know whether a consensus has formed against it, is like someone who encounters two conflicting pieces of evidence without yet knowing which carries greater weight. He must suspend judgment until it becomes clear which outweighs the other. He must neither express an opinion without knowledge nor follow a text … (1) when he considers its abrogation and its non-abrogation equally likely because of another text or a presumed consensus that he considers to conflict with it. Nor may he follow a general text when he considers it equally likely that its scope has or has not been restricted. The evidence must therefore be free of any countervailing evidence of equal weight: he must consider the absence of such countervailing evidence more probable. Otherwise, judgment must be suspended.

Furthermore, who would suppose that a consensus of this kind could be invoked against a text unless he considered the existence of that consensus more probable, or had another text abrogating the first, supported by what he believed to be a consensus?

In most matters in which the scholars of Medina invoke established practice as evidence, they also have a text supporting their position. The text supported by established practice takes precedence over the other. This is the correct position within the school of Ahmad and others—for example, giving precedence to the hadith of ʿUthmān:

“A person in ihrām must not marry,”

over the hadith of Ibn ʿAbbās, and similar cases.

As for rejecting a text solely on the basis of established practice, the overwhelming majority of scholars regard this as invalid. Scholars have differed over whether someone who opposes consensus thereby becomes a disbeliever; there are two views.

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank in the original.

---

## Page 9787

And the correct conclusion is that whoever opposes a consensus known with certainty becomes a disbeliever, just as whoever opposes a revealed text becomes a disbeliever by abandoning it. However, this occurs only in matters in which a revealed text is known to be established. As for knowing with certainty that consensus exists on a matter for which there is no revealed text, this does not occur. Where consensus is not known with certainty, one cannot declare someone a disbeliever for opposing it. Thus, consensus and the revealed text constitute two proofs, like the Quran and the Sunnah.

Scholars have also differed over whether consensus constitutes conclusive or presumptive evidence. The correct conclusion is that consensus established with certainty is conclusive, while consensus established presumptively is presumptive. And Allah knows best.

A verse similar to this one has been mentioned in Surah al-Mā’idah. In Surah al-Zukhruf, He says:

“Even if I bring you better guidance than what you found your forefathers following?”

This applies to anyone to whom it has been made clear that another position offers better guidance than the position with which he was raised: he must follow it. As He says:

“Follow the best of what has been sent down to you from your Lord.”

And He says:

“Hold firmly to them, and command your people to follow the best of them.”

And He says:

“Those who listen to what is said and follow the best of it.”

In matters of belief, one must follow the better of two positions. No one may hold a position while believing that the opposing position is better. When someone is given a choice between two actions, one of which is better, that action remains preferable. Even if he is permitted to perform the less preferable action, he must believe the other to be better and hold it dearer than the lesser one. This is following what is best.

---

## Page 9788

If one scholar reports a consensus and another reports disagreement—whether naming the scholar who held the dissenting view or merely reporting disagreement without naming anyone—it is not sufficient to say, “The report of disagreement is unsubstantiated.” For the reply would be, “The report of consensus is likewise unsubstantiated.” Indeed, the person reporting consensus denies the existence of disagreement, while the other affirms it, and affirmation takes precedence over denial.

If someone says, “The person reporting disagreement may have erred in affirming it, either because the chain of transmission is weak or because the statements cited do not indicate disagreement,” the reply is: “The person denying disagreement is even more liable to error. There may be opinions on the issue that have not reached him. Or they may have reached him, but he considered their chains of transmission weak, while others found them authentic. Or he may have thought the statements did not indicate disagreement when they did.” Every error possible for the person affirming disagreement is also possible for the person denying it, with the additional possibility of being unaware of the disagreement altogether. This applies to disagreements generally: lack of knowledge is not knowledge of nonexistence, especially regarding the opinions of the scholars of Muhammad’s community—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—which none but the Lord of all worlds can fully encompass.

For this reason, Ahmad and other scholars said: “Whoever claims consensus has lied. This is the claim of al-Marīsī and al-Asamm. Rather, one should say, ‘I know of no disagreement.’” Those who used to cite consensus, such as al-Shāfiʿī, Abū Thawr, and others, explained that they meant, “We know of no disagreement,” and would say, “This is the consensus we claim.”

It is thus clear that a consensus of this kind, countered by a report of disagreement, when neither has been established

---

## Page 9789

cannot be cited as evidence. If someone finds neither the report affirming disagreement nor the report denying it more persuasive than the other, he likewise has no grounds to give the claimed consensus precedence over the scriptural text, or the text precedence over it. Rather, he must suspend judgment, since neither carries greater weight for him. If he finds the report affirming disagreement more persuasive, he considers it most likely that no consensus contradicts the text, and he acts upon that text.

In assessing this, he considers those reporting consensus and disagreement. Someone known frequently to claim consensus when the facts are otherwise is not on the same footing as someone who has never been known to affirm a consensus subsequently shown not to exist. Likewise, someone known to err only rarely when reporting disagreement is not on the same footing as someone known to make frequent mistakes.

When two people independently report disagreement, neither having taken his report from the other, their reports establish that disagreement exists. Claims of consensus are different: even if several people concur in reporting consensus, this establishes only that they know of no disagreement. This applies to those who affirm disagreement over combining the three and those who deny it. Moreover, most of those affirming disagreement cite an authentic report that cannot be dismissed, while those denying it have nothing that invalidates it.

Many later jurists—or most of them—say that they are unable to derive all the rulings of Islamic law from the Messenger’s teachings. They therefore treat their imams’ statements as equivalent to the Messenger’s statements and follow their authority. Undoubtedly, many people need to follow the authority of scholars concerning matters that arise which they cannot understand independently. Among those who follow the path of spiritual aspiration, worship, poverty, and Sufism are some who treat their shaykh

---

## Page 9790

in the same way. Indeed, he may even regard him as infallible, taking guidance for his spiritual path only from him rather than from the Messenger—even though learning to follow the spiritual path from the Messenger is easier than learning the disputed points of law. For the spiritual path consists in following the way Allah and His Messenger have commanded in matters of belief, worship, and character. All of this is clearly explained in the Book and Sunnah, for it is like nourishment that the believer cannot do without.

Thus, all the Companions knew how to follow the spiritual path through the guidance of the Book and Sunnah and the teachings conveyed from the Messenger. They had no need to consult the jurists among the Companions about this. Nor did disagreements arise among them concerning it, as they did over certain questions of jurisprudence whose answers were unknown to most of the Companions. They would discuss fatwas and legal rulings, and some of them would seek fatwas on these matters.

As for the obligatory and recommended deeds performed by someone seeking nearness to Allah, all of them learned these from the Book and Sunnah, for the Quran and hadith are filled with such teachings. Even if one of them spoke about this without himself citing a source, his words or their meaning would be transmitted from Allah and His Messenger. One of them might utter a word of wisdom, and you would find it reported from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. This accords with what has been said in explanation of His words:

“Light upon light.”

Yet many devoted to worship and asceticism have turned away from seeking the prophetic knowledge through which they could learn the way of Allah and His Messenger. Consequently, they have needed to follow the authority of a shaykh.

---

## Page 9791

There are also matters concerning the spiritual path over which the shaykhs have disagreed. Yet the Book and Sunnah contain texts indicating the correct view that most people following this path can understand. Matters concerning the spiritual path are like matters of creed: all are explicitly addressed in the Book and Sunnah. The speculative theologians disagreed only when they turned away from the Book and Sunnah; once they ventured into religious innovations, disagreements arose. The same applies to the path of worship: most disagreements concerning it arise only because people turn away from the prescribed path, fall into religious innovations, and consequently disagree among themselves.

Likewise, disagreements in jurisprudence arose because the Prophet’s explanations of the Law were not clear to them. Such disputes, however, concern its finer points; they do not disagree over its major matters. The Companions themselves disagreed over some of these questions, but they did not disagree over matters of creed or the path to Allah by which a person becomes one of Allah’s righteous friends who are brought near to Him.

For this reason, when most shaykhs needed guidance on legal questions—such as marriage, inheritance shares, purification, the prostrations for forgetfulness, and similar matters—they followed the authority of the jurists, because deriving these rulings from the texts was difficult for them. As for matters such as reliance upon Allah, sincerity, and asceticism, they exercised their own judgment. Those among them who followed the Messenger were correct, while those who departed from his guidance were mistaken.

There is no doubt that religious innovations have proliferated far more in matters of worship and the will than in matters of belief and speech, because people share in the will more widely than

---

## Page 9792

they share in speech; for speech requires reason, and articulate speech is distinctive to human beings. Will in general, however, is an attribute shared by all animals: there is no animal without a will. These people shared the desire to worship, but differed over whom they worshipped and how they worshipped. Thus Allah described Christian monasticism in the Quran as something they had invented, and condemned the polytheists for the acts of worship and prohibitions they had invented. These were more numerous than their invented beliefs, for they were generally ignorant in matters of belief, and so their innovations in that sphere were fewer. Hence, the closer people were to the Messenger, the less grave their innovations were, and these concerned doctrines. Among the Successors and their followers, there was no one who worshipped through dancing and devotional listening, although there were Khārijites, Mu‘tazilites, and Shiites among them. There were those who denied divine decree, but none who invoked divine decree as a justification.

Most of the numerous innovations that arose among later devotees, ascetics, the poor, and Sufis did not exist in the time of the Successors and their followers. By contrast, the doctrines of those who introduced doctrinal innovations appeared during the age of the Companions and the Successors. This shows that the specious reasoning behind those doctrines was stronger and their adherents more rational. As for the innovations of these later groups, their adherents are more ignorant and further removed from following the Messenger. Thus, among them are those who claim divinity, divine indwelling, and union with Allah, and those who claim that they are superior to the Messenger, that they have no need of the Messenger, and that

---

## Page 9793

they have a path to Allah other than the path of the Messenger. This is not the kind of innovation introduced by Muslims, but the kind introduced by irreligious philosophers and others like them. People have recognized that those philosophers are not Muslims, yet these people claim to be Allah’s allies while making statements that only someone whose unbelief exceeds that of Jews and Christians would make.

Many of them—or most of them—do not realize that this contradicts the Messenger. Indeed, one group among them believes that the People of the Portico fought the Messenger and that he approved of their doing so. Others believe that the Messenger was commanded to go and greet them and ask them to supplicate for him, but that they refused him permission to enter, saying, “Go to those to whom you were sent.” They claim that he then returned to his Lord, who commanded him to humble himself and say, “Your humble servant has come to greet you.” They then comforted him and permitted him to enter.

Despite holding this monstrously unbelieving doctrine—which no Jew or Christian who acknowledges him as Allah’s Messenger to the unlettered would hold—they say that the Messenger approved of their conduct and acknowledged it, and that he acknowledged them as Allah’s chosen intimates. They claim that Allah addresses them without the Messenger’s mediation and has given them no need of him, just as some of a king’s intimates have no need of his ministers. They cite the account of al-Khidr and Moses as evidence, whereas it is evidence against them, not in their favour, in many respects that have been explained at length elsewhere.

Misguidance and ignorance are more prevalent among devotees and innovators than among proponents of doctrines. Nevertheless, they possess a degree of asceticism, worship, and good character not found among

---

## Page 9794

the latter, who in turn display a degree of arrogance, miserliness, and hardness of heart not found in the former. Thus, the former resemble Christians, while the latter resemble Jews. Allah Most High has commanded us to say:

“Guide us to the straight path,
the path of those You have blessed—not those who have incurred Your wrath, nor those who have gone astray.”

Consequently, many of their leading spiritual masters eventually came to contemplate Allah’s oneness in lordship and to affirm divine decree, which encompasses everything in existence. They regarded complete absorption in this contemplation, with the disappearance of the distinction between good and evil deeds, as the highest spiritual station. Beyond it lay only what they called “oneness,” which is a form of the indwelling and union professed by Christians. Yet they fear expressing this openly and treat it as a concealed secret.

Some of them say that this was al-Hallāj’s spiritual vision, and that he was killed only because he divulged a secret that should not have been divulged.

If, in addition, one of them has learned from speculative theologians or others who argue for divine decree and regard everything as proceeding from a single will—with no love, hatred, pleasure, displeasure, or joy, but merely different objects of that will, so that its relation to reward is called pleasure and its relation to punishment is called displeasure—then, with this spiritual vision, he no longer retains any distinction. They call this “gathering” and “overwhelming absorption.”

And al-Junayd—may Allah sanctify his soul—when his companions, such as al-Thawrī, reached

---

## Page 9795

this station, as did others like him, he instructed them to observe the second distinction: to distinguish between what is commanded and what is forbidden, between what Allah loves and is pleased with and what incurs His displeasure and hatred.

This is the contemplation of Allah’s sole right to worship, which the messengers brought and the revealed Books taught. It is the true meaning of the declaration: “There is no deity worthy of worship except Allah.”

Some of them objected to al-Junayd, some withheld judgment, and others agreed. The correct position is what al-Junayd expressed in this statement about distinguishing between what is commanded and what is forbidden, and in his other statement about distinguishing between the Lord and the servant: “Affirming divine oneness means distinguishing what comes into being from what is beginningless.” This latter statement refutes those among them who profess union or indwelling, while the former refutes those who stop at the reality of the created order. How many of them have been afflicted with these two errors!

Then there are people who uphold this distinction, but for their own sake and desires, not as worship and obedience to Allah. Such a person resembles someone who wages jihad, enjoins good, and forbids evil to satisfy his own desires—like someone who fights to display courage, out of partisan zeal, or to show off. The other resembles someone who neither enjoins good, nor forbids evil, nor wages jihad. The one who neglects these duties resembles a monk, while the self-serving person resembles someone who seeks nothing but this world. The former is an innovator, and the latter an evildoer.

Religious innovations have become widespread among those who profess asceticism and spiritual poverty, while pursuit of this world has become widespread among those who turn away from such practices. Those who seek this world oppose those who renounce it only to serve their own ends, even when the latter are religious innovators. Those others likewise oppose worldly people only to serve their own ends. Thus their disputes concern worldly interests,

---

## Page 9796

not so that Allah’s word may be supreme or that worship may be devoted to Allah alone, contrary to the way of the early generations—may Allah be pleased with them all. Both groups have departed from the straight path.

We ask Allah to guide us to the straight path, the path of those whom Allah has blessed: the prophets, the steadfastly truthful, the martyrs, and the righteous. What excellent companions they are!

Here it ends. All praise is due to Allah, Lord of all worlds.

---

## Page 9797

He was also asked—may Allah have mercy on him:

“Is someone correct in saying, ‘The scriptural texts do not cover a hundredth of the Sharia’? Does he mean an unambiguous text that admits no alternative interpretation, or transmitted wording that admits different interpretations?

“Are those Zāhirīs who reject analogical reasoning and declare it invalid correct? What evidence do they have for that?

“What do they mean by the term ‘text’?”

He replied:

All praise is due to Allah, Lord of all worlds. This statement was made by a group of proponents of speculative theology and juristic opinion, such as Abū al-Maʿālī and others, and it is mistaken. Rather, the correct position, held by the majority of Muslim imams, is that the scriptural texts cover most of the rulings governing people’s actions.

Some maintain that they cover all such rulings. Those who deny this do so only because they have not understood the meanings of the texts of general scope—the words of Allah and His Messenger—or how comprehensively they encompass the rulings governing people’s actions. For Allah sent Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, with concise yet comprehensive speech. He would utter a comprehensive statement of general scope constituting a universal proposition and a general principle encompassing numerous categories, which in turn encompass countless individual instances. It is in this way that the scriptural texts encompass the rulings governing people’s actions.

---

## Page 9798

An example of this is that Allah prohibited khamr, but some people thought that the term applied only to grape juice. Some of them therefore prohibited nothing else, while others also prohibited certain intoxicating drinks made by infusion. This is the position held by some jurists of Kufa. Abū Hanīfa prohibits grape juice that has become strong and frothy; this, in his view, is khamr. He also prohibits cooked grape juice unless two-thirds of it has evaporated; once two-thirds has evaporated, he no longer prohibits it. He prohibits uncooked date infusion, but considers it lawful if it has been cooked even slightly. These three intoxicating drinks are not khamr in his view, although they are forbidden. As for other infused drinks, he prohibits only the amount that causes intoxication. Muhammad ibn al-Hasan, however, agreed with the majority that every intoxicant is forbidden, whether in small or large quantities. The leading scholars among Abū Hanīfa’s followers issued fatwas to this effect, and this was also the position adopted by Abū al-Layth al-Samarqandī.

Some scholars prohibited every intoxicant through analogical reasoning, extending either the term or the ruling. This approach was followed by a group of jurists among the followers of Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī, and Ahmad, who thought that the prohibition of every intoxicant rested solely on analogy concerning either terminology or the ruling.

The correct position, held by the great imams, is that the khamr mentioned in the Quran encompasses every intoxicant. Thus, the prohibition of every intoxicant is established by the scriptural text of general scope

---

## Page 9799

and its comprehensive wording, not by analogy alone, although analogy provides further evidence consistent with the text. Authentic statements from the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) also establish the prohibition of every intoxicant. In Sahīh Muslim, the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) is reported to have said:

“Every intoxicant is khamr, and every intoxicant is forbidden.”

In the two Sahīhs, ‘Ā’isha (may Allah be pleased with her) reports that the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) said:

“Every drink that intoxicates is forbidden.”

The two Sahīhs also report from Abū Mūsā that the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) was asked:

“We have a drink made from honey called al-bit‘, and a drink made from sorghum called al-mizr?”

The narrator said that he had been endowed with concise, comprehensive speech, and he replied:

“Every intoxicant is forbidden.”

There are other hadiths as well, which would take too long to recount. Accordingly, the prohibition of intoxicating drinks and foods, such as intoxicating hashish, is established by the scriptural text. This text encompasses drinking every kind of intoxicant, whatever its ingredients: grains, fruits, the milk of horses, or anything else.

Those who thought that the text applied only to grape wine said that it did not explain the ruling on these other intoxicants—which are more abundant in the world than grape wine—and that their prohibition was instead established by analogy. These people misunderstood the text. This is further demonstrated by the numerous, widely transmitted hadiths establishing that, when khamr was prohibited, Medina had no

---

## Page 9800

grape wine at all; Medina had no grapevines, but only date palms, so their khamr was made from dates. When khamr was prohibited, they poured away those date-based drinks, knowing that they were forbidden khamr. This establishes that, in their usage, the word khamr was not restricted to grape juice. It makes no difference whether this was already its meaning in their language, so that it encompassed [other drinks] (1), or whether they understood its broader scope through the Messenger’s explanation (peace and blessings be upon him), for he explains what Allah intends. The Lawgiver uses language as people do in customary usage, sometimes employing a word more broadly than its linguistic meaning, and sometimes more narrowly.

Likewise, according to most scholars, the term maysir (gambling) encompasses playing backgammon and chess. It also encompasses sales involving uncertainty, which the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) prohibited, because they involve the gambling that constitutes maysir. Gambling means that a person’s money is taken while he runs the risk of receiving nothing in return. Examples include buying a runaway slave, a straying camel, habal al-habala, and similar things that the buyer may or may not obtain. Accordingly, the term maysir in the Book of Allah Most High encompasses all of these. The authentic report in Sahīh Muslim states:

“The Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) forbade sales involving uncertainty.”

This encompasses every transaction involving such risk, including selling fruit before it shows signs of ripening, selling unborn animals in the womb, and other such transactions.

The same applies to the term ribā (usury), which encompasses every prohibited form of ribā

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) The text in square brackets is absent from the printed edition.
> Usāma ibn al-Zahrā’ — book coordinator for al-Mawsū‘a al-Shāmila (the Comprehensive Encyclopedia).

---

## Page 9801

involving deferment or unequal exchange, loans that yield a benefit, and the like. The scriptural text encompasses all of this. However, determining whether particular categories and individual cases fall within its scope requires supporting evidence. This is known as tahqīq al-manāt (ascertaining a ruling’s applicability to particular cases).

Likewise, Allah Most High says:

“O Prophet, when you divorce women, divorce them at the proper time for their waiting period.”

And He says:

“Divorced women shall wait for three periods.”

These and similar passages encompass every divorced woman by their wording and indicate that every divorce is revocable. For this reason, most scholars adopted this position and said that a man may not pronounce a threefold divorce upon his wife. They also indicate that divorce takes effect only as a revocable divorce, and that an irrevocable separation does not count as one of the three divorces. Thus, khulʿ (release from marriage in return for compensation) does not count as one of the three divorces. This is the position of Ibn ʿAbbās, of al-Shāfiʿī according to one of his opinions, and of Ahmad according to the well-known position reported from him. They disagree, however, over whether this requires that khulʿ involve neither the wording nor the intention of divorce, that it involve no wording of divorce alone, or that neither condition be required. These are three opinions.

Likewise, Allah Most High says:

“Allah has already prescribed for you a way to release yourselves from your oaths.”

And:

“That is the expiation for your oaths.”

This encompasses every oath taken by Muslims. Some scholars have therefore said: “Every oath taken by a Muslim is subject to expiation, as indicated by the Quran and the Sunnah.”

Others have said: “The text encompasses only oaths sworn by Allah’s name. Other oaths are not binding and entail no obligation.”

Still others have said: “Rather,

---

## Page 9802

they are oaths that bind those who swear them to what they have undertaken, and they do not fall within the scope of the text.” There is no doubt that the text supports the first position. Whoever says, “The text does not explain the ruling on all oaths taken by Muslims,” is expressing his own opinion, not what the text indicates.

The same applies to most matters disputed among Muslims: whoever seeks what will resolve the dispute in the texts of the Quran and Sunnah will find it, and it will become clear that these texts encompass rulings governing actions in general.

Imam Ahmad used to say: “There is no question about which one is asked without the Companions having addressed it or a comparable case.” The Companions generally supported their positions with scriptural texts, as is well known of them. They also exercised independent judgment, expressed their considered opinions, and argued on the basis of sound analogy.

Sound analogy is of two kinds:

The first is when it is known that the only difference between the new case and the original case has no legal significance. Thus, it is authentically reported in al-Sahīh that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was asked about a mouse that had fallen into clarified butter. He said: “Remove it and what surrounds it, and eat your clarified butter.”

Muslims unanimously agree that this ruling is not confined to that particular mouse and that particular clarified butter. Hence, the vast majority of scholars have said that any ritually impure substance that falls into any kind of fat or oil—such as a mouse falling into oil or a cat falling into clarified butter—is subject to the same ruling as that mouse which fell into clarified butter. Whoever among

---

## Page 9803

the literalists says, “This ruling applies only to a mouse that has fallen into clarified butter,” is mistaken. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did not restrict the ruling to that particular case; rather, when asked about it, he gave a ruling concerning it. When a request for a ruling concerns a particular case or category, the mufti addresses it specifically because he was asked about it, not because the ruling applies exclusively to it. A similar example is the report:

“He was asked about a man who had entered the state of consecration for ‘umrah while wearing a robe perfumed with a saffron-based scent. He said: ‘Remove the robe, wash off the perfume, and do in your ‘umrah what you would do in your Hajj.’”

He thus answered the question about the robe. Had the man been wearing a shirt or something similar, the ruling would have been the same, by unanimous agreement.

The second kind of analogy is when a text stipulates a ruling on account of an underlying factor that is also present in another case. If evidence establishes that the ruling is tied to the underlying factor shared by the original case and the new case, the two are treated alike, and this constitutes sound analogy. The Companions and those who followed them in righteousness employed both kinds. Both are means of understanding the Lawgiver’s intent.

Drawing inferences from the Lawgiver’s words requires knowing that the wording is authentically attributable to him and knowing what he intends by it. Once we know his intent, if we know that the ruling is based on the shared underlying factor, rather than on a factor specific to

---

## Page 9804

the original case, we apply the ruling wherever the shared underlying factor is present. If, however, we know that he intended to restrict the ruling to the case addressed by the text, we disallow analogy. Thus, we know that Hajj is specifically associated with the Kaaba, obligatory fasting with the month of Ramadan, the direction faced in prayer with the Kaaba, and obligatory prayers with the five daily prayers, and so forth. In such cases, we cannot extend the ruling by analogy beyond what the text specifies.

When the Lawgiver specifies a place or time for worship, such as the Kaaba and the month of Ramadan, or specifies particular words and actions, such as recitation, bowing, and prostration in prayer—indeed, specifying the declaration “Allah is greatest” and the Opening Surah—extending this to something not specified in the text resembles the conduct of the people of Yemen who disregarded the designation of the sacred months. They said, “What is intended is any four months of the year.” Allah Most High therefore said:

“Postponing the sacred months is only an increase in disbelief, by which those who disbelieve are led astray. They permit it one year and forbid it another, so as to match the number of months Allah has made sacred, thereby permitting what Allah has forbidden.”

Drawing an analogy between what a revealed text permits and what a revealed text forbids is of the same kind as the analogy of those who said:

“‘Trade is only like usury.’ But Allah has permitted trade and forbidden usury.”

Likewise, the polytheists drew an analogy between carrion and properly slaughtered animals, saying, “Do you eat what you have killed but not what Allah has killed?” Allah Most High said:

“The devils surely inspire their allies to argue with you. If you obey them, you will indeed be polytheists.”

These are unsound analogies. Every analogy that a revealed text shows to be unsound is unsound. And anyone who equates

---

## Page 9805

a case specified in a revealed text with another whose prescribed ruling differs has drawn an unsound analogy. Likewise, anyone who equates two things or distinguishes between them on grounds other than the attributes deemed relevant in the ruling of Allah and His Messenger has drawn an unsound analogy. Some analogies, however, are known to be sound, others are known to be unsound, and others remain unresolved. Whoever rejects analogy altogether adopts a false position; whoever argues by an analogy contrary to revealed law likewise adopts a false position. Whoever relies on an analogy whose soundness has not been established by evidence relies on something he does not know to be sound, much like someone who relies on a report from an unknown man whose uprightness he cannot establish.

Arguments based on transmitted reports and on reasoning thus fall into three categories: those known to be sound, those known to be unsound, and those on which judgment is suspended until evidence establishes one or the other.

The term “text” sometimes refers to the wording of the Quran and Sunnah, whether its meaning is definitive or merely apparent. This is what is meant by the statement: “The revealed texts encompass the rulings governing the actions of those accountable under religious law.” The term “text” can also refer to wording whose meaning is definitive and admits no contrary interpretation, as in His words:

“That makes ten in all.”

And:

“It is Allah who has sent down the Book in truth and the Balance.”

The Book is the revealed text, and the Balance is justice. Sound analogy is a form of justice, for it treats like things alike and distinguishes between things that differ. What sound analogy indicates agrees with what the revealed text indicates. Every analogy that conflicts with the meaning of the revealed text is therefore unsound. No revealed text contradicts a sound analogy, just as no clear rational truth contradicts an authentic transmitted report.

---

## Page 9806

Anyone deeply versed in the evidence of revealed law can establish most rulings through both revealed texts and analogies. Thus, both the revealed text and analogy establish the same ruling, as in the examples we have mentioned. Analogy indicates that every intoxicant is forbidden, just as the revealed text does.

Allah has forbidden wine because it causes enmity and hatred among us and keeps us from remembering Allah and from prayer, as the Quran indicates. This same consideration applies to all intoxicating drinks, with no distinction between one drink and another. To distinguish between varieties belonging to this same category is to distinguish between like things and depart from what sound analogy entails, just as it is a departure from what the revealed texts entail. They acknowledge that their position runs contrary to analogy, but say: “We have transmitted reports supporting it, and we have followed them.” They also say: “The term ‘wine’ does not encompass every intoxicant.” They have misunderstood the revealed text—even though they are scholars exercising independent judgment and are rewarded for their efforts.

Knowing whether the terms occurring in a revealed text are general or specific in scope is part of knowing the limits of what Allah has revealed to His Messenger. Allah Most High says:

“The Bedouins are more extreme in disbelief and hypocrisy, and more likely to be ignorant of the limits of what Allah has revealed to His Messenger.”

An exhaustive assessment of the arguments of those who reject analogy and those who affirm it would be lengthy; this page cannot accommodate a fuller discussion. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 9807

He also said:

Section

The commanded acts of worship—such as faith in its entirety and its branches, including prayer, ablution, ritual bathing, pilgrimage, fasting, jihad, recitation, remembrance of Allah, and other acts—have three possible states, though sometimes only two are prescribed for them. A servant may either confine himself to what is obligatory, also perform what is recommended, or fall short of what is obligatory.

The first is the state of those who take a middle course in that act of worship, even if they are foremost in others. The second is the state of one who is foremost in it. The third is the state of one who wrongs himself in it.

A complete act of worship sometimes means one in which all obligations have been fulfilled, and sometimes one in which the recommended elements have also been performed. In contrast to a complete act stands a deficient one. Deficiency may mean omitting some of its obligations, or it may mean leaving out some of its recommended elements.

As for understanding completeness to mean completeness through the recommended elements, this is the predominant usage among jurists when discussing purification, prayer, and other matters. They say:

Ablution is divided into complete and sufficient forms. Ritual bathing is likewise divided into complete and sufficient forms. By “sufficient,” they mean restricting oneself

---

## Page 9808

to what is obligatory, and by “complete,” they mean an act that also includes what is recommended in number, extent, manner, and other respects.

Thus, they cited the hadith of Ibn Masʿūd, traced back to the Prophet:

“When he says three times while bowing, ‘Glory be to my Lord, the Magnificent,’ his bowing is complete, and that is its minimum. When he says three times while prostrating, ‘Glory be to my Lord, the Most High,’ his prostration is complete, and that is its minimum.”

They therefore said, “The minimum for completeness is three glorifications,” meaning the minimum for the completeness that is recommended. They also said, “The minimum for witr prayer is one unit, and the minimum for completeness is three.” Thus, they distinguished between lower and higher degrees of completeness, both referring to recommended rather than obligatory completeness.

They then differ regarding negation applied to terms defined by the revealed law, as in his words:

“There is no recitation without the Mother of the Book.”

“There is no fast for one who has not formed the intention to fast during the night.”

“There is no prayer for one who has no ablution.”

“There is no ablution for one over whose ablution Allah’s name has not been mentioned.”

Most of them say that this negates the act itself, so an act negated in this way does not suffice. Others say that it negates completeness, meaning recommended completeness.

As for understanding completeness as the fulfillment of what is obligatory, this is found in the Lawgiver’s usage. However, the word frequently used there is “completion,” as in His words:

“And complete the pilgrimage and the lesser pilgrimage for Allah.”

The completion required here means fulfilling the obligatory elements. Likewise, His words: “Then complete the fast

---

## Page 9809

until nightfall.” Likewise, his words:

“No Eid prayer is complete until the worshipper applies the water of purification to the prescribed parts”—to the end of the hadith.

And his words:

“Whatever you leave out of this, you have left out of your prayer.”

Regarding the completion of pilgrimage, fasting, and similar acts, one may say that this is an unrestricted command to complete both their obligatory and recommended elements. Where an element is obligatory, the command makes it binding; where it is recommended, the command recommends it. The word “complete” also occurs in his words:

“His bowing is complete, and that is its minimum.”

And his words:

“Straighten your rows, for straightening the row is part of completing the prayer.”

Another narration reads, “part of establishing the prayer.”

Deficiency is the opposite of completion and completeness, as in his words:

“Whoever offers a prayer without reciting the Mother of the Book in it, that prayer is deficient.”

The majority say that this means a deficiency in obligatory elements, because the word used here for “deficient” describes something lacking in its limbs and essential parts. Others say that it means falling short of recommended completeness. Indeed, deficiency frequently refers to the omission of recommended elements, as previously discussed concerning the jurists’ division of purification into that which is complete and that which is sufficient but not complete. Whatever is not complete is deficient. Likewise, his words:

“His bowing and prostration are complete, and that is their minimum.”

Whatever is not complete is deficient, even if it suffices.

Failure to fulfill what is obligatory is then of two kinds. One invalidates the act of worship, such as omitting essential elements of purification, prayer, or pilgrimage. The other does not invalidate it, such as omitting obligatory elements of pilgrimage that are not essential elements; omitting obligatory elements of prayer out of forgetfulness, according to the well-known position reported from Ahmad; and omitting obligatory elements for which Abū Hanīfa regards the person as having acted wrongly, but which do not invalidate

---

## Page 9810

his prayer, such as reciting al-Fātiha and the like. This resolves the confusion concerning “questions of religious designations and rulings,” namely, the question of faith and the dispute with the Murji’ah and the Khawārij.

For although faith denotes Allah’s religion, which He perfected—as He says:

“Today I have perfected your religion for you”—

and denotes obedience to Allah, righteousness, and righteous deeds, encompassing everything Allah has commanded, this is faith in its full and complete form. Its completeness is of two kinds: the completeness of those brought near to Allah, achieved through recommended acts, and the completeness of those who take the middle course, achieved through obligatory acts alone.

When, concerning such statements as the Prophet’s words, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“The one who commits unlawful sexual intercourse is not a believer while committing it, nor is the thief a believer while stealing,”

and:

“There is no faith in one who is not trustworthy,”

and Allah’s words:

“The believers are only those whose hearts tremble when Allah is mentioned”—and the rest of the verse;

and His words:

“The believers are only those who believe in Allah and His Messenger, then do not doubt, and strive with their wealth and their lives in the cause of Allah”;

and His words:

“Righteousness is not that you turn your faces toward the east or the west”—the verse continuing to His words:

“Those are the ones who have been truthful, and those are the ones mindful of Allah”—

when someone says concerning such passages, “He is not a believer whose faith is complete,” or denies the completeness of his faith rather than its foundation, what is meant is the obligatory completeness of faith, not its recommended completeness. This is like the case of someone who omits stoning the pillars or commits an act prohibited during ihrām other than sexual intercourse. It is not like our speaking of a complete ritual bath or complete ablution, and saying that the form which merely suffices is not

---

## Page 9811

complete. That is a denial of recommended completeness.

Likewise, a believer without qualification is one who fulfils the obligatory requirements of faith. His faith falling short of what is obligatory does not mean that it is invalid and nullified, just as in the case of Hajj. Nor does it mean that he possesses complete faith, as the Murji’ah claim. Nor should one say, “Even if he fulfilled what is obligatory, his faith would not be complete,” for the completeness denied in that case is recommended completeness.

This distinction dispels the confusion surrounding this issue and affirms the revealed texts as they stand. Likewise, concerning the Prophet’s saying:

“Whoever cheats us is not one of us,”

and similar statements, it is not permissible to say that this means, “He is not among the best of us,” as the Murji’ah claim. Nor may one say, “He has become a non-Muslim and is therefore a disbeliever,” as the Khawārij claim. Rather, the correct understanding is that this pronoun, when used without qualification, refers to believers who fulfil the obligatory requirements of faith, through which they deserve reward without punishment. They are entitled to unqualified allegiance and love, although some attain higher degrees of these through the recommended deeds they perform.

Thus, if someone cheats them, he is truly not one of them, because he falls short of the obligatory faith through which they deserve unqualified reward without punishment. Yet this does not necessarily mean that he is wholly outside their ranks. Rather, he possesses enough faith to deserve a share in their reward, while also bearing a major sin for which he deserves punishment.

This is like someone hiring a group of people to perform a task, with some of them working for only part of the time. When payment is due, it is appropriate to say:

“This one is not one of us, so he is not entitled to

---

## Page 9812

the full wages, even if he is entitled to part of them.”

I have discussed this issue at length elsewhere and explained its connection to a major principle: a single individual or act may be subject to command in one respect and prohibition in another. This is the position of Ahl al-Sunnah wa-l-Jamā‘ah, contrary to the Khawārij and the Mu‘tazilah.

A group of affirmationists—their theologians and jurists, both from our school and others—have agreed with them concerning a single act, an issue discussed in the principles of jurisprudence. They maintain that an act cannot be both commanded and prohibited, although they disagree with them concerning a single individual, an issue discussed in the foundations of religion.

There is no doubt that, according to one of the two positions reported from Ahmad, such an act does not discharge the obligation. The case in question is prayer in a usurped house. According to the other reported position, it does discharge the obligation, as most jurists hold. However, some scholars of our school have treated this as a question of reason and held that it is not rationally impossible. This is the position of most of the Mu‘tazilah and many of the Ash‘arīs, such as Ibn al-Bāqillānī and Ibn al-Khatīb.

The discussion thus concerns two distinct questions: whether this is rationally possible, and whether it discharges the obligation under religious law. There are four positions on the matter. Some maintain that it is rationally impossible and invalid under religious law. This is the position of a group of

---

## Page 9813

our school’s theologians and jurists.

Others say that it is rationally possible, but that the impediment rests on revealed evidence. This view may also be held by those in our school, and those who agree with them, who maintain that the obligation is not discharged. In my view, it is more consistent with Ahmad’s position, for his principles imply that all of this could conceivably be prescribed in religious law. This accords with the principles of Ahl al-Sunnah and the leading jurists. Others, including most jurists, hold that it is possible both rationally and under religious law.

Still others deem it rationally impossible but say that revealed evidence establishes its occurrence. This is the position of Ibn al-Bāqillānī, Abū al-Hasan, and Ibn al-Khatīb. They claimed that reason precludes a single act from being both commanded and prohibited. Yet when transmitted evidence—whether consensus or otherwise—indicated that making up the act was not obligatory, they said: “The obligation is discharged upon its occurrence, but not by virtue of it.” In my view, this is the most unsound of these positions.

The correct view is that this is rationally possible. Whether it actually occurs under religious law must be determined by the relevant evidence. For a single act to be both loved and disliked, pleasing and displeasing, commanded and prohibited, and deserving of praise and reward as well as blame and punishment is not a matter of inherent attributes such as being black or white, moving or still, alive or dead—though these attributes, too, warrant discussion. Rather, it belongs to

---

## Page 9814

attributes that involve a relation to something else, such as an act’s being beneficial and harmful, loved and disliked. What is beneficial brings pleasure, while what is harmful brings pain. Likewise, what is loved brings joy and pleasure to the one who loves it, for example, while what is disliked brings pain to the one who dislikes it. Thus, rational goodness and badness mean benefit and harm, whereas commands and prohibitions concern what is desired and what is disliked. The former is an attribute of the act in relation to its agent or someone else; the latter is an attribute of the act in relation to the one who commands and prohibits.

For this reason, I have said more than once that an act’s goodness sometimes derives from the act itself, sometimes from the one who commands it, and sometimes from both together. The Mu'tazilah and those jurists who agree with them, whether from our school or others, who rule out abrogation before there has been an opportunity to perform the act, affirm only the first. The Ash'arīs and those jurists who agree with them, whether from our school or others, who recognize no attribute of an act beyond its relation to the divine address that applies to it, affirm only the second.

The correct view is to affirm both. There is also an additional quality that the act acquires, of the same order as the divine address’s relation to it but distinct from that relation, which arises in the act after the ruling. Thus, the divine address sometimes reveals a quality, sometimes produces it, and sometimes does both. A fuller discussion of this belongs elsewhere.

This being so, we understand through reason and find through experience that a single act, whether performed by a person himself or by someone else, can bring him both benefit and harm. A man may have two enemies

---

## Page 9815

one of whom kills the other: he is pleased that one enemy is gone, but distressed that another has prevailed. He may also have two friends, one of whom removes the other from office: he is distressed at one friend’s removal, but pleased that another has assumed office. Most worldly affairs are like this, for unmixed benefit is rare. Most events contain something distressing and something pleasing. Thus, an act includes what is beneficial, loved, desired, and sought, as well as what is harmful, hated, disliked, and resisted. Likewise, one who issues commands commands the attainment of what is beneficial and prohibits the attainment of what is harmful. He therefore commands prayer, which involves benefit, and prohibits usurpation, which involves harm.

If they say:

“What is impossible is for him to command someone to perform a single act in one and the same respect, saying, ‘Pray here, and do not pray here.’ This combines contradictories, and combining contradictories is impossible, since it combines negation and affirmation,”

they may be answered as follows:

“Combining contradictories is impossible in a statement of fact. If you say, ‘Zayd prayed here; he did not pray here,’ this is impossible, because prayer here must either occur or not occur. Its occurrence consists in the prayer itself and the inseparable attributes that follow from it, which involve no relation, reference, or connection to anything else. But combining opposites in desire and aversion, seeking and resisting, love and hatred, and benefit and harm is not impossible. A thing’s existence may be desired, and its nonexistence may also be desired, if each brings some benefit to the one who desires it. Its existence or nonexistence may also be both desired and disliked, such that a person experiences pleasure and pain both at its existence and at its nonexistence, as has been said:

---

## Page 9816

Gray hair is hateful, yet hateful too to part with it;
Marvel at something loved despite being despised.

A person dislikes and hates gray hair because it entails the passing of beneficial youth and the onset of harmful aging. Yet he also loves it and dislikes its absence, because its presence means continued life, whereas its absence means death.

This is the case with everything that combines benefit and harm. On closer examination, however, a particular act—such as prayer in a particular house—cannot itself be both commanded and prohibited, since that would impose an impossible obligation: it would require the person acting to combine the occurrence and nonoccurrence of that particular act. Rather, prayer is commanded in unqualified terms, while occupying that place is prohibited. Thus, the command and the prohibition concern different things, though these become inseparably joined in the particular instance. It is the servant who has combined what is commanded with what is prohibited; the Lawgiver has not commanded him to combine them. He commanded prayer in unqualified terms and prohibited occupancy in unqualified terms. As for the particular instance itself, the Lawgiver neither commands nor prohibits it, just as with all other particular instances. This principle applies consistently to everything Allah has commanded in unqualified terms—indeed, to every command.

For when He commands the freeing of a slave without specifying one, as in His words:

‘Then a slave must be freed,’

or the feeding of sixty needy people, fasting for two consecutive months, prayer in a place, or anything else, the servant can comply only by freeing a particular slave, feeding particular food to particular needy people, fasting on particular days, and performing a prayer

---

## Page 9817

as a particular act in a particular place. Thus, in all unqualified commands, the particular instance is not itself specifically commanded. Rather, what is commanded is unqualified, and the unqualified is realized through a particular instance.

A particular instance therefore has two aspects: its individual particularity and its unqualified nature. It is the unqualified nature that is obligatory. Its individual particularity, however, is neither obligatory nor commanded; it is merely one of the instances through which the unqualified is realized, like a route to Mecca. The one issuing the command does not intend any particular specification. This discussion occurs in connection with obligations allowing a choice, unqualified obligations, and specifically designated obligations.

The distinction between them is that an obligation allowing a choice commands one of a limited set of things, whereas an unqualified obligation does not command one of a limited set of things; rather, it commands something without qualification.

Hence the disagreement concerning an obligation allowing a choice: is only the common element obligatory, as with an unqualified obligation? Or are both the common element and the distinguishing element obligatory, with a choice between the alternatives?

There are two views on this. The common element is simply being one of those alternatives. On the former view, what distinguishes one alternative from another does not earn the reward accorded to an obligation, unlike the view that the distinguishing element is also obligatory as one of the alternatives.

As for an unqualified obligation, the distinct particular instances are not intentionally addressed by the command. Nevertheless, a particular instance is necessary for actual performance. It therefore falls under the heading of that without which an obligation cannot be fulfilled. Even if this is said to be obligatory, it is obligatory in performance while still allowing a choice. Choosing one of the two particular instances does not make that instance specifically obligatory.

It thus becomes clear that specifying the particular act and the particular place is not commanded. Thus, if one is prohibited

---

## Page 9818

from being in it, this prohibited act is not something he has been commanded to do. What is commanded is unqualified, and this particular instance is not indispensable to it. Rather, compliance is achieved through it just as it is achieved through other instances.

If it is said, “Even if it is not commanded, fulfilling the command through it must nevertheless be permissible; yet combining prohibition and permissibility combines two contradictories,” the reply is: Fulfilling the command through it need not be declared permissible. It is enough that fulfilling the command through it is not prohibited. The means by which one performs an obligation need not itself be made obligatory or declared permissible. It is enough that fulfilling the command by that means is not prohibited. If fulfilling the command by that means is prohibited, however, the commanded act cannot be included in it without disobedience.

There are thus four cases:

The means of compliance may itself be obligatory, as when fasting the month of Ramadan is made obligatory by abstaining during it from what is obligatory.

It may be permissible, as with the alternative acts of expiation, since each kind of act has been permitted to the person concerned. Another example is saying, “Feed Zayd or ʿAmr.”

It may simply not be prohibited, as with fasting or freeing a slave without further qualification. The particular instance is neither prohibited nor declared permissible by a specific directive, since there is no need for that.

The fourth case is that it is prohibited, as with the prohibition on defective sacrificial animals and freeing

---

## Page 9819

an unbelieving slave.

If he prays in a permissible place, he has complied by performing the obligation through a particular instance that is not prohibited. If he prays on usurped property, it may be argued: The prohibition concerns being there in general, not specifically praying there. He has therefore performed the obligation through something he was not forbidden to use for compliance, although the general category to which his act belongs was prohibited. Thus, this particular act combines what is commanded—prayer in general—and what is prohibited—being there in general. He is therefore both obedient and disobedient. We do not say that this particular act is both commanded and prohibited; rather, it combines what is commanded and what is prohibited, just as when someone prays while engaging in a sin, such as carrying a usurped item.

Alternatively, it may be argued: He is indeed forbidden to comply through this act, just as he is forbidden to comply by praying in an impure place or an impure garment. This is because prayer requires a place, and prohibiting a general category entails prohibiting its particular kinds. He would therefore be forbidden to perform part of this prayer. This differs from a prohibited act that is separate from the prayer’s constituent parts, such as carrying a garment, for carrying it is not part of the prayer. This is the point that jurists examine, and it is open to independent legal reasoning. The question is not whether these very acts of occupying a place are both commanded and prohibited, for that is certainly false. Rather, even if these particular acts are prohibited, they include what is commanded; yet something that includes what is commanded in general is not thereby itself commanded.

It may then be said: Even if compliance in a particular manner were prohibited, as when one is told:

---

## Page 9820

“Pray, but do not pray on this spot,” and, “Sew this garment, but do not sew it in this house,” then if he prays there and sews there, he has undoubtedly not performed what was commanded in the manner required. But can it be said that he has performed part of what was commanded, or its essential act without its prescribed qualification—namely, prayer and sewing as such, without that qualification or accompanied by something prohibited—so that he is rewarded for that act even if it does not discharge the obligation or he is punished for the sin? This has already been discussed, and I have explained that this is indeed the case. It resembles the question of fasting on the day of Eid and similar cases in which Abū Hanīfa holds that the act is not invalidated.

I have also explained that discharge of the obligation and reward may coincide or occur separately. Discharge of the obligation means release from liability for fulfilling the command: freedom from the Lord’s blame or punishment. Reward is recompense for obedience. Unlike discharge of the obligation, reward is not entailed by mere compliance: a command entails that performing what is commanded discharges the obligation. Nevertheless, the two coincide under the revealed Law, since it is established therein that the obedient are rewarded and the disobedient punished.

They may, however, occur separately. An act may discharge the obligation yet carry no reward if it is accompanied by sin that offsets its reward, as has been said:

“Many a fasting person gains nothing from his fast but thirst, and many a person who stands in prayer at night gains nothing from his prayer but sleeplessness.”

Speaking falsehood and acting upon it while fasting incur sin that offsets the reward of fasting. The fast has combined compliance with what was commanded and engagement in what was prohibited. The obligation is therefore discharged through compliance, while the reward is forfeited through disobedience.

Conversely, an act may be rewarded without discharging the obligation if it is performed with deficiencies in its conditions and essential elements. A person is rewarded for what he has done, but the obligation is not discharged until he performs it in full.

---

## Page 9821

This is a sound clarification: performing what is commanded discharges the obligation. If it is accompanied by a sin of equal weight that undermines its purpose, that sin offsets the reward. If a person performs what is commanded incompletely, he is rewarded, but the obligation is not fully discharged: either the act must be repeated, its deficiency remedied, or he incurs sin.

Reflect, then, on this principle. What is commanded is like anything loved and sought: unless it is fully attained, the person commanded has not discharged his responsibility. Its deficiency must therefore be remedied by something of the same kind, by a substitute, or by repeating the entire act if its parts are interdependent. Otherwise, he remains liable, just as one does for committing what is prohibited.

The first case:
Someone pays less than the full amount of zakāh; he must pay the remainder.

The second:
Someone omits obligatory acts of Hajj; he remedies this by offering a sacrificial animal. The same applies to someone who omits obligatory elements of prayer whose omission can be remedied by prostration.

The third:
Someone offers a sacrificial animal with a defect, frees a slave with a defect, or prays without ritual purification.

The fourth:
Someone misses Friday prayer or jihad that is an individual obligation upon him.

If the commanded act is performed alongside something prohibited that conflicts with some of its constituent parts, it has not been fulfilled—as with intercourse while in ihrām, which invalidates it. If it does not conflict with any of its constituent parts, there will be

---

## Page 9822

a combination of what is commanded and what is prohibited, as when someone commits acts forbidden during ihrām while in that state, or speaks falsely and acts upon falsehood while fasting. Thus, there are three categories concerning prohibited acts, just as there are concerning commanded acts. When a commanded act is omitted, the omission is sometimes rectified by making good the deficiency and completing the act, sometimes by repeating it, and sometimes cannot be rectified at all.

Prohibited acts follow the same pattern: they may invalidate the commanded act, so that it must be repeated or cannot be made up; they may render it deficient while it still discharges the obligation, with the deficiency either remediable or not; or they may incur a sin that offsets its reward. The first is exemplified by invalidating Hajj; the second, by invalidating Friday prayer; the third, by performing Hajj while committing acts forbidden during it; the fourth, by prayer during which another worshipper passes in front of the person praying; and the fifth, by fasting while speaking falsely and acting upon falsehood.

These questions concerning a single act, a single agent, or one and the same entity resemble one another: can it be simultaneously praiseworthy and blameworthy, approved and disapproved, loved and hated, rewarded and punished, experiencing pleasure and suffering pain?

Such opposites can coexist in two different respects, but not in one and the same respect. Allah Most High has said:

“They ask you about wine and gambling. Say, ‘In them is great sin and benefits for people, but their sin is greater than their benefit.’”

---

## Page 9823

Section

I have previously discussed what is meant by knowledge of the revealed religion and explained that it falls into two categories: what the Lawgiver has reported, or what is known through his reports, and what the Lawgiver has commanded.

What he has reported is divided into matters which he has indicated can be known through reason and matters for which this is not so.

What he has commanded may be known either through reason or through revelation. It may also be either an objective intended by the Lawgiver or something necessarily entailed by that objective.

Likewise, the terms sharī'a, shar' and shir'a encompass everything Allah has ordained, both beliefs and deeds. Shaykh Abū Bakr al-Ājurrī composed Kitāb al-Sharī'a (The Book of the Revealed Religion), and Shaykh Abū 'Abd Allāh Ibn Batta composed Kitāb al-Ibāna 'an Sharī'at al-Firqa al-Nājiya (An Elucidation of the Revealed Religion of the Saved Group). There are other such works as well.

By the term sharī'a, these leading authorities on the Sunnah mean specifically the beliefs concerning faith held by Ahl al-Sunnah: for example, their belief that faith consists of speech and deeds; that Allah possesses the attributes with which He has described Himself and with which His Messenger has described Him; that the Quran is the speech of Allah, uncreated; and that Allah is the Creator of

---

## Page 9824

everything; whatever Allah wills comes to pass, and whatever He does not will does not come to pass; and He has power over all things. They do not declare those who pray towards the qiblah to be unbelievers merely because of sins, and they believe in intercession for those who commit major sins, along with other tenets of Ahl al-Sunnah. Thus, they called the foundations of their creed their sharī'a and distinguished their sharī'a from that of others.

The beliefs that these scholars call sharī'a are those which others largely call “rational doctrines” and “speculative theology,” or which all call “the foundations of religion.” Some call them “the greater understanding of religion.” This is comparable to other authors in this field entitling their works Kitāb al-Sunnah (The Book of the Sunnah), such as the works entitled al-Sunnah by 'Abd Allāh ibn Ahmad, al-Khallāl, al-Tabarānī, al-Ju'fī and al-Athram, and those of many others who wrote on these subjects. They called these works books of the Sunnah to distinguish the creed of Ahl al-Sunnah from the creed of the people of innovation.

Thus, the Sunnah, like the sharī'a, is what the Messenger instituted and prescribed. The term may refer to the beliefs he instituted and prescribed, to the deeds he instituted and prescribed, or to both. Hence, the word sunnah has several meanings, as does the word shir'a.

For this reason, Ibn 'Abbās and others explained Allah’s words, “a law and a clear way,” as “a sunnah and a path.” They thus explained shir'a as sunnah, and minhāj as a path. The terms sunnah and shir'a may concern beliefs and statements, or they may concern intentions and actions. The former pertains to the path of knowledge and discourse

---

## Page 9825

and the latter to the path of spiritual states and listening. They may also concern outward acts of worship and the policies of rulers. Speculative theologians set rational or theological doctrines over against matters of the sharī'a; Sufis set experiential insights and spiritual realities over against them; philosophers set philosophy over against the sharī'a; and kings set statecraft over against it. As for jurists and ordinary people, they depart from what they regard as the sharī'a in favour of some of these things, or set custom, a legal school, or personal opinion over against it.

The truth of the matter is that the sharī'a with which Allah sent Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, encompasses everything conducive to well-being in this world and the Hereafter. Whatever in these things conflicts with the sharī'a is false, and whatever accords with it is true. Yet most people also alter the meaning of the term sharī'a. Kings and ordinary people understand shar' and sharī'a to mean a judge’s ruling. It is well known, however, that adjudication is only one branch of the sharī'a. Rather, the sharī'a encompasses every exercise of authority and every action that promotes the good of religious and worldly life. The sharī'a is precisely the Book of Allah, the Sunnah of His Messenger, and the way followed by the early generations of the Muslim community in beliefs, spiritual states, worship, deeds, governance, rulings, offices of authority, and grants.

In people’s usage, the term has three senses:

Revealed law: what Allah and His Messenger have prescribed.

Law based on interpretation: matters in which independent scholarly judgment is permissible.

Altered law: the lies and wickedness perpetrated by those who promote falsehood under an outward guise of the sharī'a, or religious innovations, or misguidance that is attributed

---

## Page 9826

to the sharī'a by those who are astray. And Allah, glorified and exalted is He, knows best.

What I have explained concerning the meanings of sharī'a, rulings of the sharī'a, and knowledge of the sharī'a makes clear that no one may depart from the sharī'a in any of their affairs. Rather, everything conducive to a person’s well-being is encompassed by the revealed law, whether in fundamentals or subsidiary matters, spiritual states, deeds, governance, dealings, or anything else. All praise belongs to Allah, Lord of all worlds.

This is because the sharī'a consists in obeying Allah, His Messenger, and those in authority among us. Allah Most High has said:

“O you who believe, obey Allah and obey the Messenger and those in authority among you.”

In many verses of the Quran, He has enjoined obedience to Himself and His Messenger and forbidden disobedience to Himself and His Messenger. He has promised His pleasure, forgiveness, mercy, and Paradise for obedience to Himself and His Messenger, and threatened the opposite for disobedience to Himself and His Messenger. Thus everyone—whether a scholar, ruler, worshipper, or someone engaged in worldly dealings—must obey Allah and His Messenger in whatever they undertake, whether scholarship, judgment, issuing commands or prohibitions, work, worship, or anything else.

The essence of the sharī'a is to follow the messengers and submit to their authority, just as departing from it means departing from obedience to the messengers. Obedience to the messengers is the religion of Allah, for the sake of which He commanded fighting, saying:

“Fight them until there is no more persecution and worship

---

## Page 9827

is devoted entirely to Allah.” For He has said:

“Whoever obeys the Messenger has indeed obeyed Allah.”

To obey Him is to worship Him. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever obeys me has obeyed Allah, and whoever obeys my appointed commander has obeyed me. Whoever disobeys me has disobeyed Allah, and whoever disobeys my appointed commander has disobeyed me.”

Rulers and scholars have spheres in which obedience to them is obligatory, but they too must obey Allah and the Messenger in what they command. Thus rulers and subjects, leaders and subordinates, must each obey Allah and His Messenger in their respective positions and adhere to the sharī'a Allah has prescribed for them.

This is a general principle whose detailed explanation would be lengthy. Two groups of people have erred concerning it. One group has deemed it permissible to depart from the law of Allah and His Messenger and from obedience to Allah and His Messenger, supposing that the sharī'a cannot fully secure their welfare—whether out of ignorance, ignorance combined with desire, or sheer desire. The other group has failed to appreciate the true scope of the sharī'a and has so restricted it that both they and others have come to imagine that it cannot be put into practice. At the root of this lies ignorance of what the sharī'a encompasses, its true significance, and its breadth. And Allah knows best.

Some scholars and laypeople hold that the terms sharī'a and sharʿ apply only to those actions whose study is called the science of jurisprudence. They distinguish between beliefs and revealed laws, or between spiritual realities and revealed laws. This terminology differs from the usage explained above. As for His words, “Then We set you upon

---

## Page 9828

a prescribed way in the matter of religion,” it may either be interpreted as … (1).

Likewise, “rulings of the sharī'a” may mean those rulings that the Lawgiver has disclosed, on the understanding that rulings are attributes of actions which the Lawgiver has clarified and revealed. Some are known through reason, either immediately or through reflection, and some are known through both. All are called rulings of the sharī'a. Alternatively, this designation may be restricted to rulings known only from the Lawgiver. This is the terminology of the Mu'tazila and other speculative theologians and jurists, both of our school and of others.

The expression may also mean those rulings which the Lawgiver has established and introduced, and which would not have existed otherwise, on the premise that an act has a ruling in itself, and that the ruling consists only of what the Lawgiver has introduced. This is the position of the Ash'arīs and those who agree with them, both of our school and of others.

It may then be said that the ruling is the Lawgiver’s address itself: His imposition of an obligation or prohibition. Alternatively, it may be said that it is what that address entails and gives rise to: obligation or prohibited status, for example. Or it may be said that it is the relationship between the address and the action.

The correct view is that the term “ruling of the sharī'a” applies to all three. It may further be said that it applies both to what the Lawgiver has disclosed and to the address He has introduced and what it entails. This parallels what we have said concerning religious knowledge. Reflect, then, on these three fundamental concepts: religious knowledge, rulings of the sharī'a, and the sharī'a itself. And Allah knows best.
